## Select a chi-square test of independence

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.chi-square`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.chi-square.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Uncomplicated adult appendicitis with appendicolith

**Complaint:** appendicitis study

**Investigational premise:** Appendectomy technique (technique).

**Person and setting:** Sam Patel; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Sam Patel, the emergency-surgery research lead, reviews three new operative techniques for appendicitis with an appendicolith. The trial records surgical-site infection categories within thirty days. The presenter saves the methods appendix until after discussion because, he says, nobody likes a spoiler. Each participant contributes one category, and the report supplies the table's expected-count assessment for analysis.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational appendectomy technique study: invented practice counts of people by intervention and thirty-day SSI category. Each contributes once; no matching. Expected counts support the requested approximation.

| Group / reference | No SSI | Superficial SSI | Deeper SSI |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| New study intervention A | 30 | 25 | 25 |
| New study intervention B | 25 | 30 | 25 |
| New study intervention C | 25 | 25 | 30 |

**Question:** Which analysis best examines the association between study intervention and thirty-day SSI category, using the table and assumptions in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Chi-square test **(key)**

Rationale: Device group and outcome category are categorical variables, with separate observations and adequate expected counts. The requested large-sample association procedure fits those conditions.

B. McNemar test

Rationale: McNemar testing concerns paired binary responses. The exhibit instead supplies separate participants or continuous matched differences, so that observation structure does not fit.

C. Fisher exact test

Rationale: The ordinary two-by-two exact procedure does not match this table's multiple categories. Adequate expected counts support the requested large-sample association analysis.

D. Independent-samples t test

Rationale: This mean-based procedure does not fit the supplied ordinal scale or unsuitable normal-model distribution. A numerical code or a continuous time measurement alone does not establish its assumptions.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A chi-square test of independence assesses a relationship between categorical variables when observations are separate and expected counts support the approximation. Here intervention groups and outcome categories define the table. Remember to identify what each cell counts; several categories in a row do not make the same person a repeated observation.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use a chi-square test for the categorical intervention/outcome association. Observations are separate, and the supplied expected counts support the requested large-sample approximation.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.chi-square`, `claim.gs028c.appendicitis.appendicolith-operative-pathway`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.fisher-exact`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.independent-t`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.chi-square.mcnemar-boundary`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/chistest.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.chi-square.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/fishexac.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.fisher-exact.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda353.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.independent-t.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/mcnemar.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.mcnemar.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.pcori.org/evidence-updates/comparing-surgery-versus-antibiotics-treating-adults-uncomplicated-appendicitis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.pcori.coda-appendicitis.2024</a>; <a href="https://www.sages.org/publications/guidelines/guideline-for-the-diagnosis-and-treatment-of-appendicitis/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sages.appendicitis.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.chi-square.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Recurrent symptomatic gallstones

**Complaint:** gallstones study

**Investigational premise:** Gallbladder dissection instrument (device).

**Person and setting:** Casey Bennett; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Casey Bennett, the biliary research lead, reviews three new dissection instruments used during surgery for symptomatic gallstones. The trial classifies thirty-day surgical-site infection as absent, superficial or deeper. Each participant contributes once and received one instrument. Investigators want to examine the relationship between instrument group and infection category; the report includes the expected-count assessment for the table.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational gallbladder dissection instrument study: invented practice counts of people by intervention and thirty-day SSI category. Each contributes once; no matching. Expected counts support the requested approximation.

| Group / reference | No SSI | Superficial SSI | Deeper SSI |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| New study intervention A | 30 | 20 | 10 |
| New study intervention B | 20 | 30 | 10 |
| New study intervention C | 10 | 20 | 30 |

**Question:** Which analysis best examines the association between study intervention and thirty-day SSI category, using the table and assumptions in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Chi-square test **(key)**

Rationale: Device group and outcome category are categorical variables, with separate observations and adequate expected counts. The requested large-sample association procedure fits those conditions.

B. Wilcoxon rank-sum test

Rationale: Rank-sum answers a ranked-observation comparison. This study explicitly requests means and supplies reasonable assumptions for the mean-based procedure.

C. Fisher exact test

Rationale: The ordinary two-by-two exact procedure does not match this table's multiple categories. Adequate expected counts support the requested large-sample association analysis.

D. McNemar test

Rationale: McNemar testing concerns paired binary responses. The exhibit instead supplies separate participants or continuous matched differences, so that observation structure does not fit.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Device group and recorded outcome category are both categorical variables. The expected counts support a chi-square approximation, and each person contributes once. Remember that the number of columns does not establish pairing or a continuous outcome. The question concerns an association between categories, not a comparison of numerical means.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use a chi-square test for the categorical intervention/outcome association. Observations are separate, and the supplied expected counts support the requested large-sample approximation.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.chi-square`, `claim.gallstones.symptomatic-referral`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.fisher-exact`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.rank-sum`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.chi-square.mcnemar-boundary`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00535-023-02014-6" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.jsge.cholelithiasis-guideline.2023</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/gallstones/treatment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.gallstones-treatment.2017</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/chistest.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.chi-square.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/fishexac.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.fisher-exact.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/mcnemar.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.mcnemar.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/ranksum.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.rank-sum.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.chi-square.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Biopsy-confirmed localized anal-canal squamous cell carcinoma

**Complaint:** anal-canal SCC study

**Investigational premise:** Anal-cancer radiation delivery device (device).

**Person and setting:** Jamie Reed; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jamie Reed, the oncology discussion lead, reviews two new radiation-delivery devices for localized anal-canal SCC. The study records complete response or incomplete response at its stated assessment visit. A colleague proposes comparing average healing time, but the available records contain only device group and response category. Each participant contributes once, and the expected-count assessment accompanies the table.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational anal-cancer radiation device study: invented practice counts of people by intervention and tumor response at six months. Each contributes once; no matching. Expected counts support the requested approximation.

| Group / reference | Complete response | Incomplete response |
| --- | --- | --- |
| New study intervention A | 120 | 80 |
| New study intervention B | 80 | 120 |

**Question:** Which analysis best examines the association between study intervention and tumor response at six months, using the table and assumptions in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Chi-square test **(key)**

Rationale: Device group and outcome category are categorical variables, with separate observations and adequate expected counts. The requested large-sample association procedure fits those conditions.

B. McNemar test

Rationale: McNemar testing concerns paired binary responses. The exhibit instead supplies separate participants or continuous matched differences, so that observation structure does not fit.

C. Wilcoxon rank-sum test

Rationale: Rank-sum answers a ranked-observation comparison. This study explicitly requests means and supplies reasonable assumptions for the mean-based procedure.

D. Fisher exact test

Rationale: An exact two-by-two comparison could answer another analysis plan. This exhibit explicitly requests the large-sample approximation, whose expected-count conditions are met.

**Visible teachingPoint:** The observed counts describe intervention group and whether complete response occurred. With separate observations and adequate expected counts, a chi-square test fits the planned large-sample association analysis. Remember to inspect expected counts and the requested approximation, rather than choosing solely because a table is two by two or has a large total.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use a chi-square test for the categorical intervention/outcome association. Observations are separate, and the supplied expected counts support the requested large-sample approximation.

**Complaint:** carotid stenosis study

**Investigational premise:** Carotid-operation monitoring device (device).

**Person and setting:** Taylor Hayes; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Taylor Hayes, the vascular journal-club lead, reviews three new monitoring devices used during carotid operations for symptomatic carotid stenosis. The study classifies postoperative neurologic outcomes at thirty days. The committee requests a concise summary, but each manufacturer insists on a personal foreword. Each participant contributes one category, and the report supplies the expected-count assessment for the comparison.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational carotid monitoring device study: invented practice counts of people by intervention and thirty-day neurologic outcome. Each contributes once; no matching. Expected counts support the requested approximation.

| Group / reference | No event | Transient deficit | Persistent deficit | Other neurologic event |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| New study intervention A | 10 | 10 | 10 | 10 |
| New study intervention B | 8 | 12 | 11 | 9 |
| New study intervention C | 12 | 8 | 9 | 11 |

**Question:** Which analysis best examines the association between study intervention and thirty-day neurologic outcome, using the table and assumptions in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Chi-square test **(key)**

Rationale: Device group and outcome category are categorical variables, with separate observations and adequate expected counts. The requested large-sample association procedure fits those conditions.

B. One-way ANOVA

Rationale: This ordinary mean-based ANOVA does not address the supplied ordinal or unsuitable non-normal comparison. Having groups does not by itself establish its assumptions.

C. Fisher exact test

Rationale: The ordinary two-by-two exact procedure does not match this table's multiple categories. Adequate expected counts support the requested large-sample association analysis.

D. McNemar test

Rationale: McNemar testing concerns paired binary responses. The exhibit instead supplies separate participants or continuous matched differences, so that observation structure does not fit.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A table can have several rows and columns while asking one categorical-association question. Here a chi-square test fits the separate observations and adequate expected counts. Remember that both conditions matter. Counting rows and columns alone cannot establish them, and numerical cell entries remain counts within categories, not continuous outcome measurements.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use a chi-square test for the categorical intervention/outcome association. Observations are separate, and the supplied expected counts support the requested large-sample approximation.

## Select Fisher exact test

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.fisher-exact`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.fisher-exact.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Gastric gastrointestinal stromal tumor

**Complaint:** gastric GIST study

**Investigational premise:** GIST resection instrument (device).

**Person and setting:** Dana Rivera; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Dana Rivera, the upper-GI research lead, reviews a small trial of a new stapling instrument used during gastric GIST resection. The report records staple-line leak within thirty days in two separate groups. Participants were not matched, and some table entries are very small. The investigators want to compare leak outcomes; the report provides its expected-count assessment for analysis.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational GIST resection stapler: invented practice counts by group and 30-day staple-line leak. Cells count people once, unmatched. Expected counts are sparse; the large-sample approximation is unreliable.

| Group / reference | Leak | No leak |
| --- | --- | --- |
| New study intervention | 1 | 8 |
| Study comparator | 0 | 9 |

**Question:** Which analysis best compares thirty-day staple-line leak between the separate groups, using the sparse table and assumptions in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Fisher exact test **(key)**

Rationale: The exhibit supplies a sparse two-by-two table from separate participants. An exact categorical comparison fits the stated expected-count problem.

B. Chi-square test of independence

Rationale: The usual chi-square approximation is unreliable under the stated sparse expected counts. A two-by-two table does not by itself establish that approximation's adequacy.

C. Paired t test

Rationale: A paired t procedure needs repeated or deliberately matched measurements. This comparison does not supply those links between observations.

D. McNemar test

Rationale: McNemar testing concerns paired binary responses. The exhibit instead supplies separate participants or continuous matched differences, so that observation structure does not fit.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Fisher's exact test fits this sparse two-by-two comparison of intervention group and outcome status. The supplied expected-count assessment makes a large-sample approximation unreliable. Remember to check the cells, not just the total sample size. A binary outcome from different people also does not create the paired binary structure needed for McNemar testing.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use Fisher's exact test for this sparse two-by-two intervention/outcome table. The stated expected counts make the usual large-sample approximation unreliable for this exercise.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.fisher-exact`, `claim.gastric-gist.selected-eus-core-molecular-testing`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.chi-square`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.paired-t`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.fisher-exact.mcnemar-boundary`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://grupogeis.org/documentos/guias-geis/2023-GEIS-guidelines-for-GIST.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.gist-geis-2023</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/soft-tissue-sarcoma/hp/gist-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.gist-nci-pdq-2024</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/chistest.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.chi-square.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/fishexac.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.fisher-exact.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/mcnemar.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.mcnemar.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section3/prc311.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.paired-t.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.fisher-exact.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** External full-thickness rectal prolapse

**Complaint:** rectal prolapse study

**Investigational premise:** Rectal-prolapse fixation technique (technique).

**Person and setting:** Alex Morgan; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Alex Morgan, the colorectal discussion lead, reviews a new fixation technique for rectal prolapse. A small pilot records recurrence within two years in separate groups. The supplier calls the pilot extensive; its sales delegation is larger than the study population. The report contains the observed two-by-two table and the expected-count assessment requested for the recurrence comparison.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational prolapse fixation technique: invented practice counts by group and two-year recurrence. Cells count people once, unmatched. Expected counts are sparse; the large-sample approximation is unreliable.

| Group / reference | Recurrence | No recurrence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| New study intervention | 0 | 7 |
| Study comparator | 3 | 4 |

**Question:** Which analysis best compares two-year recurrence between the separate groups, using the sparse table and assumptions in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Fisher exact test **(key)**

Rationale: The exhibit supplies a sparse two-by-two table from separate participants. An exact categorical comparison fits the stated expected-count problem.

B. Chi-square test of independence

Rationale: The usual chi-square approximation is unreliable under the stated sparse expected counts. A two-by-two table does not by itself establish that approximation's adequacy.

C. Independent-samples t test

Rationale: This mean-based procedure does not fit the supplied ordinal scale or unsuitable normal-model distribution. A numerical code or a continuous time measurement alone does not establish its assumptions.

D. Wilcoxon rank-sum test

Rationale: Rank-sum answers a ranked-observation comparison. This study explicitly requests means and supplies reasonable assumptions for the mean-based procedure.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A small two-by-two table with sparse expected counts can require an exact comparison rather than the usual chi-square approximation. Fisher's exact test fits these separate intervention groups. Remember that an empty observed cell is a count, not a continuous measurement; a mean-based procedure would change the question being asked.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use Fisher's exact test for this sparse two-by-two intervention/outcome table. The stated expected counts make the usual large-sample approximation unreliable for this exercise.

B. One-way ANOVA

Rationale: This ordinary mean-based ANOVA does not address the supplied ordinal or unsuitable non-normal comparison. Having groups does not by itself establish its assumptions.

### `question.statistics-ethics.fisher-exact.v4`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Confirmed allergic contact dermatitis

**Complaint:** allergic contact dermatitis study

**Investigational premise:** Skin barrier film for contact dermatitis (device).

**Person and setting:** Jordan Brooks; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jordan Brooks, the immunology research lead, reviews a new barrier film for allergic contact dermatitis. The small trial records whether a flare occurred during protocol exposure. The representative offers to answer methods questions afterward, then books his departure for the minute his presentation ends. Each participant contributes one outcome, and the report includes its expected-count assessment.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational contact-dermatitis barrier film: invented practice counts by group and protocol-exposure flare. Cells count people once, unmatched. Expected counts are sparse; the large-sample approximation is unreliable.

| Group / reference | Flare | No flare |
| --- | --- | --- |
| New study intervention | 1 | 7 |
| Study comparator | 0 | 8 |

**Question:** Which analysis best compares flare during protocol exposure between the separate groups, using the sparse table and assumptions in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Fisher exact test **(key)**

Rationale: The exhibit supplies a sparse two-by-two table from separate participants. An exact categorical comparison fits the stated expected-count problem.

B. McNemar test

Rationale: McNemar testing concerns paired binary responses. The exhibit instead supplies separate participants or continuous matched differences, so that observation structure does not fit.

C. Wilcoxon signed-rank test

Rationale: Signed-rank needs repeated or matched observations from which to form differences. The groups in this comparison do not supply those links.

D. Chi-square test of independence

Rationale: The usual chi-square approximation is unreliable under the stated sparse expected counts. A two-by-two table does not by itself establish that approximation's adequacy.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Fisher's exact test addresses the sparse two-by-two categorical comparison described here. Each person contributes one flare outcome, with no pairing. Remember the separate checks: sparse expected counts favor the exact method, while the observation structure rules out a paired binary procedure. Neither the table's two columns nor two groups establishes linkage.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use Fisher's exact test for this sparse two-by-two intervention/outcome table. The stated expected counts make the usual large-sample approximation unreliable for this exercise.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.fisher-exact`, `claim.gs028e.type_iv`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.chi-square`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.signed-rank`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.fisher-exact.mcnemar-boundary`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/immunology/articles/10.3389/fimmu.2026.1835083/full" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028e.acd_tong2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/immunotoxicity-testing-guidance" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028e.fda_immunotoxicity</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/chistest.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.chi-square.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/fishexac.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.fisher-exact.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/mcnemar.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.mcnemar.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/software/dataplot/refman1/auxillar/signrank.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.signed-rank.2026</a>.

## Identify basic study designs

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.study-design`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.study-design.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Symptomatic reducible inguinal hernia

**Complaint:** inguinal hernia study

**Investigational premise:** Hernia reinforcement material or technique (device).

**Person and setting:** Sam Patel; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Sam Patel, the hernia-service research lead, reviews a new reinforcement material for symptomatic inguinal hernia repair. Investigators grouped patients by material already received and followed recurrence for two years. The publicity officer wants 'breakthrough' in the title and offers 'breakthrough pending' as a compromise. The clinical teams chose materials; investigators did not allocate the treatments under review.

**Exhibit — Hernia recurrence — illustrative study design:** Patients who already received one of two study reinforcement materials for inguinal hernia repair are grouped by exposure and followed for recurrence over two years. Investigators assign nothing. This unnamed investigational hernia reinforcement material protocol is invented for practice, not an actual study or efficacy estimate.

**Question:** Which study design matches how the investigators selected participants, handled the study intervention and measured the clinical outcome described in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Prospective cohort study **(key)**

Rationale: Patients enter by material already received and are followed for recurrence. With no investigator assignment, that forward observation is a prospective cohort.

B. Retrospective case-control study

Rationale: Case-control sampling would begin with recurrence cases and controls. This project instead begins with existing material exposure and observes later outcomes.

C. Cross-sectional study

Rationale: A cross-sectional snapshot measures exposure and outcome together. These investigators instead follow the exposure groups for later recurrence.

D. Randomized experiment

Rationale: An experiment would assign the materials. Following patients forward does not create the random allocation absent from this project.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Name the design by how people entered the study and what investigators did next. These patients were grouped by material already received and followed for later recurrence, without assigned treatment. That is a prospective cohort. Remember: watching forward in time does not, by itself, make a study randomized or establish a treatment effect.

**Short explanation fallback:** This is a prospective cohort: investigators grouped patients by reinforcement material already received and followed later recurrence without assigning treatment. Forward observation alone does not create randomization.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.study-design`, `claim.inguinal-hernia.symptomatic-elective-repair`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/design-conduct-analyze-field-studies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-study-design.2026</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5809582/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.herniasurge-2018</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/inguinal-hernia" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.niddk-inguinal-hernia</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.study-design.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Localized resectable colon cancer

**Complaint:** colon cancer study

**Investigational premise:** Cancer recurrence-prevention biologic (drug).

**Person and setting:** Casey Bennett; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Casey Bennett, the colorectal journal-club lead, reviews a new monoclonal antibody after resection of localized colon cancer. Investigators allocated participants to study arms and followed recurrence for two years. The representative requests equal speaking time for both products, then volunteers to speak for both. The report gives allocation details; the team is reviewing how the study was organized.

**Exhibit — Colon-cancer recurrence — illustrative study design:** Investigators randomly allocate people after localized colon-cancer resection to a new monoclonal antibody or control, then follow recurrence for two years. This unnamed investigational monoclonal antibody protocol is invented for practice, not an actual study or efficacy estimate.

**Question:** Which study design matches how the investigators selected participants, handled the study intervention and measured the clinical outcome described in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Randomized experiment **(key)**

Rationale: Investigators randomly allocate antibody or control before measuring recurrence. That investigator-created assignment is the defining experimental action in this project.

B. Prospective cohort study

Rationale: A cohort would observe existing exposure groups. Here investigators create the groups through random assignment rather than recording treatment already received.

C. Case-control study

Rationale: Case-control selection would start with recurrence status and review exposure. This project assigns study arms before measuring that outcome.

D. Cross-sectional study

Rationale: A cross-sectional survey would measure exposure and outcome together without allocation. Here investigators assign study arms and subsequently measure recurrence.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Random assignment is an action by investigators, not a description of who happened to attend. Here they allocated antibody or control randomly before measuring recurrence, making this a randomized experiment. Remember to look for assignment. A cohort observes existing exposures; case-control and cross-sectional designs organize selection and measurement differently.

**Short explanation fallback:** The investigators randomly allocated antibody or control before measuring recurrence. That assignment makes this a randomized experiment rather than observation of treatment groups that already existed.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.study-design`, `claim.colorectal.histology-confirms`, `claim.colon-cancer.oncologic-regional-resection`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ascrsu.com/ascrs/view/ASCRS-Toolkit/2851068/all/Management_" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board-batch.ascrs-colon-2022</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/colorectal/hp/colon-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board-batch.nci-colon-2025</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/design-conduct-analyze-field-studies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-study-design.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/colorectal/screening-fact-sheet" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nci.colorectal-screening.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.study-design.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Peptic ulcer bleeding

**Complaint:** peptic ulcer bleeding study

**Investigational premise:** Endoscopic hemostasis device (device).

**Person and setting:** Jamie Reed; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jamie Reed, the endoscopy research lead, reviews a new hemostasis device for peptic ulcer bleeding. Investigators selected patients who rebled and comparison patients who did not, then checked earlier device exposure. They did not assign treatment. The study concerns rebleeding after the initial procedure, and the report describes the sampling and sequence of measurements for the discussion.

**Exhibit — Ulcer rebleeding — illustrative study design:** Investigators select people with rebleeding after endoscopic treatment of peptic ulcer bleeding and controls without rebleeding, then review previous study-device exposure. Investigators assign nothing. This unnamed investigational endoscopic hemostasis device protocol is invented for practice, not an actual study or efficacy estimate.

**Question:** Which study design matches how the investigators selected participants, handled the study intervention and measured the clinical outcome described in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Case-control study **(key)**

Rationale: Selection begins with people who rebled and controls who did not; investigators then examine earlier device use. That outcome-first sampling is case-control.

B. Prospective cohort study

Rationale: A cohort comparison would begin with exposure groups. Reviewing earlier records does not make these outcome-selected groups into a cohort.

C. Randomized experiment

Rationale: The investigators assign no intervention. Selecting rebleeding cases and controls is observational, rather than allocation of a randomized treatment.

D. Cross-sectional study

Rationale: A cross-sectional study would sample without selecting on rebleeding status and measure current variables together. This design selects cases and controls by outcome.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Ask what determined entry into the comparison. Selecting people who rebled and controls who did not, then reviewing earlier device exposure, gives a case-control study. Old records alone do not define that design; outcome-based selection does. Remember the entry rule: a cohort would begin by organizing people according to exposure.

**Short explanation fallback:** Selection began with rebleeding status and then examined earlier device exposure. That outcome-first sampling defines a case-control design, rather than a cohort simply because records were reviewed.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.study-design`, `claim.peptic-ulcer-bleeding.high-risk-stigmata-require-hemostasis`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33929377/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.acg.upper-gastrointestinal-ulcer-bleeding-guideline.2021</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/design-conduct-analyze-field-studies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-study-design.2026</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42127996/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.esge.peptic-ulcer-bleeding-guideline.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.study-design.v4`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Gastroesophageal reflux disease under evaluation

**Complaint:** reflux disease study

**Investigational premise:** Wearable reflux assessment device (test).

**Person and setting:** Taylor Hayes; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Taylor Hayes, the foregut journal-club lead, reviews a new wearable test for reflux disease. A survey records device use and current reflux symptoms at one visit, without allocating treatment or following participants afterward. The author calls the questionnaire streamlined; its optional feedback section first requires an explanation of why the feedback is optional. Recruitment details accompany the report.

**Exhibit — Reflux findings — illustrative study design:** A survey records use of a new wearable reflux test and current reflux symptoms at the same visit. Recruitment is not based on symptom status; there is no follow-up or allocation. This unnamed investigational wearable reflux test protocol is invented for practice, not an actual study or efficacy estimate.

**Question:** Which study design matches how the investigators selected participants, handled the study intervention and measured the clinical outcome described in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Cross-sectional study **(key)**

Rationale: Current device use and reflux symptoms are recorded together, without outcome-based recruitment or follow-up. Those features define this cross-sectional snapshot.

B. Case-control study

Rationale: Case-control recruitment would depend on outcome status. The exhibit explicitly says recruitment does not depend on the current symptom findings.

C. Prospective cohort study

Rationale: A prospective cohort would organize exposure groups and observe later outcomes. This survey has no follow-up after the same-visit measurements.

D. Randomized experiment

Rationale: A randomized experiment requires investigator assignment. This survey records device use and symptoms without assigning a device or study arm.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A cross-sectional study records exposure and outcome at one time, giving a snapshot. Here device use and current symptoms are measured together, without outcome-based recruitment, assignment or follow-up. Remember what the study actually observed. A report title cannot create a time sequence or treatment allocation that the design never supplied.

**Short explanation fallback:** This is a cross-sectional survey: device use and current reflux symptoms were recorded together, without outcome-based recruitment, subsequent follow-up or investigator-assigned intervention.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.study-design`, `claim.early.gerd.off-ppi-reflux-monitoring`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8754510/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.acg.gerd.2022</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/design-conduct-analyze-field-studies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-study-design.2026</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31375601/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.icarus.antireflux.2019</a>.

## Distinguish bias from confounding

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.bias-confounding`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.bias-confounding.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Stable reducible midline incisional hernia

**Complaint:** incisional hernia study

**Investigational premise:** Hernia reinforcement material or technique (device).

**Person and setting:** Dana Rivera; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Dana Rivera, the abdominal-wall research lead, reviews a new reinforcement material for incisional hernia repair. The observational study compares recurrence within two years. The sponsor wants a clean comparison, meaning a report without complicated caveats. The exhibit describes a study site related to both material use and recurrence; the unadjusted report does not account for that site.

**Exhibit — Clinical study — illustrative bias review:** An unnamed investigational hernia reinforcement material study: Study site predicts both material use and two-year hernia recurrence. The unadjusted comparison ignores site; that relationship is stipulated for this exercise, not a clinical risk-factor claim. These study conditions are invented for practice, not real intervention results.

**Question:** Which problem most directly threatens the unadjusted intervention-outcome comparison when study site relates to both?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Confounding by study site **(key)**

Rationale: Site relates to both intervention use and the outcome, and the comparison ignores it. Their mixed association is the stated confounding concern.

B. Selection bias from enrollment

Rationale: The exhibit describes an ignored third factor in the comparison, not differential entry into the sample. That is the confounding rather than selection mechanism supplied.

C. Measurement bias from recall

Rationale: No unequal recall or outcome-recording method is described. The stated problem is the ignored relationship of site with both exposure and outcome.

D. Random error from sample size

Rationale: More observations do not automatically remove the systematic mixing with site. Sample-size variation is not the primary mechanism described.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Confounding occurs when an exposure comparison is mixed with another associated factor. Here site relates to both intervention use and the outcome, so the unadjusted association may combine those relationships. Remember the third-factor question: what else differs with exposure and outcome? More observations do not automatically remove that systematic problem.

**Short explanation fallback:** Study site is a confounder in this exercise because it relates to both intervention use and the outcome and is ignored in the comparison.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.bias-confounding`, `claim.ventral-hernia.elective-pulmonary-optimization`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>; <a href="https://academic.oup.com/bjs/article/110/12/1732/7277564" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ehs.midline-incisional.2023</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.bias-confounding.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Symptomatic thyroglossal duct cyst

**Complaint:** thyroglossal duct cyst study

**Investigational premise:** Thyroglossal-cyst dissection technique (technique).

**Person and setting:** Alex Morgan; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Alex Morgan, the neck-surgery journal-club lead, reviews a new dissection technique for symptomatic thyroglossal duct cysts. Investigators record surgical-site infection within thirty days. The author says every patient's voice matters; recruitment stopped whenever the coordinator went home. The report presents its conclusion as applying to all eligible patients, although enrollment covered daytime operations and excluded nighttime operations.

**Exhibit — Clinical study — illustrative bias review:** An unnamed investigational thyroglossal-cyst dissection technique study: Investigators enroll daytime operations and no nighttime operations, yet present their thirty-day SSI conclusion as applying to all eligible surgical patients. These study conditions are invented for practice, not real intervention results.

**Question:** Which bias most directly limits the claim about all eligible surgical patients when recruitment covers daytime operations only?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Selection bias from daytime-only recruitment **(key)**

Rationale: Daytime encounters alone are used to make an all-patients claim. The missing nighttime surgical patients create the stated selection concern even with a large daytime sample.

B. Confounding by an unmeasured patient characteristic

Rationale: The supplied mechanism is who entered the audit. No patient factor relating both exposure and outcome is identified as the primary concern.

C. Measurement bias from unequal recall windows

Rationale: The exhibit does not describe unequal recall. It describes exclusion of nighttime surgical patients from an all-patients conclusion.

D. Random error from insufficient sample precision

Rationale: A large daytime sample can still exclude the target population of nighttime surgical patients. Greater sampling precision does not repair that selection problem.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Selection bias concerns which observations enter the study. A large daytime-only sample still leaves nighttime surgical patients outside an audit presented as applying to all eligible surgical patients. Sample size cannot repair that missing coverage. Distinguish this selection problem from confounding within the comparison or inconsistent measurement after people have entered it.

**Short explanation fallback:** Daytime-only sampling creates the stated selection concern. Adding more daytime surgical patients does not supply the missing nighttime surgical observations needed for the all-patients conclusion.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.bias-confounding`, `claim.brief.thyroglossal-duct-cyst.sistrunk-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://iowaprotocols.medicine.uiowa.edu/protocols/thyroglossal-duct-cyst-excision" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.iowa-thyroglossal-duct-cyst-2017</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.bias-confounding.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Surgery-provoked proximal deep vein thrombosis

**Complaint:** postoperative DVT study

**Investigational premise:** Calf-stimulation device (device).

**Person and setting:** Riley Chen; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Riley Chen, the perioperative research lead, reviews a new calf-stimulation device and postoperative DVT. One group reported thrombotic events when they occurred; another recalled events six months later. A colleague points out that everyone used the same form. The investigators are reviewing the collection process, and the report specifies how the two reporting schedules differed during follow-up.

**Exhibit — Clinical study — illustrative bias review:** An unnamed investigational calf-stimulation device study: One group reports postoperative DVT events immediately; the other recalls those events six months later. The reporting schedules differ; no real device effect is supplied. These study conditions are invented for practice, not real intervention results.

**Question:** Which information problem most directly threatens the outcome comparison when one group reports immediately and the other recalls events six months later?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Measurement bias from unequal recall **(key)**

Rationale: Immediate recording in one group and recall six months later in the other use unequal measurement processes. That can systematically distort the recorded contrast.

B. Selection bias from study enrollment

Rationale: The difference concerns how outcomes were recorded after entry, rather than how people were selected for the study.

C. Confounding by baseline study site

Rationale: No baseline site factor is supplied as relating exposure and the outcome. The stated concern is the unequal reporting schedule.

D. Random error from natural variation

Rationale: The groups are measured by different recall processes. That systematic difference is not simply random variation that a larger sample necessarily removes.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Measurement bias can arise when groups' outcomes are recorded differently. Immediate reports and recall six months later use unequal measurement processes, so a difference in reports may reflect how the outcomes were remembered. This is distinct from who was recruited or which baseline factor confounds the comparison. Larger samples do not equalize the reporting methods.

**Short explanation fallback:** The primary concern is measurement bias from unequal recall schedules. The groups' recorded outcomes may differ because one reports immediately and the other recalls much later.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.bias-confounding`, `claim.postoperative-dvt.prompt-venous-duplex`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/blood-clots/testing-diagnosis/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.dvt-cdc-2024</a>; <a href="https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/deep-vein-thrombosis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.dvt-nhlbi-diagnosis-2022</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.bias-confounding.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Documented malignant-hyperthermia susceptibility

**Complaint:** malignant-hyperthermia susceptibility study

**Investigational premise:** Anesthesia monitoring sensor (device).

**Person and setting:** Jordan Brooks; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jordan Brooks, the anesthesia journal-club lead, reviews a new monitoring sensor for malignant-hyperthermia susceptibility. An observational model compares recognition of concerning intraoperative changes. Investigators adjusted for recorded differences between groups. Follow-up access relates to both sensor choice and recorded recognition, but important access differences were not measured. The report describes those stipulated relationships and the model under review.

**Exhibit — Clinical study — illustrative bias review:** An unnamed investigational anesthesia monitoring sensor study: The model adjusts for measured site and procedure category. Follow-up access relates to both sensor choice and recorded recognition of concerning intraoperative changes, but important access differences were not recorded. These relationships are stipulated, not general clinical risk-factor claims. These study conditions are invented for practice, not real intervention results.

**Question:** Which limitation remains after adjustment when an important factor related to both sensor choice and recorded outcomes was not measured?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Residual confounding by unmeasured factors **(key)**

Rationale: Unrecorded follow-up access differences relate to intervention choice and the outcome. Adjustment for other factors leaves that potential confounding unresolved.

B. Random error from the sampling process

Rationale: The exhibit identifies an unrecorded associated factor, not just sampling variability. Greater precision would not supply the missing follow-up access information.

C. Selection bias from study enrollment

Rationale: No enrollment mechanism is described as the primary concern. The known limitation is confounding by follow-up access differences absent from the model.

D. Measurement bias from inconsistent outcome recording

Rationale: No inconsistent outcome-recording process is supplied. The gap is a relevant factor the model could not adjust for because it was not recorded.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Adjustment can address recorded factors without accounting for unrecorded ones. Follow-up access still relates to both intervention choice and the outcome, so residual confounding remains possible despite adjustment for site and procedure category. Remember that an adjusted label is not a guarantee of an unconfounded result; ask which relevant differences the model could actually use.

**Short explanation fallback:** Residual confounding remains because relevant follow-up access differences were not measured. Adjusting for site and procedure category does not automatically account for that unrecorded factor.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.bias-confounding`, `claim.gs028d.mh.trigger-free-plan`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.emhg.org/recommendations-1/2018/2/27/perioperative-management-of-mh-suspected-or-susceptible-patients" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.emhg.mh-susceptible.2018</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12860801/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.jsa.malignant-hyperthermia.2026</a>.

## Interpret sensitivity and specificity

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.sensitivity-specificity`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.sensitivity-specificity.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Accessible suspicious solid breast mass

**Complaint:** Blood-test question

**Investigational premise:** Diagnostic or screening blood test (test).

**Person and setting:** Grace Patel; age 58, Female.

**Story:** Grace Patel, a 58-year-old woman, has an accessible suspicious solid breast mass on completed diagnostic imaging. Core biopsy is arranged. A recruiter offers a study of a new screening blood test. His slides call the evidence transparent, but he keeps skipping the results table. The patient asks how often the test identified disease in the separate validation cohort.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational breast-cancer blood test: invented practice counts from a separate validation cohort. Cells count people; reference classification is treated as correct. Not real test performance or a personal disease probability.

| Group / reference | New blood test positive | New blood test negative |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Breast cancer present by reference | 90 | 10 |
| Breast cancer absent by reference | 30 | 70 |

**Question:** What is the new investigational breast-cancer blood test's sensitivity in the separate constructed validation cohort shown in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. 90% **(key)**

Rationale: Of the 100 people with the condition, 90 screen positive: 90/(90 + 10) = 90%. This is the condition-present denominator sensitivity requires.

B. 10%

Rationale: Ten of 100 people with the condition screen negative. That is the false-negative fraction, not the positive fraction requested for sensitivity.

C. 75%

Rationale: The calculation 90/(90 + 30) = 75% starts with all positive screens. That gives positive predictive value rather than sensitivity.

D. 70%

Rationale: The calculation 70/(30 + 70) = 70% starts with people without the condition. It gives specificity, not sensitivity.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Sensitivity starts with people who have the condition by the reference assessment: how many tested positive? Here 90 of 100 did, giving 90%. Remember: sensitivity begins with condition present, not with a positive or negative screen. These practice counts explain the measure; they do not calculate this patient's probability after a negative result.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use the condition-present row: 90/(90 + 10) = 90% sensitivity. The positive-result column answers a different question, and this practice table is not a personal risk estimate.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.sensitivity-specificity`, `claim.bread-butter.breast-mass.image-guided-core-biopsy`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.predictive-value-prevalence`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37236740/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.acr-palpable-breast-mass-2023</a>; <a href="https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/csels/dsepd/ss1978/lesson5/appendixa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.test-accuracy.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/coronavirus-covid-19-and-medical-devices/antibody-serology-testing-covid-19-information-patients-and-consumers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.fda.predictive-values.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.sensitivity-specificity.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Uncomplicated duodenal ulcer and H. pylori evaluation

**Complaint:** Breath-test question

**Investigational premise:** Active-infection breath test (test).

**Person and setting:** Adrian Chen; age 29, Male.

**Story:** Adrian Chen, a 29-year-old man, has an uncomplicated duodenal ulcer being evaluated for H. pylori. An optional study offers a new breath test. The patient asks, 'What happened to people who did not have the infection?' Its separate validation table compares the investigational result with a reference assessment. The planned clinical evaluation has not been replaced by enrollment.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational H. pylori breath test: invented practice counts from a separate validation cohort. Cells count people; reference classification is treated as correct. Not real test performance or a personal disease probability.

| Group / reference | New breath test positive | New breath test negative |
| --- | --- | --- |
| H. pylori infection present by reference | 80 | 20 |
| H. pylori infection absent by reference | 5 | 95 |

**Question:** What is the new investigational breast-cancer blood test's specificity in the separate constructed validation cohort shown in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. 95% **(key)**

Rationale: Of the 100 people without the condition, 95 screen negative: 95/(5 + 95) = 95%. This is specificity.

B. 5%

Rationale: Five of the 100 people without the condition screen positive. That gives the false-positive fraction, the opposite screen result from the one used for specificity.

C. 80%

Rationale: The calculation 80/(80 + 20) = 80% uses the condition-present row and gives sensitivity. Specificity starts with condition absent.

D. 94%

Rationale: The calculation 80/(80 + 5) is about 94%. It uses the positive-screen column to give positive predictive value, not specificity.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Specificity asks how often the screen is negative among people without the condition. In this table, 95 of 100 condition-absent people test negative, giving 95%. Start with condition absent. Starting with positive screens would instead ask about positive predictive value, which is a different measure and denominator.

**Short explanation fallback:** Specificity is 95/(5 + 95) = 95%, using everyone without the condition as the denominator. It is not the proportion of positive results that are correct.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.sensitivity-specificity`, `claim.brief.h-pylori.active-infection-testing`, `claim.brief.h-pylori.test-of-cure-plan`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.predictive-value-prevalence`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://webfiles.gi.org/links/journals/ACG-Hpylori-Guidelines-Highlights-2024-FINAL.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.acg-h-pylori-2024</a>; <a href="https://gi.org/topics/peptic-ulcer-disease/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.acg-peptic-ulcer-patient</a>; <a href="https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/csels/dsepd/ss1978/lesson5/appendixa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.test-accuracy.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/coronavirus-covid-19-and-medical-devices/antibody-serology-testing-covid-19-information-patients-and-consumers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.fda.predictive-values.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.sensitivity-specificity.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Suspected or confirmed cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma

**Complaint:** Optical-test question

**Investigational premise:** Optical skin-lesion test (test).

**Person and setting:** Nora Hayes; age 72, Female.

**Story:** Nora Hayes, a 72-year-old woman, is discussing biopsy of a lesion suspicious for cutaneous SCC. A brochure describes a new optical skin test's validation cohort. 'The evidence gets one paragraph; the subscription terms get three pages,' the patient says. The patient asks how often the test identified disease among participants known to have it by reference assessment.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational cutaneous-SCC optical test: invented practice counts from a separate validation cohort. Cells count people; reference classification is treated as correct. Not real test performance or a personal disease probability.

| Group / reference | New optical test positive | New optical test negative |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cutaneous SCC present by reference | 20 | 5 |
| Cutaneous SCC absent by reference | 10 | 15 |

**Question:** What is the new investigational breast-cancer blood test's sensitivity in the separate constructed validation cohort shown in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. 80% **(key)**

Rationale: The condition-present row contains 20 positive and five negative screens. Its positive share is 20/25 = 80%, the sensitivity.

B. 67%

Rationale: The calculation 20/(20 + 10) is about 67% and starts with positive screens. That calculates positive predictive value, not sensitivity.

C. 20%

Rationale: The calculation 5/(20 + 5) = 20% counts negative screens among people with the condition. That is the false-negative fraction.

D. 60%

Rationale: The calculation 15/(10 + 15) = 60% uses people without the condition. That calculates specificity rather than sensitivity.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Choose the condition-present denominator before calculating sensitivity. This row contains 20 positive screens and five negative screens, so 20/25 = 80%. The remaining five show why sensitivity and the false-negative fraction differ. Counting all positive screens would answer a predictive-value question instead of the condition-based question asked here.

**Short explanation fallback:** There are 25 people with the condition, and 20 screen positive: sensitivity is 80%. The five missed cases belong in that same denominator.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.sensitivity-specificity`, `claim.cutaneous-scc.biopsy-for-diagnosis-and-risk`, `claim.cutaneous-scc.risk-directed-surgery`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.predictive-value-prevalence`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/csels/dsepd/ss1978/lesson5/appendixa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.test-accuracy.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/guidelines/scc" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.csc.aad-guideline.2018</a>; <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/clinical-care/wisely" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.csc.aad-mohs-wisely.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/coronavirus-covid-19-and-medical-devices/antibody-serology-testing-covid-19-information-patients-and-consumers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.fda.predictive-values.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.sensitivity-specificity.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Suspected post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder

**Complaint:** Blood-test question

**Investigational premise:** Diagnostic or screening blood test (test).

**Person and setting:** Julian Torres; age 42, Male.

**Story:** Julian Torres, a 42-year-old man, has suspected post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder and is discussing tissue diagnosis. A recruiter presents a new blood test's separate validation table. The patient asks what negative results mean among participants without the disorder. Reference findings and investigational results appear separately, and the patient wants help interpreting the study before deciding about optional enrollment.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational PTLD blood test: invented practice counts from a separate validation cohort. Cells count people; reference classification is treated as correct. Not real test performance or a personal disease probability.

| Group / reference | New blood test positive | New blood test negative |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PTLD present by reference | 16 | 4 |
| PTLD absent by reference | 30 | 70 |

**Question:** What is the new investigational breast-cancer blood test's specificity in the separate constructed validation cohort shown in the exhibit?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. 70% **(key)**

Rationale: The condition-absent row has 70 negative screens out of 100 people: 70/(30 + 70) = 70%, the specificity.

B. 30%

Rationale: Thirty of 100 people without the condition screen positive. That is the false-positive fraction rather than specificity.

C. 80%

Rationale: The calculation 16/(16 + 4) = 80% uses people with the condition. That is sensitivity, not specificity.

D. 35%

Rationale: The calculation 16/(16 + 30) is about 35%. It uses all positive screens to calculate positive predictive value, not specificity.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Specificity uses everyone classified as condition absent, whether their test was positive or negative. Here 70 of those 100 people test negative, so specificity is 70%. Remember the starting group: condition absent. Starting with the positive-result column would instead ask a predictive-value question, regardless of which result interests a particular patient.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use the condition-absent row: 70/(30 + 70) = 70% specificity. A particular patient’s result does not change the denominator defining this test measure.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.sensitivity-specificity`, `claim.ptld.pattern`, `claim.ptld.tissue`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.predictive-value-prevalence`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/csels/dsepd/ss1978/lesson5/appendixa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.test-accuracy.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/coronavirus-covid-19-and-medical-devices/antibody-serology-testing-covid-19-information-patients-and-consumers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.fda.predictive-values.2026</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31230381/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ptld.ast-2019</a>; <a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/transplantation/articles/10.3389/frtra.2026.1869288/full" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ptld.frontiers-review-2026</a>.

## Relate positive predictive value to prevalence

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.predictive-value-prevalence`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.predictive-value-prevalence.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Small accessible pigmented lesion concerning for melanoma

**Complaint:** Positive-test question

**Investigational premise:** Optical skin-lesion test (test).

**Person and setting:** Maya Reed; age 29, Female.

**Story:** Maya Reed, a 29-year-old woman, is discussing diagnostic excision of a small pigmented lesion concerning for melanoma. A new optical-test brochure compares two hypothetical populations. Its presenter calls the brochure personalized, then offers two identical covers to choose from. The patient asks why a positive result would have different meanings in the populations before considering optional study enrollment.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational melanoma optical test: invented testing-population assumptions for practice. These comparisons supply no real test performance or personal disease probability.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Sensitivity | Same in the two hypothetical populations |
| Specificity | Same in the two hypothetical populations |
| Prevalence | Higher in population B than in population A |

**Question:** How does the new investigational breast-cancer blood test's positive predictive value change between the hypothetical populations when sensitivity and specificity remain fixed as stated?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Positive predictive value increases **(key)**

Rationale: With sensitivity and specificity fixed, the higher-prevalence population has a greater true-positive share among positive screens. This supports an increase, without an exact percentage.

B. Positive predictive value decreases

Rationale: This reverses the direction: higher prevalence increases the true-positive share among positives under the fixed-test assumptions.

C. Positive predictive value stays fixed

Rationale: Fixed sensitivity and specificity do not fix positive predictive value. Prevalence also determines the mix of true and false positives.

D. Positive predictive value is indeterminate

Rationale: An exact percentage would need more numbers, but the direction follows from higher prevalence with fixed test characteristics. That direction is not indeterminate.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Positive predictive value asks what share of positive results come from people with the condition. With sensitivity and specificity held fixed, higher prevalence makes true positives a larger share of positives, so predictive value rises. Remember that the test and the tested population both matter; this comparison gives direction, not a personal percentage.

**Short explanation fallback:** Positive predictive value increases under the stated fixed-test assumptions because prevalence is higher. The exhibit supports the direction of change without supplying an exact personal probability.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.predictive-value-prevalence`, `claim.pigmented-skin-lesion.complete-diagnostic-biopsy`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.sensitivity-specificity`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/csels/dsepd/ss1978/lesson5/appendixa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.test-accuracy.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/coronavirus-covid-19-and-medical-devices/antibody-serology-testing-covid-19-information-patients-and-consumers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.fda.predictive-values.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/guidelines/melanoma" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.aad-melanoma-highlights</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/skin/hp/melanoma-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.nci-melanoma-pdq-2025</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.predictive-value-prevalence.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Resectable pancreatic body or tail adenocarcinoma

**Complaint:** Positive-test question

**Investigational premise:** Diagnostic or screening blood test (test).

**Person and setting:** Owen Reed; age 67, Male.

**Story:** Owen Reed, a 67-year-old man, has resectable pancreatic tail cancer and is discussing operative planning. A recruiter describes a new screening blood test using hypothetical testing populations. Its brochure advertises 'Precision You Can Hug.' The patient asks whether a positive result means the same thing when fewer people being tested have cancer. The study supplies no prognosis or treatment recommendation.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational pancreatic-cancer blood test: invented testing-population assumptions for practice. These comparisons supply no real test performance or personal disease probability.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Sensitivity | Same in the two hypothetical populations |
| Specificity | Same in the two hypothetical populations |
| Prevalence | Lower in population B than in population A |

**Question:** How does the new investigational breast-cancer blood test's positive predictive value change between the hypothetical populations when sensitivity and specificity remain fixed as stated?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Positive predictive value decreases **(key)**

Rationale: With sensitivity and specificity fixed, lower prevalence means false positives form a larger share of positive screens. Positive predictive value therefore decreases.

B. Positive predictive value increases

Rationale: This reverses the stated comparison. Lower prevalence decreases the true-positive share among positive screens under these fixed-test assumptions.

C. Positive predictive value stays fixed

Rationale: Two populations can have the same test characteristics but different predictive values because prevalence differs.

D. Positive predictive value is indeterminate

Rationale: The exhibit lacks an exact percentage, but still establishes a decrease under the lower-prevalence, fixed-test assumptions.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A test's positive predictive value depends on the population as well as its sensitivity and specificity. With those test characteristics fixed, lower prevalence makes false positives a larger share of positive screens, so predictive value falls. A fixed test is not a fixed predictive value; the exhibit establishes direction without an exact percentage.

**Short explanation fallback:** Positive predictive value decreases in the lower-prevalence setting under the stated assumptions. Unchanged sensitivity and specificity do not make predictive value constant.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.predictive-value-prevalence`, `claim.pancreatic-adenocarcinoma.body-tail-distal-pancreatectomy`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.sensitivity-specificity`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/csels/dsepd/ss1978/lesson5/appendixa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.test-accuracy.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/coronavirus-covid-19-and-medical-devices/antibody-serology-testing-covid-19-information-patients-and-consumers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.fda.predictive-values.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/pancreatic/hp/pancreatic-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nci-pdq.pancreatic-cancer-treatment.2025</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7505812/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.pancreatic-cancer-multidisciplinary-consensus.2020</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.predictive-value-prevalence.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Suspicious thyroid nodule meeting biopsy criteria

**Complaint:** Positive-test question

**Investigational premise:** Diagnostic or screening blood test (test).

**Person and setting:** Claire Bennett; age 36, Female.

**Story:** Claire Bennett, a 36-year-old woman, has a suspicious thyroid nodule meeting biopsy criteria, with needle sampling planned. An study offers a new screening blood test and a separate validation table. The patient asks, 'Among the people with a positive result, how many actually had cancer?' Study enrollment remains undecided, and the table describes a constructed cohort rather than today's biopsy.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational thyroid-cancer blood test: invented practice counts from a separate validation cohort. Cells count people; reference classification is treated as correct. Not real test performance or a personal disease probability.

| Group / reference | New blood test positive | New blood test negative |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Thyroid cancer present by reference | 40 | 20 |
| Thyroid cancer absent by reference | 10 | 30 |

**Question:** Among positive results in the separate constructed validation cohort, what is the new investigational breast-cancer blood test's positive predictive value?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. 80% **(key)**

Rationale: Start with all positive screens: 40 + 10 = 50. Forty had the condition, so positive predictive value is 40/50 = 80%.

B. 75%

Rationale: The calculation 30/(10 + 30) = 75% uses the condition-absent row. It answers a specificity question, not the positive-screen question asked.

C. 20%

Rationale: The calculation 10/(40 + 10) = 20% is the false-positive share among positives. The requested predictive value counts true positives instead.

D. 67%

Rationale: The calculation 40/(40 + 20) is about 67%. It begins with people who have the condition and therefore calculates sensitivity.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Positive predictive value asks, of all positive results, how many were true positives? Its denominator includes true and false positives. Here 40 of 50 positives had the condition, giving 80%. Sensitivity instead starts with people known to have the condition. Remember which group the question starts with before choosing a row or column.

**Short explanation fallback:** Read the positive-screen column: 40 true positives and 10 false positives. Positive predictive value is 40/50 = 80% for these practice data.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.predictive-value-prevalence`, `claim.thyroid-nodule.fna-after-selected-ultrasound`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.sensitivity-specificity`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/csels/dsepd/ss1978/lesson5/appendixa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.test-accuracy.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/coronavirus-covid-19-and-medical-devices/antibody-serology-testing-covid-19-information-patients-and-consumers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.fda.predictive-values.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.thyroid.org/thyroid-nodules/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.ata-thyroid-nodules</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.predictive-value-prevalence.v4`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Primary aldosteronism

**Complaint:** Positive-test question

**Investigational premise:** Urinary adrenal-disease assay (test).

**Person and setting:** Noah Walker; age 54, Male.

**Story:** Noah Walker, a 54-year-old man, is being evaluated for primary aldosteronism and asks about a new urinary assay. Its report compares two hypothetical testing populations. The representative promises one simple answer, then offers three subscription levels for accessing it. The patient notices different positive-result predictions despite identical sensitivity and specificity and asks what could explain that difference.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational primary-aldosteronism urinary assay: invented testing-population assumptions for practice. These comparisons supply no real test performance or personal disease probability.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Testing process and result definitions | Same at both hypothetical populations |
| Sensitivity and specificity | Same at both hypothetical populations |
| Positive predictive value | Different at the two hypothetical populations |

**Question:** Which difference can explain the new investigational breast-cancer blood test's different positive predictive values when testing definitions, sensitivity and specificity remain the same?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Different prevalence in the tested populations **(key)**

Rationale: Prevalence can change the true-positive share among positive results even with identical testing processes, sensitivity, and specificity. It is a possible explanation, not an exact value.

B. Different sensitivity in the tested populations

Rationale: The exhibit explicitly keeps sensitivity the same. A sensitivity difference cannot explain this exercise without contradicting that condition.

C. Different specificity in the tested populations
