Rationale: The exhibit explicitly keeps specificity the same. This proposed explanation changes an assumption that was held fixed.

D. Different definitions of a positive test result

Rationale: The exhibit keeps the testing process and definition of positive the same. Reclassifying results contradicts that supplied comparison.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Sensitivity and specificity describe results within condition-status groups; positive predictive value starts with positive results. Changing prevalence can change the mix of true and false positives even when those test characteristics stay fixed. Here prevalence is a possible explanation. The information does not supply its exact value or prove a personal diagnosis.

**Short explanation fallback:** Different prevalence can produce different positive predictive values despite identical test characteristics. The exhibit explicitly holds sensitivity, specificity, and result definitions constant.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.predictive-value-prevalence`, `claim.primary-aldosteronism.prepared-arr-screening`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.sensitivity-specificity`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.endocrine.org/clinical-practice-guidelines/primary-aldosteronism-2" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.primary-aldosteronism-endocrine-society-2025</a>; <a href="https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/csels/dsepd/ss1978/lesson5/appendixa.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.test-accuracy.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/coronavirus-covid-19-and-medical-devices/antibody-serology-testing-covid-19-information-patients-and-consumers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.fda.predictive-values.2026</a>.

## Distinguish risk ratio and odds ratio

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Acute calculous cholecystitis in a fit adult

**Complaint:** acute cholecystitis study

**Investigational premise:** Gallbladder dissection instrument (device).

**Person and setting:** Sam Patel; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Sam Patel, the biliary journal-club lead, reviews a new dissection instrument for acute cholecystitis operations. The observational study reports surgical-site infection within thirty days. Its sponsor requests a balanced account: equal space for the data and the company's response to the data. The report supplies risks in exposed and unexposed groups, and the team is reviewing the comparison.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational gallbladder dissection instrument study: invented thirty-day SSI estimates for practice. These describe the constructed study, not real efficacy or personal risk.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Exposed group risk | 20% |
| Unexposed group risk | 10% |

**Question:** What risk ratio compares exposed with unexposed participants for the clinical outcome in the constructed cohort?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Risk ratio of 2.0 **(key)**

Rationale: Divide exposed risk by unexposed risk: 0.20/0.10 = 2.0. The inputs are probabilities, so the result is a risk ratio.

B. Odds ratio of 2.0

Rationale: The quotient 0.20/0.10 compares risks, not odds. Calling that number an odds ratio changes the scale without doing an odds calculation.

C. Risk ratio of 0.5

Rationale: The value 0.5 comes from 0.10/0.20, reversing the requested exposed-versus-unexposed direction.

D. Odds ratio of 0.5

Rationale: This both reverses the comparison and assigns an odds label to a calculation of probabilities. Neither change preserves the requested estimate.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Risk is a probability; a risk ratio divides one group's probability by the comparator's. Here 20%/10% = 2.0 for exposed versus unexposed over 30 days. An odds ratio compares odds instead. Remember both the scale and direction: reversing the groups gives a reciprocal, while changing the label changes the claimed measure.

**Short explanation fallback:** Dividing the exposed risk by the unexposed risk gives 0.20/0.10 = 2.0, a risk ratio. That calculation does not become an odds ratio by relabeling it.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio`, `claim.gs028c.cholecystitis.index-admission-cholecystectomy`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/design-conduct-analyze-field-studies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-study-design.2026</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7643471/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.wses.cholecystitis.2020</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Resectable proximal gastric adenocarcinoma

**Complaint:** gastric adenocarcinoma study

**Investigational premise:** Bowel anastomosis technique or instrument (technique).

**Person and setting:** Casey Bennett; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Casey Bennett, the upper-GI research lead, reviews a new anastomosis technique used during resection of gastric adenocarcinoma. Investigators selected people with a postoperative leak and controls without a leak, then reviewed previous technique exposure. The table gives exposure counts for those selected groups. The report does not supply leak risks in the entire surgical population under study.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational gastric anastomosis technique study: invented practice counts of people selected by thirty-day leak status. Cells count prior technique exposure; population leak risks are not supplied.

| Group / reference | Exposed | Unexposed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cases | 18 | 12 |
| Controls | 10 | 12 |

**Question:** Which association measure can be calculated directly from the case-control exposure table for postoperative leak?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Exposure odds ratio of 1.8 **(key)**

Rationale: Compare exposure odds in cases and controls: (18/12)/(10/12) = 1.8. This gives an exposure odds ratio for the supplied outcome-selected sample.

B. Exposure risk ratio of 1.8

Rationale: The sampling plan selects cases and controls by outcome. Their totals do not provide the population risks needed to justify a risk-ratio label.

C. Exposure odds ratio of 0.56

Rationale: About 0.56 is the reciprocal of 1.8. It describes controls versus cases, reversing the requested exposure-odds comparison.

D. Exposure risk ratio of 0.56

Rationale: This reverses the direction and uses a risk scale unsupported by the outcome-selected sampling totals.

**Visible teachingPoint:** In this outcome-selected table, compare exposure odds in cases with exposure odds in controls: (18/12)/(10/12) = 1.8. That is an exposure odds ratio. The sampled case and control totals do not supply population event risks. Remember what the design selected before deciding whether a risk-ratio label is justified.

**Short explanation fallback:** The exposure odds ratio is (18/12)/(10/12) = 1.8 for cases versus controls. Population risks are not supplied, so a risk-ratio label is unsupported.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio`, `claim.gastric-adenocarcinoma.prophylactic-splenectomy-no-survival-benefit`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/design-conduct-analyze-field-studies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-study-design.2026</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9813208/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.japanese-gastric-cancer-guideline.2021</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27280511/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.jcog0110.prophylactic-splenectomy.2017</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11739648/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.korean-gastric-cancer-guideline.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Merkel cell carcinoma

**Complaint:** Merkel cell carcinoma study

**Investigational premise:** Cancer recurrence-prevention biologic (drug).

**Person and setting:** Jamie Reed; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jamie Reed, the skin-oncology discussion lead, reviews a new biologic for Merkel cell carcinoma. An adjusted model reports recurrence odds over two years. The representative offers to simplify the statistics by removing the parts customers ask about. The report supplies an adjusted odds ratio, but no underlying group risks; the team is reviewing the wording of its result.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational Merkel-cell biologic study: invented two-year recurrence estimates for practice. These describe the constructed study, not real efficacy or personal risk.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Reported measure | Adjusted odds ratio |
| Estimate | 0.60 |
| Underlying group risks | Not supplied |

**Question:** How should the adjusted odds ratio of 0.60 for two-year recurrence be described when group risks are unavailable?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Adjusted odds are 0.60 times as high **(key)**

Rationale: The reported adjusted odds ratio is 0.60. Describing the adjusted odds as 0.60 times the comparison odds keeps the estimate on its reported scale.

B. Adjusted risk is exactly 0.60 times as high

Rationale: The model reports odds, not an exact ratio of probabilities. Group risks needed for that asserted risk comparison are not supplied.

C. Absolute risk falls by 40 percentage points

Rationale: An odds ratio alone does not provide an absolute risk difference. Subtracting it from one cannot create a 40-percentage-point reduction.

D. Adjusted event probability is 40% lower

Rationale: An odds ratio of 0.60 cannot automatically be translated into a 40% reduction in event probability. That changes odds into risk without the needed information.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Odds and probability are different scales. An adjusted odds ratio of 0.60 says the compared odds are 0.60 times the reference odds, within the reported model. It does not supply the same proportional probability reduction or an absolute risk difference. Keep the measure's name attached to its number when translating a report.

**Short explanation fallback:** The estimate describes adjusted odds at 0.60 times the comparison odds. Without group risks, it cannot provide the asserted exact risk ratio or absolute risk reduction.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio`, `claim.gs028g.merkel-cell.integrated-diagnosis`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/design-conduct-analyze-field-studies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-study-design.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/skin/hp/merkel-cell-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028g.nci-merkel-pdq-2025</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio.v4`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Marginal ulcer after gastric bypass

**Complaint:** marginal ulcer study

**Investigational premise:** Marginal-ulcer protective agent (drug).

**Person and setting:** Taylor Hayes; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Taylor Hayes, the bariatric journal-club lead, reviews a new protective agent for marginal ulcer after gastric bypass. The observational report labels its estimate as an exposure odds ratio for rare ulcer recurrence. The editor requests a more exciting conclusion while retaining 'strictly factual.' Underlying group risks are unavailable, and the investigators discuss approximate similarity to a risk ratio.

**Exhibit — Ulcer recurrence — illustrative study:** An invented study of an unnamed investigational marginal-ulcer protective agent reports an exposure odds ratio for rare one-year ulcer recurrence. Authors discuss approximate similarity to a risk ratio, but underlying group risks are unavailable. These practice conditions supply no real efficacy or clinical risk estimate.

**Question:** Which interpretation preserves the reported measure when an exposure odds ratio for rare ulcer recurrence is discussed as approximately similar to a risk ratio?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Report the estimate as an odds ratio **(key)**

Rationale: The report actually estimates an odds ratio. Retaining its label preserves the measure despite the stated approximate similarity for this rare outcome.

B. Report the estimate as a relative risk

Rationale: Approximate similarity does not establish an exact risk ratio. Baseline risks for a direct risk comparison are not supplied.

C. Report the estimate as a relative risk reduction

Rationale: A relative risk reduction is a different claim from the reported odds ratio. The supplied information does not justify relabeling the measure that way.

D. Report the estimate as an absolute risk difference

Rationale: An absolute risk difference requires probabilities to subtract. The reported odds ratio and missing baseline risks do not supply those values.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Approximate similarity is not permission to rename an estimate. The report gives an odds ratio, so retain that label even when discussing a rare outcome. Without the underlying risks, the exhibit does not supply an exact risk ratio or an absolute difference. A useful reporting rule is to preserve the scale you actually estimated.

**Short explanation fallback:** Report the estimate as an odds ratio. The stated approximate similarity does not convert it into an exact risk ratio or supply an absolute risk difference.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.risk-ratio-odds-ratio`, `claim.gs028c.marginal-ulcer.h-pylori`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://asmbs.org/resources/asmbs-literature-review-on-the-treatment-of-marginal-ulcers-after-metabolic-and-bariatric-surgery/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.asmbs.marginal-ulcer.2024</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/design-conduct-analyze-field-studies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-study-design.2026</a>.

## Calculate absolute risk reduction and NNT

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.arr-nnt`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.arr-nnt.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Localized resectable colon cancer

**Complaint:** Surgical-study question

**Investigational premise:** Cancer recurrence-prevention biologic (drug).

**Person and setting:** Maya Reed; age 61, Female.

**Story:** Maya Reed, a 61-year-old woman, has biopsy-confirmed localized colon cancer and is discussing resection. An optional trial tests a new monoclonal antibody, reporting recurrence over two years. Its brochure announces 'a major improvement' in a typeface larger than the results table. The patient asks what absolute benefit the study showed and how many participants needed treatment for one fewer recurrence.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational monoclonal antibody trial: invented practice counts of colon-cancer recurrence within two years. Cells count recurrences and people followed. Follow-up is complete; not real efficacy or personal risk.

| Group / reference | Recurrence within two years | People followed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Control arm | 20 | 100 |
| New unnamed intervention | 10 | 100 |

**Question:** For colon-cancer recurrence over two years in the constructed trial, what ARR and NNT describe the new unnamed intervention versus control?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. ARR 10 percentage points; NNT 10 over two years **(key)**

Rationale: The practice risks are 20/100 and 10/100. Subtracting gives 0.10; 1/0.10 = 10 for the specified outcome over two years.

B. ARR 50 percentage points; NNT 2 over two years

Rationale: The outcome risk halves relative to its 20% starting value, but falls by 10 percentage points, not 50. The relative wording does not supply this ARR or NNT.

C. ARR 10 percentage points; NNT 100 over two years

Rationale: The absolute difference is right, but its reciprocal uses the proportion 0.10: 1/0.10 = 10, not 100.

D. ARR 5 percentage points; NNT 20 over two years

Rationale: An NNT of 20 would correspond to an absolute difference of 0.05. These practice risks differ by 0.10 instead.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Calculate each group's risk first, then subtract intervention risk from comparator risk. Here 20% minus 10% is 10 percentage points, so NNT is 1/0.10 = 10 over two years. Half the starting risk and a 10-point absolute drop describe the same practice numbers; keep the outcome, comparator, and period attached.

**Short explanation fallback:** The practice risks are 20/100 and 10/100. Their difference is 0.10, giving ARR 10 percentage points and NNT 10 for the specified two-year outcome.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.arr-nnt`, `claim.colorectal.histology-confirms`, `claim.colon-cancer.oncologic-regional-resection`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ascrsu.com/ascrs/view/ASCRS-Toolkit/2851068/all/Management_" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board-batch.ascrs-colon-2022</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/colorectal/hp/colon-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board-batch.nci-colon-2025</a>; <a href="https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-15" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cochrane.chapter-15.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/colorectal/screening-fact-sheet" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nci.colorectal-screening.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.arr-nnt.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Suspected or confirmed cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma

**Complaint:** Research-results discussion

**Investigational premise:** Topical wound or skin agent (drug).

**Person and setting:** Owen Reed; age 38, Male.

**Story:** Owen Reed, a 38-year-old man, has a resected low-risk cutaneous SCC and asks about an optional new topical-agent trial reporting recurrence over three years. The investigator calls the calculation obvious, then opens a spreadsheet containing twelve hidden tabs. The arms have different totals. The patient wants the absolute benefit and treatment number for the stated follow-up period.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational topical agent trial: invented practice counts of cutaneous-SCC recurrence within three years. Cells count recurrences and people followed. Follow-up is complete; not real efficacy or personal risk.

| Group / reference | Recurrence within three years | People followed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Control arm | 30 | 200 |
| New unnamed intervention | 10 | 100 |

**Question:** For cutaneous-SCC recurrence over three years in the constructed trial, what ARR and NNT describe the new unnamed intervention versus control?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. ARR 5 percentage points; NNT 20 over three years **(key)**

Rationale: First divide within each group: 30/200 = 0.15 and 10/100 = 0.10. ARR is 0.05 and its reciprocal is NNT 20 over three years.

B. ARR 20 percentage points; NNT 5 over three years

Rationale: Subtracting 30 and 10 events ignores that the groups contain 200 and 100 people. Risks, rather than raw counts, must be subtracted.

C. ARR 33 percentage points; NNT 3 over three years

Rationale: About 33% describes the relative reduction from 15% to 10%. The absolute drop is five percentage points, giving NNT 20.

D. ARR 10 percentage points; NNT 10 over three years

Rationale: The comparator risk is 30/200 = 15%, not 20%. Using the actual denominator gives a five-point difference rather than ten.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Unequal group sizes make raw event-count subtraction misleading. Convert each count to a risk: 30/200 = 15% and 10/100 = 10%. Their absolute difference is five percentage points, so NNT is 1/0.05 = 20 over three years. Remember: divide within groups before subtracting between groups, retaining the specified recurrence outcome and comparator.

**Short explanation fallback:** First calculate risks: 30/200 = 0.15 and 10/100 = 0.10. ARR is 0.05, or five percentage points, and three-year NNT is 20.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.arr-nnt`, `claim.cutaneous-scc.biopsy-for-diagnosis-and-risk`, `claim.cutaneous-scc.risk-directed-surgery`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-15" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cochrane.chapter-15.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/guidelines/scc" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.csc.aad-guideline.2018</a>; <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/clinical-care/wisely" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.csc.aad-mohs-wisely.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.arr-nnt.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Primary spontaneous pneumothorax

**Complaint:** Evidence review

**Investigational premise:** Pleural recurrence-prevention device (device).

**Person and setting:** Claire Bennett; age 47, Female.

**Story:** Claire Bennett, a 47-year-old woman, has recovered from a primary spontaneous pneumothorax and is discussing recurrence prevention. An optional study tested a new pleural device, reporting recurrence over five years. The patient compares the two trial arms and asks how the absolute benefit translates into the number treated for one fewer recurrence during that stated period.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational pleural device trial: invented practice counts of pneumothorax recurrence within five years. Cells count recurrences and people followed. Follow-up is complete; not real efficacy or personal risk.

| Group / reference | Recurrence within five years | People followed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Control arm | 30 | 100 |
| New unnamed intervention | 10 | 100 |

**Question:** For pneumothorax recurrence over five years in the constructed trial, what ARR and NNT describe the new unnamed intervention versus control?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. ARR 20 percentage points; NNT 5 over five years **(key)**

Rationale: Subtract 10/100 from 30/100 to get 0.20. The reciprocal is 1/0.20 = 5 for the same comparator and five-year outcome.

B. ARR 67 percentage points; NNT 2 over five years

Rationale: The risk falls by about two thirds relative to its starting value, but the absolute drop is 20 percentage points, not 67.

C. ARR 20 percentage points; NNT 50 over five years

Rationale: The ARR is correct, but 1/0.20 is 5. An NNT of 50 is a reciprocal calculation error.

D. ARR 10 percentage points; NNT 10 over five years

Rationale: The two practice risks are 30% and 10%, a 20-point difference. A ten-point reduction would describe different inputs.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Absolute reduction subtracts risks on the same scale: 30% minus 10% is 20 percentage points. Taking the reciprocal of 0.20 gives NNT 5 for this comparator and outcome over five years. The relative reduction uses a different starting denominator. Remember that percentage points, percentages, and proportions are not interchangeable labels.

**Short explanation fallback:** The practice outcome risks differ by 0.30 - 0.10 = 0.20. ARR is 20 percentage points and NNT is 5 over the stated five-year period.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.arr-nnt`, `claim.gs028d.psp.recurrence-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-15" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cochrane.chapter-15.2026</a>; <a href="https://publications.ersnet.org/content/erj/63/5/2300797" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ers-eacts-ests.pneumothorax.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.arr-nnt.v4`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Gastric gastrointestinal stromal tumor

**Complaint:** Study interpretation

**Investigational premise:** Cancer recurrence-prevention biologic (drug).

**Person and setting:** Noah Walker; age 73, Male.

**Story:** Noah Walker, a 73-year-old man, has a resected gastric GIST and asks about an optional trial of a new biologic reporting recurrence over two years. The patient brings a calculator: 'They called this simple arithmetic, so I assumed they'd brought someone to explain it.' The discussion concerns absolute benefit and the treatment number calculated from the trial's results.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational biologic trial: invented practice counts of gastric-GIST recurrence within two years. Cells count recurrences and people followed. Follow-up is complete; not real efficacy or personal risk.

| Group / reference | Recurrence within two years | People followed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Control arm | 20 | 100 |
| New unnamed intervention | 14 | 100 |

**Question:** For gastric-GIST recurrence over two years in the constructed trial, what ARR and NNT describe the new unnamed intervention versus control?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. ARR 6 percentage points; NNT 17 over two years **(key)**

Rationale: The absolute difference is 0.20 - 0.14 = 0.06. Its reciprocal is about 16.67, conventionally rounded upward to NNT 17 over two years.

B. ARR 6 percentage points; NNT 16 over two years

Rationale: The six-point difference is right, but conventionally rounding NNT upward gives 17. Rounding down to 16 changes that reporting convention.

C. ARR 30 percentage points; NNT 4 over two years

Rationale: Thirty percent is the relative reduction from the 20% comparator risk. The absolute reduction is six percentage points, not thirty.

D. ARR 14 percentage points; NNT 8 over two years

Rationale: Fourteen percent is the intervention risk, not the difference between group risks. Subtracting from the comparator gives six percentage points.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Subtract the risks before taking a reciprocal: 20% minus 14% is six percentage points, or 0.06. Then 1/0.06 is about 16.67; conventionally round NNT upward to 17. Keep the specified recurrence outcome, comparator, and two-year period with the result. The intervention's 14% risk is not itself the reduction.

**Short explanation fallback:** ARR is 0.20 - 0.14 = 0.06. Its reciprocal is about 16.67, conventionally rounded up to NNT 17 for the stated two-year outcome.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.arr-nnt`, `claim.gastric-gist.selected-eus-core-molecular-testing`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://grupogeis.org/documentos/guias-geis/2023-GEIS-guidelines-for-GIST.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.gist-geis-2023</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/soft-tissue-sarcoma/hp/gist-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.gist-nci-pdq-2024</a>; <a href="https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-15" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cochrane.chapter-15.2026</a>.

## Interpret a p-value

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.p-value`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.p-value.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Acute calculous cholecystitis in a fit adult

**Complaint:** acute cholecystitis study

**Investigational premise:** Gallbladder dissection instrument (device).

**Person and setting:** Sam Patel; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Sam Patel, the biliary journal-club lead, reviews a new dissection instrument used in acute cholecystitis operations. The trial compares thirty-day surgical-site infection and reports its preplanned analysis. Investigators specified the null model before collecting outcomes. The team has the p-value but is reviewing what that value says about the infection results before drafting the study's written conclusion.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational gallbladder dissection instrument trial: invented analysis of thirty-day SSI for practice. The null specifies no group difference; the p-value is not an effect size or personal outcome probability.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Null model and analysis | Specified before data collection |
| p-value | 0.03 |

**Question:** Which interpretation is justified by the reported p-value for thirty-day SSI in the constructed trial?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. A 3% chance of results this extreme or more under the null model **(key)**

Rationale: Assume the specified null model and analysis conditions. Results this incompatible with that model, or more so, have probability 0.03; the probability is conditional on the model.

B. A 3% probability that the null hypothesis is true after observing these results

Rationale: This reverses the conditioning. The p-value assumes the null model to evaluate data, rather than assigning a 3% probability to the null hypothesis after observing data.

C. A 97% probability that the intervention truly changed the outcome after observing these results

Rationale: One minus p does not supply a posterior probability that the intervention truly changed scores. The complement 0.97 is not that truth probability.

D. An estimated difference of 0.03 outcome units between the two groups being compared

Rationale: The 0.03 is a probability under a null model, not a difference in outcome units. Read the effect magnitude from its separately reported estimate.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A null model is the comparison's starting assumption. A p-value asks how probable data this incompatible with that model, or more so, would be under the model and analysis conditions. Here that probability is 0.03. Remember: probability of data under a model is not probability that the model is true, and is not effect size.

**Short explanation fallback:** Under the specified null model, results this extreme or more have probability 0.03. That is neither the probability of a true null nor the size of the clinical difference.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.p-value`, `claim.gs028c.cholecystitis.index-admission-cholecystectomy`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc131.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.p-values.2026</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7643471/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.wses.cholecystitis.2020</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.p-value.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** External full-thickness rectal prolapse

**Complaint:** rectal prolapse study

**Investigational premise:** Rectal-prolapse fixation technique (technique).

**Person and setting:** Casey Bennett; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Casey Bennett, the colorectal research lead, reviews a new fixation technique for rectal prolapse and recurrence within two years. The press officer wants a headline saying the study settled the issue; the statistician has been offered a footnote in exchange for missing the press conference. The report includes its preplanned null model and the p-value from that analysis.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational rectal-prolapse fixation technique trial: invented analysis of two-year recurrence for practice. The null specifies no group difference; the p-value is not an effect size or personal outcome probability.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Null model and analysis | Specified before data collection |
| p-value | 0.40 |

**Question:** Which interpretation is justified by the reported p-value for two-year recurrence in the constructed trial?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. A 40% chance of results this extreme or more under the null model **(key)**

Rationale: The 0.40 is the probability of data this extreme or more under the specified null model and its analysis assumptions. A larger number retains the same conditioning.

B. A 40% probability that the null hypothesis is true after observing these results

Rationale: A p-value does not estimate the probability that the null is true. The 0.40 concerns data calculated assuming the null model.

C. A 60% probability that no group difference exists after observing these results

Rationale: Subtracting p from one gives 0.60 arithmetically, but does not make that the probability of no effect.

D. An estimated difference of 0.40 outcome units between the two groups being compared

Rationale: A probability under the null model is not a 0.40-unit outcome difference. Effect magnitude requires its own estimate.

**Visible teachingPoint:** The null model supplies a starting comparison assumption. A p-value describes the probability of data this extreme or more if that model and its analysis assumptions hold. Here that probability is 0.40. Neither p nor one minus p gives the probability of no effect. Effect size requires a separate estimate.

**Short explanation fallback:** The 0.40 describes data at least this extreme under the specified null model. It does not assign 40% or 60% probability to a hypothesis or quantify the effect.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.p-value`, `claim.brief.rectal-prolapse.surgical-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ascrsu.com/ascrs/repview?name=2_2851051_PDF&amp;type=784-162" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.ascrs-rectal-prolapse-2017</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc131.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.p-values.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.p-value.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Localized low-grade gastric MALT lymphoma associated with H. pylori

**Complaint:** gastric MALT lymphoma study

**Investigational premise:** Gastric lymphoma recurrence-prevention agent (drug).

**Person and setting:** Jamie Reed; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jamie Reed, the gastric-oncology discussion lead, reviews a new agent for gastric MALT lymphoma in an invented recurrence-prevention trial after remission. Follow-up lasts two years. The sponsor requests 'life-changing' on every slide, including the Wi-Fi password. The trial reports a very small p-value, with its effect estimate supplied separately; the team is reviewing the interpretation for its report.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational gastric-MALT agent trial: invented analysis of two-year lymphoma recurrence for practice. The null specifies no group difference; the p-value is not an effect size or personal outcome probability.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| p-value | 0.001 |
| Effect estimate | Reported separately |

**Question:** Which interpretation is justified by the reported p-value for two-year lymphoma recurrence in the constructed trial?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. A 0.1% chance of results this extreme or more under the null model **(key)**

Rationale: Under the specified null model and conditions, data this incompatible or more have probability 0.001. The small value does not change what the probability describes.

B. A 0.1% probability that the null hypothesis is true after observing these results

Rationale: The test assumes the null model when calculating this probability; it does not assign 0.001 probability to the null hypothesis itself.

C. A 99.9% probability that the intervention truly changed the outcome after observing these results

Rationale: The complement 0.999 is not a posterior probability that an effect is true. That interpretation does not follow from the reported p-value.

D. An estimated difference of 0.001 outcome units between the two groups being compared

Rationale: The number 0.001 is a probability, not an effect in outcome units. The exhibit says the effect estimate is reported separately.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A null model is a starting comparison assumption, not the conclusion. Under that model and its assumptions, results at least this incompatible have probability 0.001. A small p-value does not make the effect 0.001 units or assign 0.999 probability to its truth. Read magnitude from the separately reported estimate.

**Short explanation fallback:** The 0.001 refers to data under the specified null model, including more extreme results. It is not a posterior truth probability or the effect estimate.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.p-value`, `claim.gastric-malt.hpylori-eradication-initial-localized`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://b-s-h.org.uk/media/x0bbg4bs/guideline-for-the-diagnosis-and-management-of-marginal-zone-lymphomas-a-british-society.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bsh.marginal-zone-lymphoma-guideline.2023</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4962427/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.hu-gastric-malt-diagnosis-management.2016</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10417821/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.matysiak-budnik-gastric-malt-management.2023</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/lymphoma/hp/indolent-b-cell-lymphoma-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nci.indolent-b-cell-nhl-pdq.2025</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc131.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.p-values.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.p-value.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Symptomatic Meckel diverticulum involving adjacent ileum

**Complaint:** Meckel diverticulum study

**Investigational premise:** Bowel anastomosis technique or instrument (technique).

**Person and setting:** Taylor Hayes; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Taylor Hayes, the emergency-surgery journal-club lead, reviews a new anastomosis instrument after ileal resection for symptomatic Meckel diverticulum. The trial records leak within thirty days. Its analysis does not reject the prespecified null model, and a draft conclusion says the instruments have exactly equal effects. The report gives the p-value and that proposed conclusion for the team's discussion.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational Meckel resection anastomosis instrument trial: invented analysis of thirty-day leak for practice. The null specifies no group difference; the p-value is not an effect size or personal outcome probability.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| p-value | 0.08 |
| Draft claim | The groups are exactly identical |

**Question:** Which interpretation is justified by the reported p-value for thirty-day leak in the constructed trial?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. The result does not prove exact equality **(key)**

Rationale: The p-value does not prove a zero group difference. The exact-equality sentence claims more than the reported probability establishes.

B. The result favors equal group effects

Rationale: Failure to establish a difference is not proof or a supplied probability of equality. The p-value alone does not settle whether the means are exactly equal.

C. The result indicates a small clinical difference

Rationale: The exhibit supplies no effect magnitude or clinical-importance criterion. A p-value alone cannot establish a small clinical difference.

D. The result gives an 8% probability of no effect

Rationale: The 0.08 describes data conditional on a null model, not an 8% probability that no effect exists after observing the data.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A null model is a starting comparison assumption; a p-value describes data under that model. It does not establish exact group equality, the probability of no effect, or the clinical size of a difference. Remember the boundary: this number alone cannot certify equality, magnitude, or clinical importance.

**Short explanation fallback:** The reported p-value does not prove exact equality. It also does not give an 8% probability of no effect or establish that any difference is clinically small.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.p-value`, `claim.meckel-diverticulum.resection-extent-base-and-ileum`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/surgery/articles/10.3389/fsurg.2018.00055/full" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.blouhos.meckel-adult-surgical-concerns.2018</a>; <a href="https://www.medsci.org/v09p0243.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ding.perforated-meckel-adults.2012</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc131.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.p-values.2026</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36821518/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.tree.meckel-diverticulectomy-multicentre.2023</a>.

## Interpret a confidence interval

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.confidence-interval`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.confidence-interval.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Stable reducible femoral hernia

**Complaint:** femoral hernia study

**Investigational premise:** Hernia reinforcement material or technique (device).

**Person and setting:** Dana Rivera; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Dana Rivera, the groin-surgery research lead, reviews a new reinforcement material for femoral hernia repair. The trial reports recurrence within two years as a risk ratio with an interval. The sponsor welcomes uncertainty in principle, but asks whether it can be moved to the appendix. The team is reviewing how the reported range relates to the no-difference value.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational femoral-hernia reinforcement material: invented two-year recurrence risk ratio, unitless ratio (new/control), for practice. No real efficacy or clinical-importance threshold.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Estimate | 0.70 |
| 95% confidence interval | 0.40 to 1.20 |

**Question:** How does the constructed two-year recurrence risk-ratio interval relate to its null value, and what interpretation is justified?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. The interval includes the null value 1 **(key)**

Rationale: One lies between 0.40 and 1.20, so the interval includes the no-association value for a ratio. Inclusion does not establish an exactly true ratio of one.

B. The interval excludes the null value 1

Rationale: The upper limit is 1.20 and the lower limit is 0.40. One is inside these limits, so it is not excluded.

C. The interval gives 95% probability of a real association

Rationale: The 95% level describes coverage of the interval method. It does not give a 95% probability that an association is real.

D. The interval supports an exactly absent association

Rationale: An interval containing one is compatible with the ratio null value, but also contains other ratios. It does not prove that the association is exactly absent.

**Visible teachingPoint:** For a ratio, the no-association value is 1, because the compared quantities are equal. One falls within 0.40 to 1.20, so this interval includes that null value. Inclusion is not proof that the true ratio is exactly 1. Remember the measure's scale before looking for its null value.

**Short explanation fallback:** The ratio interval from 0.40 to 1.20 contains 1. It is compatible with the ratio null value, without proving an exactly absent association.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.confidence-interval`, `claim.bread-butter.femoral-hernia.timely-repair`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5809582/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.herniasurge-2018</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc14.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.confidence-intervals.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda352.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.confidence-limits-mean.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.confidence-interval.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Chronic pilonidal sinus disease

**Complaint:** pilonidal disease study

**Investigational premise:** Pilonidal excision or closure technique (technique).

**Person and setting:** Alex Morgan; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Alex Morgan, the colorectal journal-club lead, reviews a new operative closure technique for chronic pilonidal disease. The trial reports recurrence within two years as an absolute risk difference with a confidence interval. One colleague says the range establishes an important benefit. The report provides no threshold for what difference would matter to patients, and the team is reviewing that interpretation.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational pilonidal closure technique: invented two-year recurrence risk difference, percentage points (new minus control), for practice. No real efficacy or clinical-importance threshold.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Estimate | -4 |
| 95% confidence interval | -6 to -2 |

**Question:** Which statement is justified by the constructed two-year recurrence risk difference of -4 percentage points with a 95% interval from -6 to -2?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. The interval excludes the null value 0 **(key)**

Rationale: All values from -6 to -2 are below zero. The interval therefore excludes zero, the no-difference value on this scale.

B. The interval includes the null value 0

Rationale: Zero is outside the stated limits. The null for a difference is zero, even though a ratio would use one.

C. The interval gives 95% probability of clinical benefit

Rationale: The confidence level is not a probability that the intervention provides clinical benefit. The exercise gives no clinical-importance criterion.

D. The interval establishes a clinically important effect

Rationale: Excluding zero addresses compatibility with the difference null. It does not, by itself, establish a clinically important magnitude.

**Visible teachingPoint:** For a difference, the no-difference value is 0, rather than the ratio null of 1. Every value from -6 to -2 lies below zero, so this interval excludes the null. That describes the estimate's uncertainty on its stated scale; it does not alone establish clinical importance or a probability of benefit.

**Short explanation fallback:** The interval from -6 to -2 excludes zero, the null for a risk difference. Excluding zero does not by itself establish a clinically important effect.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.confidence-interval`, `claim.pilonidal-disease.chronic-sinus-recognition`, `claim.pilonidal-disease.off-midline-closure-planning`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc14.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.confidence-intervals.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda352.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.confidence-limits-mean.2026</a>; <a href="https://fascrs.org/Web/Web/Patients/Diseases-and-Conditions/A-Z/Pilonidal-Disease.aspx" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.ascrs-pilonidal-patient</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8580911/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.siccr-pilonidal-2021</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.confidence-interval.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Lactational breast abscess

**Complaint:** lactational breast abscess study

**Investigational premise:** Abscess drainage adjunct (device).

**Person and setting:** Riley Chen; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Riley Chen, the breast-service research lead, reviews two studies of a new aspiration device for lactational breast abscesses. Both report repeat drainage within thirty days on the same scale. A colleague prefers the paper with the most confident title; its author underlined 'definitive' twice. The intervals use the same method and confidence level, and the team is comparing precision.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational lactational-abscess aspiration device: invented thirty-day repeat-drainage risk difference, percentage points (new minus control), for practice. No real efficacy or clinical-importance threshold.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Interval A, 95% | 1 to 9 |
| Interval B, 95% | 4 to 6 |
| Both estimates | 5 |

**Question:** Which constructed repeat-drainage risk-difference interval is more precise when method, confidence level and measurement scale are the same?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. The 4-to-6 interval is more precise **(key)**

Rationale: The 4-to-6 interval is narrower than 1-to-9 under the same method, confidence level, and scale. It therefore displays greater sampling precision.

B. The 1-to-9 interval is more precise

Rationale: The 1-to-9 interval is wider. Under the supplied comparable conditions, that width displays more sampling uncertainty rather than greater precision.

C. Both intervals have equal precision

Rationale: Both point estimates are five, but the interval widths differ. A shared center does not imply equal precision.

D. Precision cannot be ranked without sample sizes

Rationale: The widths already display relative sampling precision under the same method and scale. The exercise does not require sample sizes to compare those widths.

**Visible teachingPoint:** With the same method, confidence level, and scale, a narrower interval displays less sampling uncertainty and therefore greater precision. The 4-to-6 interval is narrower than 1-to-9, even though both center on 5. Remember that the center gives the point estimate while the width conveys precision; equal centers do not imply equal uncertainty.

**Short explanation fallback:** The 4-to-6 interval is more precise because it is narrower under the same method and scale. Both estimates being 5 does not equalize the interval widths.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.confidence-interval`, `claim.lactational-breast-abscess.targeted-ultrasound`, `claim.lactational-breast-abscess.selected-drainage`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://abm.memberclicks.net/assets/DOCUMENTS/PROTOCOLS/36-mitchell-et-al-2022-academy-of-breastfeeding-medicine-clinical-protocol-36-the-mastitis-spectrum-revised-2022.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.expansion.abm-mastitis-2022</a>; <a href="https://acsearch.acr.org/docs/3196809/Narrative" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.expansion.acr-lactation-imaging-2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc14.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.confidence-intervals.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda352.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.confidence-limits-mean.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.confidence-interval.v4`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Resectable proximal gastric adenocarcinoma

**Complaint:** gastric adenocarcinoma study

**Investigational premise:** Cancer recurrence-prevention biologic (drug).

**Person and setting:** Jordan Brooks; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jordan Brooks, the upper-GI journal-club lead, reviews a new biologic after resection of gastric adenocarcinoma. Its trial reports a three-year recurrence risk difference with a frequentist interval. The presenter lists an invited lecture on his CV; the invitation came from the seminar series he organizes himself. The team is reviewing what the stated confidence level describes across repeated studies.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational gastric-cancer biologic: invented three-year recurrence risk-difference procedure, percentage points (new minus control), for practice. No real efficacy or clinical-importance threshold.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Framework | Standard frequentist procedure |
| Confidence level | 95% |

**Question:** What does a 95% confidence level describe for repeated use of the stated frequentist three-year recurrence risk-difference interval procedure?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. The method covers the true value in 95% of repetitions **(key)**

Rationale: The 95% level describes long-run coverage by intervals constructed with this procedure under its assumptions. The fixed true value is covered in that proportion of repetitions.

B. This interval has a 95% chance of containing the truth

Rationale: After observation, this frequentist framework treats the parameter as fixed. Its confidence level does not assign a fresh probability to this particular interval containing that value.

C. The null hypothesis has a 5% chance of being correct

Rationale: Neither 95% confidence nor its 5% complement supplies a truth probability for a hypothesis. That is a different question from interval coverage.

D. The estimate has a 95% chance of being unbiased

Rationale: Coverage under a procedure does not guarantee the absence of systematic bias. The confidence level is not a probability that the estimate is unbiased.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A frequentist confidence level describes repeated use of the interval method under its assumptions. In 95% of those repetitions, intervals produced by the procedure cover the fixed true value. It is a property of the method, not a new probability assigned to this observed interval or a guarantee that the estimate is unbiased.

**Short explanation fallback:** The 95% refers to long-run coverage of intervals produced by the procedure under its assumptions. It does not assign a 95% truth probability to this particular interval.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.confidence-interval`, `claim.gastric-adenocarcinoma.prophylactic-splenectomy-no-survival-benefit`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9813208/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.japanese-gastric-cancer-guideline.2021</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27280511/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.jcog0110.prophylactic-splenectomy.2017</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11739648/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.korean-gastric-cancer-guideline.2024</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc14.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.confidence-intervals.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda352.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.confidence-limits-mean.2026</a>.

## Distinguish type I error, type II error, and power

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.errors-power`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.errors-power.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Pure ductal carcinoma in situ

**Complaint:** DCIS study

**Investigational premise:** Local DCIS treatment device (device).

**Person and setting:** Sam Patel; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Sam Patel, the breast-oncology research lead, reviews a new local treatment device for DCIS. A simulation concerns recurrence within five years and stipulates the underlying state. The investigator lists one conference abstract as a presentation, publication and public-engagement project on his appraisal. The analysis rejects its null, and the team is reviewing how to classify that simulated result.

**Exhibit:** Simulated unnamed investigational local DCIS treatment device trial: the underlying state for five-year recurrence is stipulated for practice. It is not learned from a p-value or asserted for a real intervention.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Underlying state | Null hypothesis true |
| Analysis decision | Reject the null hypothesis |

**Question:** Which testing error occurs in the simulated DCIS-recurrence analysis when the null is true but the analysis rejects it?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Type I error **(key)**

Rationale: The underlying null is stipulated to be true, yet the analysis rejects it. That truth-and-decision combination defines a type I error.

B. Type II error

Rationale: Type II error would mean failing to reject under a specified true alternative. Both the underlying state and decision differ from this exhibit.

C. Measurement bias

Rationale: The exhibit gives the hypothesis state and decision, without describing inconsistent outcome measurement. It identifies a testing error rather than that bias mechanism.

D. Selection bias

Rationale: No systematic exclusion from recruitment is supplied. The stated problem is rejection of a true null, not a selection process.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Testing errors compare a decision with the underlying state. Type I error means rejecting a null that is actually true; type II means failing to reject when a specified alternative is true. Here the null is true and was rejected. Remember: first identify what is true, then what the analysis decided.

**Short explanation fallback:** Rejecting a true null is a type I error. The exhibit supplies the true state and testing decision, not evidence of a measurement or recruitment mechanism.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.errors-power`, `claim.gs028c.dcis.margin-two-mm`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.bias-confounding`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.breastsurgeons.org/docs/statements/asbrs-breast-conservation-surgery-margins.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.asbrs.bcs-margins.2024</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda35.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.errors-power.2026</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5477830/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sso-astro-asco.dcis-margin.2016</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.errors-power.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Solitary hepatocellular carcinoma in a favorable resection profile

**Complaint:** hepatocellular carcinoma study

**Investigational premise:** Hepatic resection instrument (device).

**Person and setting:** Casey Bennett; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Casey Bennett, the liver-surgery discussion lead, reviews a new resection instrument for solitary hepatocellular carcinoma in a favorable operative profile. A simulation concerns recurrence within three years and stipulates that a specified difference exists. The analysis fails to reject its null model. The team has the underlying state and analysis decision available and is reviewing the testing-error classification.

**Exhibit:** Simulated unnamed investigational hepatic resection instrument trial: the underlying state for three-year HCC recurrence is stipulated for practice. It is not learned from a p-value or asserted for a real intervention.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Underlying state | Specified alternative true |
| Analysis decision | Fail to reject the null hypothesis |

**Question:** Which testing error occurs in the simulated HCC-recurrence analysis when the specified alternative is true but the analysis fails to reject the null?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Type II error **(key)**

Rationale: The specified alternative is stipulated to be true, but the test fails to reject the null. That combination defines a type II error.

B. Type I error

Rationale: Type I error requires rejection under a true null. This exercise instead supplies nonrejection under a true alternative.

C. Measurement bias

Rationale: No unequal outcome-recording process is described. The facts identify a missed specified alternative by the test.

D. Selection bias

Rationale: The exhibit does not describe systematic sampling exclusion. The testing decision, compared with the supplied true alternative, identifies the error.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Failure to reject does not guarantee a true null. If the specified alternative is actually true and the analysis misses it, the decision is a type II error. Type I is the opposite truth-and-decision combination: rejection under a true null. Keep the underlying state separate from the decision reported by the test.

**Short explanation fallback:** This is a type II error: the specified alternative is true, yet the test fails to reject the null. Nonrejection does not establish that no effect exists.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.errors-power`, `claim.hcc.resection.favorable-compensated-cirrhosis-profile`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.bias-confounding`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://doi.org/10.1097/HEP.0000000000000466" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.aasld.hcc-practice-guidance.2023</a>; <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/field-epi-manual/php/chapters/analyze-interpret-data.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.cdc.field-epi-analysis.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda35.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.errors-power.2026</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.errors-power.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Resectable pancreatic body or tail adenocarcinoma

**Complaint:** pancreatic cancer study

**Investigational premise:** Pancreatic-cancer radiation delivery technique (technique).

**Person and setting:** Jamie Reed; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jamie Reed, the pancreatic-oncology research lead, reviews a new radiation-delivery technique after pancreatic tail cancer resection. Its trial is planned around recurrence within two years. The grant writer wants a guaranteed discovery and asks whether 'statistically likely' can be upgraded in the budget justification. The calculation specifies the effect, variability and sampling assumptions before any outcomes are collected.

**Exhibit — Recurrence trial — illustrative analysis:** An unnamed investigational pancreatic-cancer radiation technique study of two-year recurrence. Before collecting data, investigators specify the recurrence effect, variability, alpha and sampling design. Power is calculated under that alternative. These study conditions are invented for practice, not real efficacy findings.

**Question:** What does prospective power describe under the specified alternative in the constructed pancreatic-cancer recurrence trial?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Chance of rejection if that alternative is true **(key)**

Rationale: Under the specified true alternative and design assumptions, power is the probability of rejecting the null. It describes how the planned procedure behaves in that situation.

B. Chance that a statistically positive result is true

Rationale: This reverses what is conditioned on. Power assumes an alternative to evaluate rejection, rather than estimating whether a statistically positive conclusion is true.

C. Chance that the null hypothesis itself is true

Rationale: The plan does not assign a probability to the null hypothesis being true. It calculates a rejection probability under the specified alternative.

D. Chance of nonrejection if that alternative is true

Rationale: Nonrejection under a true alternative describes a type II error probability. Power instead concerns rejection under that alternative.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Power is the probability that the analysis rejects the null if the specified alternative is true, under the planned design assumptions. It is a statement about a procedure operating in that situation. Remember the direction of conditioning: it does not give the probability that a positive study conclusion or the alternative itself is true.

**Short explanation fallback:** Planned power is the chance of rejecting the null under the specified true alternative and design assumptions. It is not the truth probability of a positive result.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.errors-power`, `claim.pancreatic-adenocarcinoma.body-tail-distal-pancreatectomy`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/pancreatic/hp/pancreatic-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nci-pdq.pancreatic-cancer-treatment.2025</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda35.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.errors-power.2026</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7505812/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.pancreatic-cancer-multidisciplinary-consensus.2020</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.errors-power.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Basal cell carcinoma

**Complaint:** basal cell carcinoma study

**Investigational premise:** Topical wound or skin agent (drug).

**Person and setting:** Taylor Hayes; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Taylor Hayes, the skin-oncology journal-club lead, reviews a new topical agent for basal cell carcinoma. The trial evaluates recurrence within two years and does not reject its null model. After seeing the results, authors calculate low power and conclude the agent has no effect. The team is reviewing that conclusion alongside the reported effect estimate and uncertainty.

**Exhibit — Recurrence trial — illustrative analysis:** An unnamed investigational basal-cell-carcinoma topical agent study of two-year recurrence. The trial does not reject its recurrence null model. Authors calculate low post-hoc power and claim that no recurrence effect exists. These study conditions are invented for practice, not real efficacy findings.

**Question:** Which statement is justified when the constructed basal-cell-carcinoma recurrence trial fails to reject its null and reports low post-hoc power?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Post-hoc power does not prove no effect **(key)**

Rationale: A low post-hoc calculation does not establish that the effect is exactly absent. Power is conditional on a specified alternative, rather than a truth probability for the null.

B. Low post-hoc power favors an absent effect

Rationale: The later calculation does not make the no-effect conclusion valid. Neither low post-hoc power nor nonrejection supplies proof of an absent effect.

C. Post-hoc power gives the probability of the null

Rationale: Power does not assign a probability to the null hypothesis. It concerns rejection behavior under a specified alternative.

D. The observed p-value is the study's achieved power

Rationale: A p-value concerns observed-or-more-extreme data under a null model; power concerns rejection under an alternative. They are different conditional quantities.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Power describes rejection probability under a specified alternative, not the probability that a hypothesis is true. A low post-hoc power calculation after nonrejection does not establish an exactly absent effect. Remember that a second conditional calculation cannot transform the first result into proof of equality or supply the effect size missing from this exercise.

**Short explanation fallback:** Low post-hoc power does not prove no effect. It neither gives the probability of the null nor makes the observed p-value equal to power.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.errors-power`, `claim.bcc.pattern`, `claim.bcc.local-invasion`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.p-value`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/skin/hp/skin-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bcc.nci-pdq-current</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11626229/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bcc.s2k-2024</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/eda/section3/eda35.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.errors-power.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.itl.nist.gov/div898/handbook/prc/section1/prc131.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.nist.p-values.2026</a>.

## Act and iterate after a PDSA test

Concept: `concept.quality-improvement.pdsa-act-and-iterate`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.quality-improvement.pdsa-act-and-iterate.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Biopsy-confirmed celiac disease

**Complaint:** celiac disease study

**Investigational premise:** Dietitian care pathway for celiac disease (care pathway).

**Person and setting:** Dana Rivera; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Dana Rivera, the GI improvement lead, reviews an authorized pilot of a new dietitian care pathway for celiac disease. Its symptom aim is met without worsening workload. The project director has booked the national rollout speech; the pilot team has seven minutes to discuss whether a national rollout is warranted. The results describe only the tested local setting.

**Exhibit — Investigational care pathway — illustrative local cycle:** An authorized local pilot of an unnamed investigational celiac dietitian pathway meets its symptom-improvement aim without worsening care-planning workload. Findings apply to the tested setting. These observations are invented for practice, not real pathway effectiveness.

**Question:** Which Act-step decision fits the authorized celiac pathway pilot when its symptom aim is met and the balancing measure does not worsen?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Adopt locally and plan the next test **(key)**

Rationale: The test met its aim without worsening balance in the tested setting. Local adoption uses that learning while a next linked test investigates the next step.

B. Spread the change and monitor other clinics

Rationale: The results apply to the local test. Spreading immediately to other clinics goes beyond that scope instead of testing the next setting.

C. Repeat the same pilot before making any change

Rationale: The supplied favorable aim and balancing findings support local action. Repeating the identical pilot without acting does not use the stated learning.

D. Adapt the pathway before another local test

Rationale: The findings identify no feature requiring revision before local adoption. Adaptation needs a reason in the learning, rather than a change for its own sake.

**Visible teachingPoint:** The Act step turns learning into a decision and a next test. This pathway version met its aim without worsening the balancing measure, supporting local adoption and a linked next cycle. Keep the scope of action tied to the scope of the evidence: a successful small test does not automatically establish performance at every clinic.

**Short explanation fallback:** Adopt the successful pathway locally and plan the next linked test. The favorable aim and balancing findings apply to the tested setting, not an untested wider rollout.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.pdsa-act`, `claim.celiac.adult-biopsy`, `claim.celiac.gluten-free-treatment`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.qi-measures`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://gi.org/topics/celiac-disease/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.acg.celiac-patient-education.2026</a>; <a href="https://digital.ahrq.gov/health-it-tools-and-resources/evaluation-resources/workflow-assessment-health-it-toolkit/all-workflow-tools/plan-do-check-act-cycle" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.pdca.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/vtguide/guide6.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.qi-measures.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/celiac-disease/diagnosis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.celiac-diagnosis.2020</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/clinical-tools-patient-management/digestive-diseases/celiac-disease-health-care-professionals?dkrd=www2.niddk.nih.gov" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.celiac-tests.2021</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/celiac-disease/treatment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.celiac-treatment.2020</a>.

### `question.quality-improvement.pdsa-act-and-iterate.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Venous leg ulcer

**Complaint:** venous leg ulcer study

**Investigational premise:** Compression device (device).

**Person and setting:** Alex Morgan; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Alex Morgan, the wound-service improvement lead, reviews an authorized new compression-device pilot for venous leg ulcers after adequate arterial assessment. Healing is faster, but application rework doubles. The manager calls the pilot paperless; staff print one online form to enter it into another. The team has the healing and workload findings available while planning the next local cycle.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational venous-ulcer compression device pilot: invented wound closure and application rework findings for practice. Results belong only to this authorized local test, not real intervention efficacy.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Wound-closure time | Shorter |
| Application rework | Doubled |

**Question:** Which Act-step decision fits the authorized venous-ulcer device pilot when healing is faster but application rework doubles?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Adapt device use and retest locally **(key)**

Rationale: Healing improved but rework doubled. Revise device use to address that burden and use the next test to study the adapted version.

B. Adopt device use and monitor application rework

Rationale: Adopting this version unchanged accepts the documented worsening of rework. The balancing finding calls for adaptation before adoption.

C. Abandon device use and test another local idea

Rationale: The target improvement supports retaining and adapting the idea. The supplied findings do not require discarding it instead of addressing the added burden.

D. Repeat device use without revising the process

Rationale: Repeating the same device unchanged does not respond to the increased rework already observed. The next cycle should use that learning.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Improvement needs both the intended result and a check for shifted burden. This device shortened wound-closure time but doubled rework, so adapt its use and retest rather than adopting the current version unchanged. The Act step can preserve a promising idea while changing the part that caused trouble. Learning should change the next cycle.

**Short explanation fallback:** Adapt device use to address increased rework, then retest. The improved healing target supports keeping the idea, while the worsened balancing measure argues against adopting this version unchanged.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.pdsa-act`, `claim.venous-leg-ulcer.arterial-assessment-before-compression`, `claim.venous-leg-ulcer.compression-after-adequate-arterial-assessment`, `claim.gs028se.statistics.qi-measures`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://digital.ahrq.gov/health-it-tools-and-resources/evaluation-resources/workflow-assessment-health-it-toolkit/all-workflow-tools/plan-do-check-act-cycle" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.pdca.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/vtguide/guide6.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.qi-measures.2026</a>; <a href="https://esvs.org/guideline/chronic-venous-disease-of-lower-limbs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.vlu.esvs-cvd.2022</a>.

### `question.quality-improvement.pdsa-act-and-iterate.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Gastroparesis after exclusion of mechanical obstruction

**Complaint:** gastroparesis study

**Investigational premise:** Nutrition care pathway for gastroparesis (care pathway).

**Person and setting:** Riley Chen; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Riley Chen, the foregut improvement lead, reviews an authorized new nutrition-pathway pilot for gastroparesis after obstruction was excluded. Its symptom aim is not met and planning takes longer. The vendor insists the pathway saves time after staff complete extra training on saving time. No specific correctable defect has emerged from review, and the next cycle has not been planned.

**Exhibit:** Unnamed investigational gastroparesis nutrition pathway pilot: invented symptom relief and care-planning time findings for practice. Results belong only to this authorized local test, not real intervention efficacy.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom-relief aim | Not met |
| Care-planning time | Longer |

**Question:** Which Act-step decision fits the authorized gastroparesis pathway pilot when its symptom aim is not met and care planning takes longer, with no specific correctable defect identified?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Abandon this version and test another idea **(key)**

Rationale: This version missed its stated aim and worsened care-planning time. Abandoning the version and testing another idea follows both unfavorable findings.

B. Adapt the pathway labels and repeat this version

Rationale: The observations do not identify a labeling problem to fix. That specific adaptation invents an explanation rather than using the supplied findings.

C. Adopt the pathway and monitor the next cycle

Rationale: The tested version missed the aim and increased care-planning time, so the evidence does not support adopting it while monitoring later.

D. Repeat the same test to confirm the findings

Rationale: The stated findings already support changing direction from this version. An unchanged repeat does not use the unfavorable learning to choose the next idea.

**Visible teachingPoint:** The Act step permits abandoning a tested version when findings are unfavorable. This pathway missed its symptom aim and increased planning time, supporting another idea for the next test. Remember that the verdict concerns this version.
