The observations do not establish that every nutrition pathway fails or identify a specific defect to repair.

**Short explanation fallback:** Abandon this unsuccessful pathway version and test another idea. Its symptom aim was missed and planning time increased, without a specific correctable defect identified in the supplied findings.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.pdsa-act`, `claim.gastroparesis.diagnostic-framework`, `claim.gastroparesis.four-hour-solid-meal-scintigraphy`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35926490/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.acg.gastroparesis-guideline.2022</a>; <a href="https://gi.org/topics/gastroparesis/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.acg.gastroparesis-patient-information.2026</a>; <a href="https://gastro.org/clinical-guidance/clinical-guidance-on-the-management-of-gastroparesis/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.aga.gastroparesis-guideline.2025</a>; <a href="https://digital.ahrq.gov/health-it-tools-and-resources/evaluation-resources/workflow-assessment-health-it-toolkit/all-workflow-tools/plan-do-check-act-cycle" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.pdca.2026</a>.

### `question.quality-improvement.pdsa-act-and-iterate.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Carpal tunnel syndrome

**Complaint:** carpal tunnel syndrome study

**Investigational premise:** Wrist-bracing care pathway (care pathway).

**Person and setting:** Jordan Brooks; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jordan Brooks, the hand-service improvement lead, reviews an authorized new wrist-bracing pathway for carpal tunnel syndrome. The pilot intended to improve hand function, but too many follow-up observations are missing to judge its aim. Colleagues suggest continuing, changing or stopping the pathway. The team has the incomplete records and is preparing its next cycle before deciding about broader use.

**Exhibit — Investigational care pathway — illustrative local cycle:** An authorized local pilot of an unnamed investigational carpal-tunnel wrist-bracing pathway lacks too many hand-function observations to judge its stated aim. These observations are invented for practice, not real pathway effectiveness.

**Question:** Which Act-step decision fits the authorized wrist-bracing pathway pilot when missing hand-function observations prevent judging its stated aim?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Fix data capture and repeat a bounded test **(key)**

Rationale: The missing observations prevent judging the aim. Repair data capture and repeat a bounded test so the next decision rests on interpretable results.

B. Adopt the change and improve later measurement

Rationale: The incomplete records do not establish success. Adopting first and measuring later turns an information gap into an unsupported favorable verdict.

C. Adapt the intervention before collecting more data

Rationale: The data gap does not identify an intervention feature to adapt. Fixing measurement addresses the known problem before choosing a specific revision.

D. Abandon the change and test a different idea

Rationale: Incomplete measurement does not establish that the intervention failed. Abandonment would turn missing evidence into an unsupported unfavorable verdict.

**Visible teachingPoint:** An uninterpretable test needs better measurement before an intervention verdict. Here missing observations prevent judging the aim, so repair data capture and repeat a bounded test. Remember the distinction between evidence of failure and failure to collect evidence; the latter does not establish adoption, adaptation, or abandonment of the intervention itself.

**Short explanation fallback:** Fix data capture and repeat a bounded test. The missing observations prevent judging this intervention; they do not establish either success or failure.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.pdsa-act`, `claim.gs028f.nerve.median-carpal-localization`, `claim.gs028f.nerve.median-wrist-symptoms`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://digital.ahrq.gov/health-it-tools-and-resources/evaluation-resources/workflow-assessment-health-it-toolkit/all-workflow-tools/plan-do-check-act-cycle" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.pdca.2026</a>; <a href="https://medlineplus.gov/genetics/condition/carpal-tunnel-syndrome/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.nlm.carpal-tunnel-genetics.2018</a>.

## Choose process, outcome, and balancing measures

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.qi-measures`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qi-measures.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Persistently high-output ileostomy after stabilization

**Complaint:** high-output ileostomy study

**Investigational premise:** Ileostomy rehydration delivery device (device).

**Person and setting:** Sam Patel; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Sam Patel, the stoma-service improvement lead, reviews a proposed rehydration delivery device for high-output ileostomy after stabilization. The pilot aims to reduce dehydration while tracking use and workload. The committee wants fewer forms; its proposal requires a form for each form removed. The team is preparing the measurement plan before testing the new device in its authorized local pilot.

**Exhibit — Investigational clinical pilot — illustrative measurement plan:** An authorized pilot of an unnamed investigational ileostomy rehydration device for high-output ileostomy aims to improve dehydration episodes. The team is concerned about application workload. This protocol is invented for practice and supplies no real efficacy, diagnostic accuracy or patient-specific prediction.

**Question:** Which set lists a process measure, the intended clinical outcome measure and a balancing measure, in that order, for the ileostomy rehydration device pilot?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Device use as planned; dehydration episodes; application workload **(key)**

Rationale: Device use tracks implementation, dehydration episodes track the clinical aim, and application workload checks an unwanted tradeoff in delivering the intervention.

B. Dehydration episodes; device use as planned; application workload

Rationale: Dehydration episodes measure the clinical outcome, while device use measures implementation. This ordering reverses process and outcome.

C. Device use as planned; application workload; dehydration episodes

Rationale: Application workload checks a tradeoff, while dehydration episodes is the aim; this ordering swaps outcome and balancing measures.

D. Application workload; dehydration episodes; device use as planned

Rationale: Application workload belongs in the balancing position, while device use as planned belongs first; this ordering places both incorrectly.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A process measure asks whether planned care happened; an outcome measure asks whether patients experienced the intended result. A balancing measure checks an unwanted tradeoff. Here device use, dehydration and workload answer those three questions. Remember: delivery, result, tradeoff. More device applications alone would not establish that dehydration improved.

**Short explanation fallback:** Device use tracks implementation, dehydration episodes track the clinical aim, and application workload checks a tradeoff. Counting applications alone does not establish a clinical improvement.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.qi-measures`, `claim.bread-butter.high-output-ileostomy.renal-electrolyte-magnesium-assessment`, `claim.bread-butter.high-output-ileostomy.sodium-glucose-oral-rehydration`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/vtguide/guide6.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.qi-measures.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.bapen.org.uk/pdfs/bifa/bifa-top-tips-series-1-updated.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.bifa-high-output-stoma-2023</a>; <a href="https://www.bapen.org.uk/pdfs/bifa/bifa-top-tips-series-30.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.bifa-oral-rehydration-2025</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qi-measures.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Hidradenitis suppurativa

**Complaint:** hidradenitis suppurativa study

**Investigational premise:** Hidradenitis dressing device (device).

**Person and setting:** Casey Bennett; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Casey Bennett, the skin-service improvement lead, reviews a new dressing device for hidradenitis suppurativa. The pilot aims to shorten closure time for draining skin lesions while tracking device use and application burden. The coordinator promises real-time documentation, provided staff first leave the room to find a computer. The team is preparing the pilot's measures before any results are collected.

**Exhibit — Investigational clinical pilot — illustrative measurement plan:** An authorized pilot of an unnamed investigational hidradenitis dressing device for hidradenitis suppurativa aims to improve time to lesion closure. The team is concerned about application workload. This protocol is invented for practice and supplies no real efficacy, diagnostic accuracy or patient-specific prediction.

**Question:** Which set lists a process measure, the intended clinical outcome measure and a balancing measure, in that order, for the hidradenitis dressing device pilot?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Protocol dressing applications; time to lesion closure; extra application work **(key)**

Rationale: Applications track delivery, lesion-closure time tracks the healing aim, and extra application work checks the unwanted burden of delivering the new dressing.

B. Patients enrolled; protocol dressing applications; extra application work

Rationale: Enrollment and application counts both track study or delivery activity. This set leaves out the clinical healing outcome the pilot intends to improve.

C. Protocol dressing applications; extra application work; time to lesion closure

Rationale: Extra application work checks burden, not the healing aim. This ordering swaps the clinical outcome and balancing positions.

D. Time to lesion closure; extra application work; protocol dressing applications

Rationale: Lesion-closure time is the clinical outcome, while applications track delivery. This ordering moves both outside their requested positions.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A completed application is evidence of delivery, not evidence that a lesion closed sooner. Keep the clinical outcome separate from implementation counts, and check added work alongside both. Remember: doing the intervention and benefiting from it are different measurements. A balanced set can show whether better healing came with an application burden.

**Short explanation fallback:** Track dressing applications as process, lesion-closure time as outcome, and extra application work as balance. Enrollment and delivery counts cannot replace the intended clinical result.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.qi-measures`, `claim.hs.pattern-recognition`, `claim.hs.multimodal-planning`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/vtguide/guide6.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.qi-measures.2026</a>; <a href="https://doi.org/10.1111/ajd.14388" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.hs.australasian-2025</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9131894/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.hs.north-american-2019</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qi-measures.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Primary spontaneous pneumothorax

**Complaint:** spontaneous pneumothorax study

**Investigational premise:** Pleural recurrence-prevention device (device).

**Person and setting:** Jamie Reed; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Jamie Reed, the thoracic improvement lead, reviews a new pleural device for recurrence prevention after spontaneous pneumothorax. The pilot will track recurrence and extra procedure time. The supplier offers every dashboard measure except the one requested; adding it requires the advanced transparency package. The team is preparing the measurement plan before the authorized test begins, with outcomes still unobserved.

**Exhibit — Investigational clinical pilot — illustrative measurement plan:** An authorized pilot of an unnamed investigational pleural recurrence-prevention device for spontaneous pneumothorax aims to improve recurrence at protocol follow-up. The team is concerned about extra procedure time. This protocol is invented for practice and supplies no real efficacy, diagnostic accuracy or patient-specific prediction.

**Question:** Which set lists a process measure, the intended clinical outcome measure and a balancing measure, in that order, for the pleural recurrence-prevention device pilot?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Protocol device use; recurrence at follow-up; extra procedure time **(key)**

Rationale: Device use records implementation, recurrence records the prevention aim, and extra procedure time checks the burden added by the intervention.

B. Protocol device use; completed procedures; extra procedure time

Rationale: Completed procedures count delivery activity rather than recurrence prevention. The intended clinical outcome is missing from this otherwise organized set.

C. Patients enrolled; completed procedures; protocol device use

Rationale: Enrollment, procedure completion and device use all describe participation or delivery. This set omits both the recurrence result and the extra-time tradeoff.

D. Recurrence at follow-up; protocol device use; extra procedure time

Rationale: Recurrence is the outcome and device use is process. This set reverses those first two positions while keeping the time tradeoff last.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Recurrence at follow-up is the clinical result; completing a procedure is a delivery event. Device use therefore belongs among process measures, while extra procedure time checks added burden. Remember to keep the result after treatment distinct from the act of treatment. A busy procedure list cannot by itself establish recurrence prevention.

**Short explanation fallback:** Record protocol device use, recurrence at follow-up and extra procedure time as process, outcome and balance. Completed procedures alone cannot establish that recurrence was prevented.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.qi-measures`, `claim.gs028d.psp.recurrence-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/vtguide/guide6.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.qi-measures.2026</a>; <a href="https://publications.ersnet.org/content/erj/63/5/2300797" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ers-eacts-ests.pneumothorax.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qi-measures.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Lung-cancer screening in eligible adults

**Complaint:** lung-cancer screening study

**Investigational premise:** Lung-cancer screening program (screening program).

**Person and setting:** Taylor Hayes; staff discussion lead. L2+ with a hired eligible clinical host.

**Story:** Taylor Hayes, the lung-screening improvement lead, reviews a new lung-cancer screening program for eligible adults. The pilot aims to reduce missed lung cancers at protocol follow-up and may require extra diagnostic visits. Colleagues propose different combinations of measures for the report. The team is planning what to record before testing the unnamed program; no benefit or screening-test performance is supplied.

**Exhibit — Investigational clinical pilot — illustrative measurement plan:** An authorized pilot of an unnamed investigational lung-cancer screening program for lung-cancer screening aims to improve missed cancers at protocol follow-up. The team is concerned about extra diagnostic visits. This protocol is invented for practice and supplies no real efficacy, diagnostic accuracy or patient-specific prediction.

**Question:** Which set lists a process measure, the intended clinical outcome measure and a balancing measure, in that order, for the lung-cancer screening program pilot?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Completed program screening; missed cancers at follow-up; extra diagnostic visits **(key)**

Rationale: Completed screening tracks delivery, missed cancers track the program’s diagnostic aim, and extra diagnostic visits check the additional assessment burden.

B. Screening invitations sent; screening visits completed; extra diagnostic visits

Rationale: Invitations and completed screening visits both track program activity. This set does not measure missed cancers, the stated clinical aim.

C. Completed program screening; extra diagnostic visits; missed cancers at follow-up

Rationale: Extra diagnostic visits check burden, while missed cancers measure the aim. This ordering swaps the outcome and balancing positions.

D. Missed cancers at follow-up; completed program screening; extra diagnostic visits

Rationale: Missed cancers are the outcome and completed screening is process. This set places those two measures in the reverse order requested.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Screening activity and screening success need different measures. Completed screens track delivery; missed cancers at follow-up track the stated diagnostic aim; extra diagnostic visits check burden. Remember to count both what the program does and what it misses. Invitation or attendance totals alone do not establish that the clinical aim improved.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use completed program screening as process, missed cancers at follow-up as outcome, and extra diagnostic visits as balance. Attendance counts do not substitute for the diagnostic aim.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.statistics.qi-measures`, `claim.gs028d.lung-screening.eligibility`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/vtguide/guide6.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ahrq.qi-measures.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.uspreventiveservicestaskforce.org/uspstf/recommendation/lung-cancer-screening" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.uspstf.lung-screening.2021</a>.

## Use qualified interpretation

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.qualified-interpreter`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qualified-interpreter.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Recurrent symptomatic gallstones

**Complaint:** Preguntas sobre cirugía

**Person and setting:** Marta López; age 43, Female.

**Story:** Marta López, una mujer de 43 años, consulta por cálculos biliares sintomáticos confirmados por ecografía. Quiere hablar de la operación propuesta y prefiere español. El cirujano no puede comunicarse adecuadamente con ella. Su hija menor ofrece traducir; no hay una intérprete cualificada presente. La paciente trae preguntas sobre las opciones antes de decidir si acepta la cirugía.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** Before discussing laparoscopic cholecystectomy for Marta López's symptomatic gallstones, what communication support is needed when patient and surgeon cannot communicate adequately?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Arrange qualified Spanish interpretation **(key)**

Rationale: Qualified Spanish interpretation supports an understandable discussion and the patient's own questions. Neither the child's offer nor the employee's bilingual skill establishes competence to interpret clinical information.

B. Use the child's interpretation for consent

Rationale: The child wants to help, but willingness does not establish the competence needed for this clinical consent discussion. The patient still needs qualified interpretation.

C. Ask a bilingual clinic clerk to interpret

Rationale: Speaking Spanish is not itself evidence of competence to interpret clinical information. The employee's qualification has not been established.

D. Use a translated Spanish consent handout

Rationale: Written information can support the discussion, but cannot provide the qualified two-way exchange needed for the patient's own clinical questions.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Consent needs an understandable conversation, including the patient's own questions. When patient and clinician cannot communicate adequately, arrange qualified interpretation. A child's offer or an employee's conversational bilingual skill does not establish that competence. Make the conversation possible before asking for a decision; written materials can support, but cannot replace, that exchange.

**Short explanation fallback:** Arrange qualified Spanish interpretation so the patient can discuss the proposed cholecystectomy and ask questions. Neither the child's willingness nor the employee's bilingual conversation establishes interpreter competence.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.1`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.3`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.4`, `claim.gallstones.symptomatic-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/professional-training/lepguide/chapter2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ahrq-lep-2020</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/faqs/may-an-lep-person-use-a-family-member-as-an-interpreter/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.hhs-ocr-interpreter-2015</a>; <a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00535-023-02014-6" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.jsge.cholelithiasis-guideline.2023</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/gallstones/treatment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.gallstones-treatment.2017</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qualified-interpreter.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Graves hyperthyroidism

**Complaint:** Opciones para Graves

**Person and setting:** Marisol Reyes; age 68, Female.

**Story:** Marisol Reyes, una mujer de 68 años, tiene enfermedad de Graves y consulta sobre las opciones de tratamiento, incluida la cirugía. Prefiere español. Una aplicación traduce parte de la conversación, pero sus preguntas y respuestas siguen poco claras. La paciente quiere entender las alternativas antes de decidir; el equipo aún no cuenta con una intérprete cualificada.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What communication support is needed for Marisol Reyes's Graves-treatment discussion when the translation application leaves the patient's questions and answers unclear?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Arrange qualified medical interpretation **(key)**

Rationale: The app has left the patient's question about the Graves treatment unresolved. Qualified interpretation is needed to make the clinical exchange understandable before relying on consent.

B. Use the application's translated discussion

Rationale: The application's output is already confusing and has not answered the patient's question. Reusing it does not establish an understandable exchange.

C. Ask a bilingual companion to interpret

Rationale: A companion's language ability does not automatically establish qualified clinical interpretation. The patient's question needs an accurate, understandable exchange.

D. Provide translated written treatment information

Rationale: A translated handout may support information, but it does not by itself resolve the patient's unanswered question through a two-way interpreted conversation.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A translation tool has not solved communication merely because it produces words. Here the patient's clinical question remains unclear, so qualified interpretation is needed for an understandable exchange. Remember the purpose: let the patient understand and ask questions before choosing. A companion or handout does not automatically supply that two-way clinical conversation.

**Short explanation fallback:** The application has left the treatment question unresolved. Arrange qualified interpretation before relying on the consent discussion; translated text alone does not establish that the exchange is understood.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.2`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.1`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.3`, `claim.graves-rai-appropriate-candidate.graves-context`, `claim.graves-rai-appropriate-candidate.preference-sensitive-options`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.thyroid.org/graves-disease/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ata.graves-disease.undated</a>; <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/professional-training/lepguide/chapter2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ahrq-lep-2020</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/faqs/may-an-lep-person-use-a-family-member-as-an-interpreter/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.hhs-ocr-interpreter-2015</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/graves-disease" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.graves-disease.2021</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qualified-interpreter.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Suspected or confirmed cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma

**Complaint:** Preguntas sobre biopsia

**Person and setting:** Elena Torres; age 43, Female.

**Story:** Elena Torres, una mujer de 43 años, consulta sobre una biopsia de una lesión sospechosa de carcinoma escamoso cutáneo. Prefiere español. Su pareja contesta en inglés, pero las preguntas de ella siguen sin entenderse. El equipo no tiene una intérprete cualificada disponible todavía. La paciente intenta preguntar qué se hará antes de dar permiso para la biopsia.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What communication support is needed for Elena Torres's skin-biopsy discussion when a relative answers but the patient's own questions remain unheard?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Arrange qualified Spanish interpretation **(key)**

Rationale: Qualified interpretation lets the patient speak and ask questions directly. The partner's answers cannot replace that participation when accurate independent communication is lacking.

B. Ask the partner to relay the patient's questions

Rationale: The patient specifically wants to ask questions directly. Relying on the partner neither provides that exchange nor establishes qualified interpretation.

C. Ask a bilingual staff member to translate

Rationale: Bilingual ability alone does not establish clinical interpreter competence. Another employee's language skills must not be treated as qualification by assumption.

D. Use a translated form for the patient's questions

Rationale: A translated form cannot conduct the understandable two-way discussion the patient requests. Written support does not substitute for qualified interpretation.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Family participation can support a patient without replacing the patient's voice. When accurate communication is lacking, qualified interpretation lets the patient ask questions and make the decision directly. Remember to address the patient, not simply whoever speaks most. A partner's willingness or another employee's bilingual ability does not establish qualified clinical interpretation.

**Short explanation fallback:** Arrange qualified Spanish interpretation for the patient's own questions. The partner's answers do not replace an understandable direct discussion, and bilingual ability alone does not establish interpreter competence.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.3`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.1`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.4`, `claim.cutaneous-scc.biopsy-for-diagnosis-and-risk`, `claim.cutaneous-scc.risk-directed-surgery`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/guidelines/scc" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.csc.aad-guideline.2018</a>; <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/clinical-care/wisely" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.csc.aad-mohs-wisely.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/professional-training/lepguide/chapter2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ahrq-lep-2020</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/faqs/may-an-lep-person-use-a-family-member-as-an-interpreter/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.hhs-ocr-interpreter-2015</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qualified-interpreter.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Achalasia after exclusion of structural obstruction

**Complaint:** Preguntas sobre manometría

**Person and setting:** Lucía Vega; age 68, Female.

**Story:** Lucía Vega, una mujer de 68 años, consulta por sospecha de acalasia tras excluir una obstrucción estructural. Quiere hablar de la manometría propuesta y prefiere español. La recepcionista conoce algunas frases, pero no está cualificada para interpretar. La paciente todavía no entiende la conversación clínica y trae preguntas antes de decidir si acepta el estudio propuesto.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What communication support is needed before Lucía Vega decides about esophageal manometry when the receptionist's limited Spanish cannot support the clinical discussion?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Arrange qualified medical interpretation **(key)**

Rationale: The receptionist is not qualified to interpret clinical information, and the clinician cannot support the full exchange. Arrange qualified interpretation for the patient's manometry questions and informed choice.

B. Use the receptionist's conversational Spanish

Rationale: Knowing some phrases is not competence to interpret complex clinical information accurately and impartially. The supplied employee cannot fill that role.

C. Use an application to translate the discussion

Rationale: The proposed app does not establish that this clinical consent conversation will be understood. The missing support is qualified interpretation.

D. Use a translated handout with written replies

Rationale: Written exchanges do not establish an understandable clinical consent discussion or replace the qualified interpretation needed for the patient's questions.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Knowing some phrases is different from interpreting clinical information accurately and impartially. This employee is not qualified for the discussion, so arrange qualified interpretation before relying on consent. Remember to assess whether the exchange is understandable, not whether someone can produce a translation. Written exchanges and apps do not establish that missing competence here.

**Short explanation fallback:** Arrange qualified interpretation for the clinical discussion. The receptionist is explicitly not qualified, and the proposed app or written exchanges do not establish the needed understandable conversation.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.4`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.1`, `claim.achalasia.high-resolution-manometry`, `claim.achalasia.manometric-recognition`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/professional-training/lepguide/chapter2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ahrq-lep-2020</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/faqs/may-an-lep-person-use-a-family-member-as-an-interpreter/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.hhs-ocr-interpreter-2015</a>; <a href="https://www.jnmjournal.org/journal/view.html?doi=10.5056/jnm20014" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.seoul-achalasia-2020</a>.

## Make consent understandable and voluntary

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.informed-consent`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.informed-consent.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Recurrent symptomatic gallstones

**Complaint:** Preguntas sobre cirugía

**Person and setting:** Marta López; age 43, Female.

**Reached story:** With qualified Spanish interpretation, Marta López, a 43-year-old woman, has recurrent symptoms with ultrasound-confirmed gallstones and is discussing laparoscopic cholecystectomy. A signed form lists the operation, but material risks, benefits and alternatives have not been discussed. The surgeon calls the form self-explanatory, then asks the nurse to explain its instructions. The patient has questions before agreeing to surgery.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** With qualified interpretation in place, what is needed for informed consent to Marta López's laparoscopic cholecystectomy when material information has not been discussed?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Discuss risks, benefits, and alternatives **(key)**

Rationale: The patient lacks the clinical information needed to choose. Discuss the intervention's relevant risks, benefits, and alternatives, including foregoing it, in an understandable conversation.

B. Confirm the form was signed voluntarily

Rationale: Voluntary signing matters, but confirming it does not supply the omitted risks, benefits, and alternatives. Information and voluntariness are separate needs.

C. Have the patient reread the consent document

Rationale: Rereading the document does not by itself supply the clinical conversation that never occurred. A form can document consent without replacing the discussion.

D. Ask the patient to describe the planned operation

Rationale: Describing the procedure's purpose checks only part of understanding. The stated gaps about relevant risks, benefits, and alternatives remain.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Informed consent helps a person understand the intervention, its relevant risks and benefits, and alternatives including declining, so the person can choose voluntarily. A signature cannot replace that conversation. The patient needs that discussion before the form can be relied on for the operation. Remember: document the conversation, rather than substituting a document for it.

**Short explanation fallback:** The missing step is an understandable discussion of the intervention, risks, benefits, and alternatives. Confirming a signature or willingness alone does not fill those information gaps.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.informed-consent.1`, `claim.gs028se.informed-consent.4`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.1`, `claim.gallstones.symptomatic-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/professional-training/lepguide/chapter2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ahrq-lep-2020</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/faqs/may-an-lep-person-use-a-family-member-as-an-interpreter/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.hhs-ocr-interpreter-2015</a>; <a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00535-023-02014-6" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.jsge.cholelithiasis-guideline.2023</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/gallstones/treatment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.gallstones-treatment.2017</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.informed-consent.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Graves hyperthyroidism

**Complaint:** Opciones para Graves

**Person and setting:** Marisol Reyes; age 68, Female.

**Reached story:** With qualified Spanish interpretation, Marisol Reyes, a 68-year-old woman, has Graves disease and is considering surgery after discussing treatment goals. The patient can describe the proposed operation but has not heard the alternatives, including foregoing it. The clinic advertises a choice of appointments; both booking buttons lead to the same unavailable slot. The patient asks what other options exist.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** With qualified interpretation in place, what information is missing before Marisol Reyes can make an informed choice about surgery for Graves disease?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Discuss alternatives, including no procedure **(key)**

Rationale: The patient asks whether declining is an option. Discuss relevant alternatives and the implications of foregoing the operation, tailored to the situation.

B. Review the operation's expected diagnostic benefit

Rationale: Expected diagnostic benefit is relevant, but repeating it leaves the question about declining and other options unanswered.

C. Review the operation's principal procedural risks

Rationale: Procedural risks are relevant, but reviewing them alone still omits the alternatives and implications of no procedure that the patient is asking about.

D. Ask the patient to choose a surgical appointment

Rationale: Choosing an appointment assumes the procedure decision is made. The patient still needs information about alternatives before making that choice.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A decision is informed only if the patient can consider relevant alternatives, including foregoing the proposed intervention and what that would mean. Reviewing the procedure's benefits or risks alone leaves that question unanswered. Remember to explain the choice, not only the offered procedure, using an understandable conversation tailored to the patient's situation.

**Short explanation fallback:** Discuss relevant alternatives, including no procedure and its implications. Repeating operation benefits or risks does not answer the patient's specific question about declining.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.informed-consent.2`, `claim.gs028se.informed-consent.1`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.2`, `claim.graves-rai-appropriate-candidate.graves-context`, `claim.graves-rai-appropriate-candidate.preference-sensitive-options`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.thyroid.org/graves-disease/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ata.graves-disease.undated</a>; <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/professional-training/lepguide/chapter2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ahrq-lep-2020</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/faqs/may-an-lep-person-use-a-family-member-as-an-interpreter/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.hhs-ocr-interpreter-2015</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/graves-disease" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.graves-disease.2021</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.informed-consent.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Suspected or confirmed cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma

**Complaint:** Preguntas sobre biopsia

**Person and setting:** Elena Torres; age 43, Female.

**Reached story:** With qualified Spanish interpretation, Elena Torres, a 43-year-old woman, is discussing biopsy of a lesion suspicious for cutaneous SCC. After hearing the explanation, the patient says the procedure guarantees no scar. The presenter promises questions are welcome, then announces that questions ended on the previous slide. The biopsy has not started, and there is time to revisit the discussion.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** With qualified interpretation in place, what should the team do when Elena Torres mistakenly expects the skin biopsy to guarantee no scar?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Clarify scarring and ask for teach-back **(key)**

Rationale: The patient has demonstrated a specific misunderstanding about scarring. Clarify it, then ask the patient to explain the plan in their own words before relying on consent.

B. Repeat the explanation of the biopsy technique

Rationale: Repeating the biopsy technique does not necessarily address the supposed guarantee about scarring or check that the misunderstanding has been corrected.

C. Ask the patient to confirm willingness to proceed

Rationale: Willingness to proceed is different from understanding the expected result. Confirming willingness leaves the demonstrated scarring misconception unresolved.

D. Review the signed consent form with the patient

Rationale: Reviewing the form alone does not establish that the specific mistaken expectation has changed. Clarification and a check of understanding are needed.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A nod or signature does not settle a demonstrated misunderstanding. Clarify the mistaken expectation, then ask the patient to explain the plan in their own words to check understanding. Here the concern is a supposed guarantee about scarring. Remember: check what the patient understood, rather than assuming that hearing an explanation means understanding it.

**Short explanation fallback:** Clarify the mistaken scarring guarantee and ask for teach-back before relying on consent. Willingness or another reading of the form does not by itself establish corrected understanding.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.informed-consent.3`, `claim.gs028se.informed-consent.1`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.3`, `claim.cutaneous-scc.biopsy-for-diagnosis-and-risk`, `claim.cutaneous-scc.risk-directed-surgery`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/guidelines/scc" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.csc.aad-guideline.2018</a>; <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/clinical-care/wisely" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.csc.aad-mohs-wisely.2026</a>; <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/professional-training/lepguide/chapter2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ahrq-lep-2020</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/faqs/may-an-lep-person-use-a-family-member-as-an-interpreter/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.hhs-ocr-interpreter-2015</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.informed-consent.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Achalasia after exclusion of structural obstruction

**Complaint:** Preguntas sobre manometría

**Person and setting:** Lucía Vega; age 68, Female.

**Reached story:** With qualified Spanish interpretation, Lucía Vega, a 68-year-old woman, is discussing nonurgent manometry for suspected achalasia after structural obstruction was excluded. Information has been provided, but a companion threatens to withhold transportation unless the patient signs. In private, the patient says the study is unwanted. Testing has not started, and there is time to discuss the patient's own choice.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** With qualified interpretation in place, how should the team respond when Lucía Vega privately declines nonurgent manometry after a companion threatens to withhold transportation unless the patient signs?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Pause and support the patient's voluntary choice **(key)**

Rationale: The patient privately says the procedure is unwanted after a threat about transport. Pause the elective process and support a voluntary choice rather than rely on the pressured signature.

B. Ask the patient to confirm consent with the companion

Rationale: Asking for confirmation in the companion's presence preserves the setting of pressure. It does not protect the voluntary choice expressed in private.

C. Offer another appointment with the same companion

Rationale: A later appointment with the same companion does not itself address the pressure undermining consent. Changing the date is not a voluntariness assessment.

D. Ask the companion to explain the patient's preferences

Rationale: The companion's account cannot replace the patient's private expressed preference. Practical support does not give the companion authority to impose the elective choice.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Consent requires a voluntary choice as well as information and understanding. A signature obtained under the pressure described here cannot substitute for the patient's expressed preference. Pause the nonurgent process and support a voluntary decision in private. Remember that a companion's wishes do not become the patient's wishes merely because the companion provides practical support.

**Short explanation fallback:** Pause and support the patient's voluntary choice. The private refusal and companion's threat undermine reliance on a pressured signature for an nonurgent study with no urgent need.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.informed-consent.4`, `claim.gs028se.qualified-interpreter.1`, `claim.achalasia.high-resolution-manometry`, `claim.achalasia.manometric-recognition`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/professional-training/lepguide/chapter2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ahrq-lep-2020</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/civil-rights/for-individuals/faqs/may-an-lep-person-use-a-family-member-as-an-interpreter/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.hhs-ocr-interpreter-2015</a>; <a href="https://www.jnmjournal.org/journal/view.html?doi=10.5056/jnm20014" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.seoul-achalasia-2020</a>.

## Respect capable refusal

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.capacity-refusal`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.capacity-refusal.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Uncomplicated adult appendicitis with appendicolith

**Complaint:** Declining appendectomy

**Person and setting:** Grace Patel; age 32, Female.

**Story:** Grace Patel, a 32-year-old woman, has acute appendicitis with an appendicolith and is discussing appendectomy in hospital. The patient accurately explains the recommendation, alternatives and consequences of declining, gives coherent personal reasons and consistently refuses. No immediate emergency prevents discussion. The surgeon favors surgery and asks whether disagreement means somebody else should make the decision.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team respond to Grace Patel's informed refusal of appendectomy when the stated decision-making abilities are intact?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Respect refusal and discuss follow-up **(key)**

Rationale: The patient demonstrates informed understanding, coherent reasoning, and a consistent refusal. Respect that choice and discuss follow-up; surgeon disagreement does not establish incapacity.

B. Request psychiatric clearance before accepting refusal

Rationale: The supplied concern is disagreement, not inability to decide. That alone does not establish a need for psychiatric clearance before accepting an informed refusal.

C. Ask the family to confirm the treatment decision

Rationale: The patient's demonstrated abilities support making this decision. Family agreement is not what validates this capable adult's informed refusal.

D. Repeat consent counseling until surgery is accepted

Rationale: Further counseling can address an unanswered question, but repeating it until agreement replaces the informed choice already demonstrated in this scenario.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Capacity concerns the decision in front of you and the patient's demonstrated abilities, not agreement with the clinician. This patient understands the options and personal implications, reasons coherently, and communicates a consistent refusal. Respect that informed choice and discuss follow-up. Remember: a recommendation can be reasonable without making disagreement evidence of incapacity.

**Short explanation fallback:** Respect the informed refusal and discuss follow-up. The patient demonstrates the required abilities; the surgeon's disagreement alone does not justify replacing the decision or requiring psychiatric or family approval.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.capacity-refusal.1`, `claim.gs028se.capacity-refusal.3`, `claim.gs028c.appendicitis.appendicolith-operative-pathway`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/decisions-adult-patients-who-lack-capacity" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-capacity-2-1-2</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.pcori.org/evidence-updates/comparing-surgery-versus-antibiotics-treating-adults-uncomplicated-appendicitis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.pcori.coda-appendicitis.2024</a>; <a href="https://www.sages.org/publications/guidelines/guideline-for-the-diagnosis-and-treatment-of-appendicitis/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sages.appendicitis.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.capacity-refusal.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Primary hyperparathyroidism with renal involvement

**Complaint:** Confusion before consent

**Person and setting:** Adrian Chen; age 74, Male.

**Story:** Adrian Chen, a 74-year-old man, has confirmed primary hyperparathyroidism with kidney stones and is attending elective parathyroid surgical assessment. Despite signing earlier, the patient is newly confused, cannot explain the operation's purpose after discussion and gives rapidly changing answers. Surgery has not started. A colleague asks whether the earlier signature can authorize the proposed operation.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should the team do before elective parathyroid surgery when Adrian Chen is newly confused and cannot understand the operation despite an earlier consent signature?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Pause and assess capacity for this decision **(key)**

Rationale: New confusion, inability to explain the purpose, and changing answers raise present capacity concerns. Pause and assess this decision before the elective intervention.

B. Verify the earlier consent and continue preparation

Rationale: The earlier signature records an earlier discussion. It does not resolve the patient's new inability to understand the current decision.

C. Obtain a fresh signature after repeating the explanation

Rationale: A fresh signature after another repetition does not establish understanding. The patient's present abilities need assessment rather than replacement documentation.

D. Ask a relative to approve the procedure first

Rationale: A relative's approval first bypasses assessment of the patient's current decision-making abilities. Address the present concern before assuming someone else should decide.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Capacity can change, so an earlier signature does not answer a present concern about understanding. New confusion and inability to explain this procedure warrant pausing and assessing the current decision. Remember to assess the person now for this choice. A fresh signature or a relative's approval does not replace addressing that concern.

**Short explanation fallback:** Pause the elective process and assess capacity for the current decision. The earlier form does not resolve new confusion and inability to explain the procedure's purpose.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.capacity-refusal.2`, `claim.primary-hyperparathyroidism.biochemical-pattern`, `claim.primary-hyperparathyroidism.renal-bone-surgery-assessment`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/decisions-adult-patients-who-lack-capacity" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-capacity-2-1-2</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://endocrinediseases.org/parathyroid/hyperparathyroidism-treatment/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.aaes-phpt-treatment</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/primary-hyperparathyroidism" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.niddk-primary-hyperparathyroidism</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.capacity-refusal.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Suspicious thyroid nodule meeting biopsy criteria

**Complaint:** Declining thyroid sampling

**Person and setting:** Nora Hayes; age 45, Female.

**Story:** Nora Hayes, a 45-year-old woman, has a suspicious thyroid nodule meeting biopsy criteria and is discussing needle sampling. The chart records treated depression. A colleague questions accepting refusal because of that diagnosis. The patient accurately explains the options and personal consequences, reasons coherently and communicates a stable choice against sampling. The procedure has not started.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team assess Nora Hayes's refusal of thyroid needle sampling when treated depression is recorded but the stated decision-making abilities are intact?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Assess current decision-making abilities **(key)**

Rationale: Assess the demonstrated abilities for this specific decision. Accurate understanding, personal appreciation, coherent reasoning, and a stable choice cannot be dismissed because depression is recorded.

B. Require psychiatric clearance because of depression

Rationale: Depression alone does not establish incapacity or a requirement for psychiatric clearance. The actual abilities demonstrated in this discussion matter.

C. Use the family's account of the patient's usual judgment

Rationale: Family observations can provide context, but a description of usual judgment does not replace assessing the patient's present abilities for this decision.

D. Judge capacity by agreement with the recommendation

Rationale: Agreement with the clinician is not a capacity criterion. A reasoned refusal can demonstrate the required abilities even when the clinician favors revision.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A diagnosis is not a capacity verdict. Assess understanding, personal appreciation, reasoning, and communication for the actual decision. The described abilities support respecting this refusal despite the history of depression. Remember to examine what the patient can do in this discussion, rather than replacing an individual assessment with a diagnostic label or agreement test.

**Short explanation fallback:** Judge current abilities for this decision, not depression alone. The patient demonstrates understanding, appreciation, coherent reasoning, and a stable choice, supporting respect for the refusal.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.capacity-refusal.3`, `claim.gs028se.capacity-refusal.1`, `claim.thyroid-nodule.fna-after-selected-ultrasound`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/decisions-adult-patients-who-lack-capacity" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-capacity-2-1-2</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://www.thyroid.org/thyroid-nodules/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.ata-thyroid-nodules</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.capacity-refusal.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Symptomatic axial superficial venous reflux

**Complaint:** Supported treatment decision

**Person and setting:** Julian Torres; age 68, Male.

**Story:** Julian Torres, a 68-year-old man, has symptomatic varicose veins with documented axial reflux and is discussing an intervention. Mild cognitive impairment is recorded. With plain-language support, the patient describes the purpose, relevant risks and alternatives, applies them personally and consistently chooses treatment. A colleague asks whether the diagnosis requires a surrogate's signature before planning proceeds.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team approach Julian Torres's decision about a varicose-vein intervention when mild cognitive impairment is recorded but supported understanding is demonstrated?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Support and assess the patient's own decision **(key)**

Rationale: Support understanding and assess the patient's own abilities for vein intervention. The described informed, consistent choice should not be displaced solely by the cognitive diagnosis.

B. Obtain a surrogate's decision because of the diagnosis

Rationale: Cognitive impairment does not eliminate capacity for every decision. Seeking a surrogate solely from that label skips the abilities demonstrated after support.

C. Require a family countersignature for this consent

Rationale: A family countersignature does not replace assessing the patient's supported understanding and choice. The diagnosis alone does not establish that another person must validate consent.

D. Require formal testing before discussing the choice

Rationale: The supplied discussion already demonstrates relevant abilities with support. A blanket testing requirement based on the diagnosis does not address those actual abilities.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Cognitive impairment does not remove capacity for every decision. Support understanding and assess the patient's actual abilities for this choice before seeking a substitute decision-maker. Plain language enables an informed, consistent choice. Remember that the diagnosis and the decision are separate questions; a label alone does not transfer authority to family or a surrogate.

**Short explanation fallback:** Support and assess the patient's own decision about treatment. Demonstrated understanding after plain-language explanation cannot be dismissed solely because cognitive impairment is recorded.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.capacity-refusal.4`, `claim.gs028se.capacity-refusal.3`, `claim.varicose.intervention-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/decisions-adult-patients-who-lack-capacity" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-capacity-2-1-2</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11523430/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.varicose.svs-avf-avls-2023</a>.

## Guide surrogates using patient values

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.surrogate-decisions`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.surrogate-decisions.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Acute calculous cholecystitis in a fit adult

**Complaint:** Operative decision

**Person and setting:** Maya Reed; age 71, Female.

**Story:** Maya Reed, a 71-year-old woman, is hospitalized with acute calculous cholecystitis and lacks capacity for the proposed operative decision. An authorized surrogate recalls specific conversations rejecting surgery in these circumstances but personally favors it. The surrogate asks whose preferences should guide the choice. The team has those prior wishes available and has not agreed on a plan.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should guide the authorized surrogate's operative decision for Maya Reed's acute cholecystitis when known patient wishes differ from the surrogate's own preference?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Use the patient's known preferences **(key)**

Rationale: Specific prior discussions identify what this patient would choose in these circumstances. Substituted judgment uses those known wishes rather than the surrogate's own preference.

B. Use the surrogate's preferred treatment plan

Rationale: The surrogate represents the patient's known perspective. Personally favoring treatment does not justify replacing the patient's specific contrary wishes.

C. Use the family's agreed treatment preference

Rationale: Family agreement does not displace a known patient preference. Counting relatives' choices answers whose plan is popular, rather than what this patient would choose.

D. Use the option with the greatest expected benefit

Rationale: Best-interest deliberation is the fallback when wishes cannot be determined. Here known preferences supply the patient's perspective rather than leaving it unknown.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Substituted judgment asks what this patient would choose, using known preferences and values. It does not ask what the surrogate would choose for themselves or what most relatives prefer. Here specific prior wishes are available. Remember that the surrogate represents the patient's perspective; best-interest deliberation is the fallback when that perspective cannot be determined.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use the patient's known preferences through substituted judgment. The surrogate's own preference does not replace specific prior wishes about these circumstances.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.surrogate-decisions.1`, `claim.gs028se.surrogate-decisions.2`, `claim.gs028c.cholecystitis.index-admission-cholecystectomy`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/decisions-adult-patients-who-lack-capacity" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-capacity-2-1-2</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7643471/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.wses.cholecystitis.2020</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.surrogate-decisions.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** External full-thickness rectal prolapse

**Complaint:** Prolapse surgery deliberation

**Person and setting:** Owen Reed; age 35, Male.

**Story:** Owen Reed, a 35-year-old man, has full-thickness rectal prolapse and lacks capacity for the proposed surgical decision. An authorized surrogate is present. Reasonable efforts have not identified prior wishes or relevant values. The team can discuss this patient's likely benefits and burdens. The surrogate asks how to approach the choice without knowing what the patient would have wanted.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should guide the authorized surrogate's rectal-prolapse surgical deliberation for Owen Reed when prior wishes and relevant values cannot be determined?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. The patient's benefits and burdens **(key)**

Rationale: The patient's wishes and values remain unknown after reasonable efforts. Best-interest deliberation therefore weighs likely benefits, burdens, and suffering for the patient.

B. The choice most family members would make

Rationale: A family majority does not establish the patient's best interests. The supplied standard requires attention to the patient's benefits and burdens.

C. The option with the longest possible survival

Rationale: Possible survival is one consideration, not a complete substitute for weighing benefits, burdens, and suffering. The standard is broader than maximizing duration alone.

D. The surrogate's preferred treatment for themself

Rationale: The surrogate's own treatment preference is not the patient's known preference or a complete assessment of the patient's welfare.

**Visible teachingPoint:** When the patient's preferences and values cannot be determined, consider the patient's best interests, including likely benefits, burdens, and suffering. This differs from substituting the surrogate's personal choice or counting family votes. Remember to look for the patient's perspective first; when it is unavailable, keep the patient's welfare at the center of deliberation.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use the patient's best interests, weighing benefits and burdens. No prior preferences can be identified, so neither family voting nor the surrogate's personal treatment preference supplies the standard.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.surrogate-decisions.2`, `claim.gs028se.surrogate-decisions.1`, `claim.brief.rectal-prolapse.surgical-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ascrsu.com/ascrs/repview?name=2_2851051_PDF&amp;type=784-162" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.ascrs-rectal-prolapse-2017</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/decisions-adult-patients-who-lack-capacity" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-capacity-2-1-2</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.surrogate-decisions.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Recurrent nephrolithiasis

**Complaint:** Unresolved investigation decision

**Person and setting:** Claire Bennett; age 56, Female.

**Story:** Claire Bennett, a 56-year-old woman, has recurrent kidney stones and lacks capacity for a proposed metabolic evaluation. Documented wishes oppose that evaluation, but the authorized surrogate requests it. Further conversation has not resolved the disagreement. No immediate emergency prevents additional discussion. The team and surrogate are asking what should happen before the investigation is arranged.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What is the appropriate next step when the surrogate's requested kidney-stone evaluation conflicts with Claire Bennett's documented wishes and discussion has not resolved the disagreement?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Seek ethics support for the conflict **(key)**

Rationale: The request still conflicts with documented wishes despite discussion. Appropriate institutional ethics support addresses that unresolved conflict instead of accepting the surrogate's request automatically.

B. Ask another relative to approve the requested treatment

Rationale: Another relative's agreement does not resolve the conflict with the patient's documented wishes. Changing who endorses the plan leaves the same ethical concern.

C. Use the surrogate's current request as the final decision

Rationale: An authorized surrogate's request is not automatic permission to disregard known patient wishes. The unresolved disagreement calls for appropriate support.

D. Repeat the same family meeting before seeking advice

Rationale: The supplied discussion has already failed to resolve the conflict. Repeating it unchanged is not a substitute for seeking the appropriate institutional ethics support.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A surrogate request does not automatically override known patient wishes. When discussion leaves that conflict unresolved, appropriate institutional ethics support can help address it rather than accepting the request by default. Remember to preserve the patient's perspective during disagreement. Substituting a different relative or repeating an unchanged meeting does not itself resolve the ethical conflict.

**Short explanation fallback:** Seek appropriate institutional ethics support for the unresolved conflict. The surrogate's request remains inconsistent with documented wishes, and there is time to address it without automatic compliance.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.surrogate-decisions.3`, `claim.gs028se.surrogate-decisions.1`, `claim.nephrolithiasis.recurrent-metabolic`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/decisions-adult-patients-who-lack-capacity" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-capacity-2-1-2</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/urologic-diseases/kidney-stones/diagnosis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.kidney-stones-diagnosis.2017</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/urologic-diseases/kidney-stones/treatment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.kidney-stones-treatment.2017</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.surrogate-decisions.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Persistent unexplained adult neck mass

**Complaint:** Participation in sampling

**Person and setting:** Noah Walker; age 51, Male.

**Story:** Noah Walker, a 51-year-old man, has a persistent neck mass under malignancy evaluation and needs an authorized surrogate for the complex needle-sampling decision. The patient can express comfort concerns and simple preferences, and wants to remain in the discussion. The surrogate asks whether the patient should leave while the proposed sampling plan is considered by the team.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should Noah Walker participate in the neck-mass sampling discussion when a surrogate must make the complex decision but the patient can express concerns and preferences?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Include the patient as abilities allow **(key)**

Rationale: The patient retains abilities to express concerns and preferences. Include that participation while the authorized surrogate handles the complex choice requiring substitute authority.

B. Ask the surrogate to relay the patient's concerns

Rationale: Relying only on the surrogate to relay concerns omits the direct participation the patient can still provide. Needing a surrogate does not require that exclusion.

C. Limit discussion to the surrogate's treatment choice

Rationale: Limiting discussion to the surrogate's choice ignores the patient's remaining abilities and expressed comfort concerns. Those views still deserve attention.

D. Use the patient's simple preference as the final decision

Rationale: A simple preference can inform deliberation without establishing capacity for the complex decision. Participation should not be mistaken for complete final authority.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Needing a surrogate for one complex choice does not erase every ability to participate. Include the patient to the extent possible and attend to expressed comfort concerns and preferences, while the surrogate handles the complex decision. Remember that participation and final decision authority are separate; neither exclusion nor substitution of a simple preference fits here.

**Short explanation fallback:** Include the patient as abilities allow, while the surrogate makes the complex decision. Remaining comfort concerns and simple preferences still deserve direct attention.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.surrogate-decisions.4`, `claim.gs028f.neck.adult-lateral-malignancy-evaluation`, `claim.gs028f.neck.needle-before-open`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/10193598/1/head-and-neck-cancer-united-kingdom-national-multidisciplinary-guidelines-sixth-edition.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.uk.head-neck-guidelines.2024</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/decisions-adult-patients-who-lack-capacity" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-capacity-2-1-2</a>.

## Reconsider perioperative resuscitation plans

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.perioperative-dnr`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.perioperative-dnr.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Symptomatic reducible inguinal hernia

**Complaint:** DNR before repair

**Person and setting:** Grace Patel; age 61, Female.

**Story:** Grace Patel, a 61-year-old woman, is planning elective repair of a symptomatic reducible inguinal hernia and has an existing DNR order. The preoperative checklist proposes automatic suspension during every operation. The patient asks how the order will apply while under anesthesia. No individualized perioperative discussion has occurred, and the team is preparing the operative plan.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team address Grace Patel's existing DNR order before elective inguinal-hernia repair when the checklist proposes automatic suspension?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Discuss and document the patient's preferences **(key)**

Rationale: Individualized discussion with the patient or surrogate and responsible clinicians establishes and documents preferences for the operative context. The checklist cannot make that decision automatically.

B. Use the operating room's usual resuscitation plan

Rationale: A standard operating-room plan does not substitute for reviewing this patient's preferences. It bypasses the individualized discussion required here.

C. Carry the existing order forward without reconsideration

Rationale: Leaving the order unchanged without reconsideration skips review of how it applies to the proposed operation and anesthesia. Individualization is needed rather than an automatic default.

D. Suspend the order while obtaining routine surgical consent

Rationale: Suspending first and obtaining routine consent does not replace an individualized resuscitation discussion. An automatic suspension bypasses the patient's preferences.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Perioperative DNR planning requires an individualized review with the patient or surrogate and responsible clinicians. Automatically suspending the order bypasses that discussion; carrying it forward without reconsideration also leaves the operative context unexplored. Remember to discuss and document the patient's preferences rather than treating entry to the operating room as the decision itself.

**Short explanation fallback:** Discuss and document an individualized resuscitation plan with the patient or surrogate and responsible clinicians. An automatic operating-room rule does not replace that review.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.perioperative-dnr.1`, `claim.inguinal-hernia.symptomatic-elective-repair`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/orders-not-attempt-resuscitation-dnar" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-dnar-5-4</a>; <a href="https://www.asahq.org/standards-and-practice-parameters/statement-on-ethical-guidelines-for-the-anesthesia-care-of-patients-with-do-not-resuscitate-orders" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.asa-dnar-2023</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5809582/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.herniasurge-2018</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/inguinal-hernia" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.niddk-inguinal-hernia</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.perioperative-dnr.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Resectable proximal gastric adenocarcinoma

**Complaint:** Gastrectomy preferences

**Person and setting:** Adrian Chen; age 48, Male.

**Story:** Adrian Chen, a 48-year-old man, has resectable proximal gastric adenocarcinoma and is planning gastrectomy with an existing DNR order. The patient worries that every anesthesia-related intervention would conflict with that order. The anesthesiology team has time to discuss particular interventions and preferences before surgery. The perioperative plan has not yet been documented for this operation.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should the anesthesia team clarify and document with Adrian Chen before gastrectomy when an existing DNR order raises questions about perioperative interventions?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Clarify feasible limits and document the plan **(key)**

Rationale: Clarify requested limits, procedures essential to the proposed anesthesia, and an agreed documented plan. That answers the patient's practical questions before the operation.

B. Carry the current limits into the anesthetic plan

Rationale: Carrying limits forward without discussion does not clarify their application to anesthesia or the interventions essential to the proposed care.

C. Use a procedure-specific standard resuscitation plan

Rationale: A procedure-specific standard is still a generic plan. It does not resolve this patient's requested limits through individualized discussion.

D. Leave decisions to the anesthetist during the operation

Rationale: Waiting for decisions during the operation leaves the supplied questions unresolved beforehand. Clarification and agreement belong in the planned discussion.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Requested limits need discussion in the context of anesthesia. Clarify the interventions, identify what is essential to planned care, and document an agreed individualized plan with the patient or surrogate and responsible clinicians. Remember that neither a generic procedure policy nor an unchanged order answers every practical question about how those limits apply.

**Short explanation fallback:** Clarify feasible limits, essential anesthetic procedures, and an agreed documented plan. Leaving the issue until the operation or using a generic policy does not resolve the patient's questions beforehand.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.perioperative-dnr.2`, `claim.gs028se.perioperative-dnr.1`, `claim.gastric-adenocarcinoma.prophylactic-splenectomy-no-survival-benefit`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/orders-not-attempt-resuscitation-dnar" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-dnar-5-4</a>; <a href="https://www.asahq.org/standards-and-practice-parameters/statement-on-ethical-guidelines-for-the-anesthesia-care-of-patients-with-do-not-resuscitate-orders" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.asa-dnar-2023</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9813208/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.japanese-gastric-cancer-guideline.2021</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27280511/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.jcog0110.prophylactic-splenectomy.2017</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11739648/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.korean-gastric-cancer-guideline.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.perioperative-dnr.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Symptomatic Meckel diverticulum involving adjacent ileum

**Complaint:** DNR reinstatement plan
