**Person and setting:** Nora Hayes; age 69, Female.

**Story:** Nora Hayes, a 69-year-old woman, is planning ileal resection for symptomatic Meckel diverticulum involving adjacent bowel. An existing DNR order has been reviewed, and the patient agreed to specific temporary perioperative changes. The written plan does not say when the original limits resume. The patient asks what happens afterward while the team completes the documentation.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What must the team add to Nora Hayes's ileal-resection plan after agreeing on temporary perioperative changes to an existing DNR order?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. The time for resuming the original limits **(key)**

Rationale: The temporary change needs a documented endpoint for reinstating the original directive. That is the omitted detail identified in this note.

B. The clinician authorizing the temporary change

Rationale: Identifying an authorizing clinician does not say when the original limits resume. The missing endpoint remains unresolved.

C. The interventions permitted during anesthesia

Rationale: Permitted interventions are already recorded. Repeating them would not supply the omitted timing or condition for reinstatement.

D. The surrogate confirming the operative goals

Rationale: The operative goals are already documented. Surrogate confirmation of those goals does not itself establish when the prior limits return.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Temporary modification needs a documented endpoint for reinstating the original directive. Recording the permitted interventions alone leaves uncertainty about when the earlier limits resume. Remember to plan both the change and its end with the responsible team. This question asks for that missing endpoint, not a universal time that applies to every operation.

**Short explanation fallback:** Document when the original limits will resume. The note already describes the temporary intervention permissions and goals, but a modification without an endpoint can remain ambiguous.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.perioperative-dnr.3`, `claim.meckel-diverticulum.resection-extent-base-and-ileum`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/surgery/articles/10.3389/fsurg.2018.00055/full" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.blouhos.meckel-adult-surgical-concerns.2018</a>; <a href="https://www.medsci.org/v09p0243.htm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ding.perforated-meckel-adults.2012</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/orders-not-attempt-resuscitation-dnar" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-dnar-5-4</a>; <a href="https://www.asahq.org/standards-and-practice-parameters/statement-on-ethical-guidelines-for-the-anesthesia-care-of-patients-with-do-not-resuscitate-orders" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.asa-dnar-2023</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36821518/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.tree.meckel-diverticulectomy-multicentre.2023</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.perioperative-dnr.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Acute superficial perianal abscess

**Complaint:** Abscess care preferences

**Person and setting:** Julian Torres; age 58, Male.

**Story:** Julian Torres, a 58-year-old man, has a superficial perianal abscess requiring drainage and an existing DNR order. A staff member proposes omitting routine treatment and comfort measures because of the order. The patient asks whether abscess treatment and other care can continue. No individualized decision to refuse those treatments has been expressed; the team is preparing care.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team interpret Julian Torres's DNR order when a staff member proposes omitting abscess treatment and comfort care?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Resuscitation after cardiopulmonary arrest **(key)**

Rationale: DNR alone concerns resuscitative efforts after cardiopulmonary arrest. Other care remains a separate decision guided by the patient's goals.

B. Resuscitation and anesthetic airway support

Rationale: The order alone does not establish a blanket ban on anesthetic airway support. Perioperative interventions require their own individualized discussion.

C. Resuscitation and treatment of a new infection

Rationale: A DNR order alone does not decline appropriate treatment of infection. Adding that restriction silently expands its scope beyond resuscitation.

D. Resuscitation and diagnostic testing

Rationale: Diagnostic testing is not automatically prohibited by DNR status. It remains a separate care decision consistent with the patient's goals.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A DNR order concerns resuscitative efforts after cardiopulmonary arrest. By itself it does not prohibit other appropriate evaluation or treatment consistent with the patient's goals. Remember to distinguish the scope of the order from separate decisions about other care. Any further limits need their own discussion rather than being silently inferred from those letters.

**Short explanation fallback:** DNR alone limits resuscitation after cardiopulmonary arrest. It does not by itself decline symptom treatment, diagnostic testing, or other appropriate care consistent with the patient's goals.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.perioperative-dnr.4`, `claim.bread-butter.perianal-abscess.prompt-drainage`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ascrsu.com/ascrs/view/ASCRS-Toolkit/2851069/all/Management_of_Anorectal_Abscess_Fistula_in_Ano_and_Rectovaginal_Fistula__2022_" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.ascrs-anorectal-abscess-2022</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/orders-not-attempt-resuscitation-dnar" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-dnar-5-4</a>; <a href="https://www.asahq.org/standards-and-practice-parameters/statement-on-ethical-guidelines-for-the-anesthesia-care-of-patients-with-do-not-resuscitate-orders" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.asa-dnar-2023</a>.

## Disclose errors and address safety

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.error-disclosure`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.error-disclosure.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Recognized securely repaired enterotomy during incisional-hernia repair

**Complaint:** Operation explanation

**Person and setting:** Maya Reed; age 46, Female.

**Story:** Maya Reed, a 46-year-old woman, is recovering after incisional-hernia repair. A small-bowel enterotomy was recognized and securely repaired, with controlled contamination and adequate source control. The team confirms an unintended operative error and has arranged continuing care. The patient asks why the operation changed. A colleague proposes describing only the repair while omitting the verified error.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should the team disclose to Maya Reed about the verified unintended enterotomy during incisional-hernia repair?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Explain the operative error and continuing care plan **(key)**

Rationale: Explain the verified unintended injury, its repair, known implications and continuing care plan honestly. Reporting the repair alone would conceal why the operation changed.

B. Explain the repair and leave its cause unstated

Rationale: Describing the repair alone omits the verified error that prompted it. A technically true fragment can still leave the patient with a misleading account.

C. Explain that review must finish before discussing events

Rationale: An internal review may clarify remaining uncertainties, but it does not require withholding the verified injury and repair. Known facts and the care plan can be discussed now.

D. Explain the planned care without mentioning the injury

Rationale: A forward-looking care plan does not replace an honest account of the verified operative event. The patient has asked why the operation changed.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Honest disclosure explains the verified error, its known implications and the steps taken to address it. Here that includes the unintended bowel injury, repair and continuing care plan. Remember: separate known facts from remaining uncertainty. A description of the repair alone can mislead if it conceals why the operation changed.

**Short explanation fallback:** Explain the verified unintended injury, its repair, known implications and continuing care plan. An incomplete account or unfinished internal review does not justify concealing known facts.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.error-disclosure.1`, `claim.ventral-hernia.controlled-enterotomy-selected-single-stage-repair`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.frontierspartnerships.org/journals/journal-of-abdominal-wall-surgery/articles/10.3389/jaws.2026.16228/full" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ehs.emergency-ventral-incisional-hernia.2026</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/promoting-patient-safety" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-safety-8-6</a>; <a href="https://www.sages.org/publications/guidelines/guidelines-for-laparoscopic-ventral-hernia-repair/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sages.laparoscopic-ventral-hernia.2016</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5545868/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.wses.complicated-abdominal-wall-hernia.2017</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.error-disclosure.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Painful unequivocally simple breast cyst

**Complaint:** Symptoms before aspiration

**Person and setting:** Owen Reed; age 63, Male.

**Story:** Owen Reed, a 63-year-old man, is preparing for aspiration of a painful simple breast cyst. An unintended medication is given, and the patient develops symptoms. The team recognizes the error, but clinical help has not been arranged. A staff member proposes completing an incident form before seeking assessment. The patient asks for help while the procedure remains paused.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should the team prioritize after Owen Reed develops symptoms from an unintended medication while preparing for breast-cyst aspiration, before clinical help is arranged?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Arrange immediate assessment and patient care **(key)**

Rationale: The patient is symptomatic after an unintended medication. Immediate assessment and appropriate care take priority, with disclosure and reporting accompanying the response.

B. Complete the safety report to obtain a review referral

Rationale: Completing an incident report first delays needed assessment. The form documents the event; it is not the clinical response to the symptoms.

C. Contact the disclosure lead before arranging assessment

Rationale: Disclosure coordination does not need to come before arranging clinical help. The symptomatic patient requires assessment and care now.

D. Verify the medication record before calling for clinical help

Rationale: The team already recognizes an unintended medication and new symptoms. Record checking must not become a reason to delay calling for the clinical help required.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Immediate patient safety and appropriate treatment take priority when symptoms develop after an unintended medication. Arrange assessment and care while candid disclosure and reporting accompany the response. Remember that an incident form records the event; it does not treat the patient. Administrative or disclosure coordination should not delay the indicated clinical help.

**Short explanation fallback:** Arrange immediate assessment and patient care. Disclosure and safety reporting remain part of the response, but completing those steps first must not delay help for the symptomatic patient.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.error-disclosure.2`, `claim.breast-cyst.simple-symptomatic.aspiration`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.acog.org/womens-health/faqs/benign-breast-problems-and-conditions" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.acog.benign-breast-conditions.2025</a>; <a href="https://www.breastsurgeons.org/docs/resources/ASBrS_benign_5things_list.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.asbrs.benign-breast-five-things.2023</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/promoting-patient-safety" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-safety-8-6</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.error-disclosure.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Small accessible pigmented lesion concerning for melanoma

**Complaint:** Missing specimen result

**Person and setting:** Claire Bennett; age 28, Female.

**Story:** Claire Bennett, a 28-year-old woman, returns after diagnostic excision of a small accessible pigmented lesion concerning for melanoma. The clinic confirms the specimen is lost; the cause and clinical implications remain uncertain. A supervisor suspects the courier and proposes that explanation. The patient asks what is known and what happens next while further assessment is arranged.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team explain the confirmed lost diagnostic specimen to Claire Bennett when its cause and implications remain uncertain?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Explain known facts, uncertainty, and next steps **(key)**

Rationale: State the confirmed loss, current uncertainty, and care and investigation plan. This separates verified facts from the unverified courier theory.

B. Explain the likely courier cause and offer a replacement plan

Rationale: The proposed courier explanation remains a theory. Presenting it as the cause replaces acknowledged uncertainty with unverified blame.

C. Wait for a cause to be established before discussing the loss

Rationale: A cause need not be established before explaining the known loss. The team can disclose verified facts while acknowledging the investigation is incomplete.

D. Discuss replacement sampling and defer the reason for it

Rationale: A plan for further sampling cannot replace explaining the verified loss and uncertainty. The exercise also does not establish that a specific repeat procedure is always required.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Honest disclosure separates verified facts from theories. You can explain a confirmed loss while its cause is still under investigation, acknowledging uncertainty and describing the care and investigation plan. Remember that uncertainty is information to disclose, not a gap to fill with blame. The known fact is specimen loss, not the suspected courier explanation.

**Short explanation fallback:** Explain the confirmed loss, what remains uncertain, and the care and investigation plan. Do not state the courier theory as fact or withhold the known loss until the investigation is complete.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.error-disclosure.3`, `claim.pigmented-skin-lesion.complete-diagnostic-biopsy`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/promoting-patient-safety" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-safety-8-6</a>; <a href="https://www.aad.org/member/clinical-quality/guidelines/melanoma" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.aad-melanoma-highlights</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/skin/hp/melanoma-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.nci-melanoma-pdq-2025</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.error-disclosure.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Suspected post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder

**Complaint:** Changed biopsy recommendation

**Person and setting:** Noah Walker; age 77, Male.

**Story:** Noah Walker, a 77-year-old man, has suspected post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder and returns to clarify a tissue-biopsy discussion. The clinic confirms that another person's chart materially influenced its earlier recommendation. The mistake was caught before treatment, with no demonstrated injury. A colleague proposes silently correcting the plan. The patient asks why the recommendation has changed during today's visit.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should the team tell Noah Walker about the wrong-chart error that materially affected the tissue-biopsy discussion, despite no demonstrated injury?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Explain the material error and its correction **(key)**

Rationale: The wrong-chart recommendation materially affected care information. Explain that mistake and its correction even though it was intercepted without demonstrated injury.

B. Discuss the corrected recommendation and defer the error

Rationale: Silently giving a corrected recommendation omits the material error that changed the earlier discussion. Absence of injury is not a blanket reason to leave it unexplained.

C. Use the internal safety report as the disclosure response

Rationale: Internal reporting and patient disclosure serve different parts of the response. Filing a report does not tell the patient about the material mistake and correction.

D. Wait for an injury assessment before discussing the mistake

Rationale: The supplied error materially affected the care discussion. Waiting to demonstrate injury treats harm as a blanket disclosure requirement that the existing claim does not impose.

**Visible teachingPoint:** An error can materially affect a care discussion even when it is intercepted before treatment and no injury is demonstrated. That absence of harm is not a blanket reason to conceal it. Explain the material mistake and correction honestly. Consider what changed the patient's care information, rather than using injury alone as the disclosure test.

**Short explanation fallback:** Explain the material wrong-chart error and its correction. An internal report or silent replacement of the recommendation does not substitute for candid disclosure merely because no injury is demonstrated.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.error-disclosure.4`, `claim.ptld.pattern`, `claim.ptld.tissue`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/promoting-patient-safety" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-safety-8-6</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31230381/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ptld.ast-2019</a>; <a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/transplantation/articles/10.3389/frtra.2026.1869288/full" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ptld.frontiers-review-2026</a>.

## Protect patients when a colleague may be impaired

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.impaired-colleague`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.impaired-colleague.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Uncomplicated cutaneous abscess

**Complaint:** Drainage team coverage

**Person and setting:** Grace Patel; age 61, Female.

**Story:** Grace Patel, a 61-year-old woman, is awaiting drainage of a confirmed cutaneous abscess. The assigned clinician becomes newly unsteady, slurs speech and cannot follow the setup checklist. The cause is unknown, qualified coverage is available and drainage has not started. The team is discussing protection of the patient and assessment of the clinician before care proceeds.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should the team do before Grace Patel's abscess drainage when the assigned clinician is newly unsteady and unable to follow the setup checklist?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Arrange safe coverage and clinical assessment **(key)**

Rationale: Observed inability to perform safely requires protecting the patient and arranging assessment. Available qualified coverage supports doing that without assuming why the clinician changed.

B. Proceed with a second clinician observing the procedure

Rationale: Observation by another clinician does not itself resolve the assigned clinician's inability to perform the setup safely. Use available safe coverage and arrange assessment.

C. Pause the procedure and ask the clinician to self-assess

Rationale: Self-assessment alone does not address the observed inability. The team must protect care and arrange appropriate assessment rather than placing the whole response on the affected clinician.

D. Recheck performance after a brief rest before arranging help

Rationale: Waiting to see whether rest changes performance delays the available protection and assessment. The present unsafe performance, not an established diagnosis, warrants action.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Observable inability to practice safely calls for patient protection and appropriate assessment, even before the cause is known. Arrange safe coverage and help for the clinician rather than assuming intoxication or relying only on self-assessment. Remember to act on the demonstrated performance problem while keeping its cause an open question until appropriately assessed.

**Short explanation fallback:** Arrange safe coverage and appropriate assessment for the clinician. The observed inability requires action, but the behavior alone does not establish its cause.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.impaired-colleague.1`, `claim.gs028se.impaired-colleague.2`, `claim.gs028se.impaired-colleague.4`, `claim.bread-butter.cutaneous-abscess.incision-drainage`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.acep.org/sonoguide/procedures/abscess-evaluation" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.acep-sonoguide-abscess</a>; <a href="https://www.idsociety.org/practice-guideline/skin-and-soft-tissue-infections/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.idsa-ssti-2014</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/physician-responsibilities-colleagues-illness-disability-or-impairment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-impairment-9-3-2</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.impaired-colleague.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Biopsy-confirmed celiac disease

**Complaint:** Biopsy follow-up concerns

**Person and setting:** Adrian Chen; age 48, Male.

**Story:** Adrian Chen, a 48-year-old man, needs duodenal-biopsy follow-up during a celiac disease evaluation. The team documents recent clinician confusion and missed instructions that could compromise that follow-up. A supervisor wants the concerns kept private solely to protect the clinician's reputation. The cause remains unknown; the patient's follow-up and the colleague's assessment still need attention today.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team respond to credible clinician-confusion concerns that may compromise Adrian Chen's duodenal-biopsy follow-up?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Arrange assessment and appropriate safety reporting **(key)**

Rationale: Documented confusion that could compromise care warrants assessment, appropriate reporting, and patient protection while supporting the colleague. A final cause is not required to start that response.

B. Arrange peer coaching and recheck performance at follow-up

Rationale: Coaching alone treats the concern as a routine skills issue without addressing the documented confusion, appropriate assessment, or safety reporting.

C. Transfer the follow-up and await an impairment diagnosis

Rationale: Transferring follow-up may protect that task, but waiting for a diagnosis omits the appropriate assessment and reporting response to the current credible concern.

D. Ask the clinician to explain the missed instructions first

Rationale: The clinician's account may inform assessment, but requesting an explanation first must not replace protection and the appropriate response to documented confusion.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Credible concerns about unsafe practice need patient protection, appropriate assessment, and reporting suited to the setting, alongside support for the colleague. Waiting for a final diagnosis does not resolve the care concern. Remember that protecting reputation cannot replace a safety response; use observed evidence without inventing the cause or treating reporting as a diagnosis.

**Short explanation fallback:** Arrange assessment, appropriate safety reporting, and protection of follow-up while supporting the clinician. Concealment or waiting for a diagnosis leaves the documented practice concern unaddressed.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.impaired-colleague.2`, `claim.gs028se.impaired-colleague.1`, `claim.celiac.adult-biopsy`, `claim.celiac.gluten-free-treatment`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://gi.org/topics/celiac-disease/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.acg.celiac-patient-education.2026</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/physician-responsibilities-colleagues-illness-disability-or-impairment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-impairment-9-3-2</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/celiac-disease/diagnosis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.celiac-diagnosis.2020</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/clinical-tools-patient-management/digestive-diseases/celiac-disease-health-care-professionals?dkrd=www2.niddk.nih.gov" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.celiac-tests.2021</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/celiac-disease/treatment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.niddk.celiac-treatment.2020</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.impaired-colleague.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Chronic anal fissure

**Complaint:** Clinician fitness question

**Person and setting:** Nora Hayes; age 69, Female.

**Story:** Nora Hayes, a 69-year-old woman, is discussing treatment options for chronic anal fissure. The clinician performs the relevant duties safely with accommodations for a hand disability, with no observed performance concern. The patient asks how the team determines whether someone can perform the work safely. The consultation has not started, and there is time to address the question.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should determine the clinician's fitness to conduct Nora Hayes's fissure-treatment consultation when a disability is accommodated and actual performance is safe?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Actual ability to perform the care safely **(key)**

Rationale: The relevant issue is actual safe performance, including appropriate accommodations. The scenario establishes that ability and supplies no performance concern.

B. The clinician's diagnosis and expected disease course

Rationale: A diagnosis and its expected course are not by themselves a finding of inability to perform this care safely. The actual relevant duties and performance matter.

C. The clinician's ability without workplace accommodations

Rationale: Excluding accommodations changes the supplied assessment. Safe performance with appropriate accommodations is relevant to whether the clinician can perform the duties.

D. The patient's comfort with the clinician's appearance

Rationale: Appearance or patient discomfort about it does not establish unsafe practice. The scenario supplies safe performance and no observed concern to support that inference.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Disability and inability to practice safely are different questions. Judge the clinician's actual performance of the relevant duties, including appropriate accommodations. Here safe performance is established and no performance concern exists. Remember to assess the relevant work rather than using appearance, a diagnosis, or ability without accommodations as a substitute for that assessment.

**Short explanation fallback:** Use actual ability to perform the relevant care safely, including appropriate accommodations. The supplied safe performance does not support treating visible disability itself as impairment.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.impaired-colleague.3`, `claim.chronic-anal-fissure.topical-ccb-first-line`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.ascrsu.com/ascrs/repview?name=3_2851088_PDF&amp;type=784-162" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.chronic-fissure.ascrs-cpg.2023</a>; <a href="https://doi.org/10.1186/s12893-023-02223-z" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.chronic-fissure.siucp.2023</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/physician-responsibilities-colleagues-illness-disability-or-impairment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-impairment-9-3-2</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.impaired-colleague.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Arteriovenous dialysis-access dysfunction

**Complaint:** Requested clinical coverage

**Person and setting:** Julian Torres; age 58, Male.

**Story:** Julian Torres, a 58-year-old man, needs assessment of new dialysis-access dysfunction. The assigned clinician privately reports that a current illness prevents safe concentration and asks to be relieved. Qualified coverage is available and the consultation has not started. A colleague asks how to respond while arranging the patient's assessment and appropriate support for the clinician.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team respond when the clinician assigned to Julian Torres's dialysis-access assessment reports inability to concentrate safely and requests relief?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Arrange safe coverage and appropriate assistance **(key)**

Rationale: Arrange the available safe coverage and appropriate assistance. The clinician has recognized a current inability to concentrate safely and asked for relief before the procedure.

B. Reassign demanding tasks and have the clinician continue

Rationale: Reassigning only demanding tasks still requires practice despite the stated inability to concentrate safely. It does not provide the requested relief and available coverage.

C. Offer a short rest before considering relief from duty

Rationale: A rest trial postpones relief despite the clinician's stated inability and available coverage. Support the request rather than requiring further unsafe practice to demonstrate it.

D. Cancel the visit and defer assistance to occupational health

Rationale: Qualified coverage is available, so cancellation does not use that option. Deferring assistance also misses the colleague's current request for help.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A clinician who recognizes inability to practice safely should be supported in obtaining coverage and appropriate assistance. Here that concern is already expressed and qualified coverage is available. Remember to respond before an unsafe performance is required to prove the problem. Patient protection and help for the clinician belong in the same response.

**Short explanation fallback:** Arrange safe coverage and appropriate assistance now. The clinician has identified inability to concentrate safely, and qualified replacement care is available before the clinical consultation begins.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.impaired-colleague.4`, `claim.gs028se.impaired-colleague.1`, `claim.gs028d.access.dysfunction-referral`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/physician-responsibilities-colleagues-illness-disability-or-impairment" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-impairment-9-3-2</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12351868/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.ukka.vascular-access.2025</a>.

## Protect voluntary research participation

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.research-consent`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.research-consent.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Gastric gastrointestinal stromal tumor

**Complaint:** Optional GIST study

**Investigational premise:** Cancer recurrence-prevention biologic (drug).

**Person and setting:** Maya Reed; age 46, Female.

**Story:** Maya Reed, a 46-year-old woman, has a gastric GIST and is discussing specialist tissue and operative planning. A recruiter offers an optional study of a new biologic and says ordinary care requires enrollment. That study is not required for clinical care. The patient asks whether declining research would prevent the clinical consultation from continuing as planned.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should the team explain when Maya Reed is told that ordinary gastric-GIST care depends on joining an optional new-biologic study?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Declining enrollment does not forfeit ordinary care **(key)**

Rationale: This study is optional and not required for ordinary care. Clarify that declining enrollment does not forfeit that care, so the invitation can be considered voluntarily.

B. Declining enrollment requires transfer to another clinic

Rationale: Requiring transfer would impose a loss of ordinary care for declining this optional study. The scenario gives no basis for making that care contingent on enrollment.

C. Declining enrollment postpones the surgical consultation

Rationale: Postponing the ordinary consultation because enrollment is declined still makes care conditional on joining. That conflicts with voluntary choice in this setting.

D. Declining enrollment limits access to routine follow-up

Rationale: Limiting routine follow-up uses ordinary care to pressure enrollment. The optional study is not required for that care.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Research participation must be voluntary. When a study is optional and not required for ordinary care, declining it should not forfeit that care. Remember to separate the clinical care decision from the research invitation. Making enrollment a condition of routine care pressures the patient instead of supporting an independent choice about participation.

**Short explanation fallback:** Clarify that declining this optional study does not forfeit ordinary clinic care. The recruiter's stated condition is inconsistent with voluntary participation in a study not required for that care.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.research-consent.1`, `claim.gastric-gist.selected-eus-core-molecular-testing`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://grupogeis.org/documentos/guias-geis/2023-GEIS-guidelines-for-GIST.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.gist-geis-2023</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/types/soft-tissue-sarcoma/hp/gist-treatment-pdq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.board.gist-nci-pdq-2024</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent-research" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-research-7-1-2</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.research-consent.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Biopsy-confirmed Barrett esophagus

**Complaint:** Additional research sampling

**Investigational premise:** Optical Barrett-esophagus study test (test).

**Person and setting:** Owen Reed; age 35, Male.

**Story:** Owen Reed, a 35-year-old man, has biopsy-confirmed Barrett esophagus and has consented to a clinical endoscopic examination. An optical-test study would add tissue sampling solely for research, which has not been discussed. The coordinator calls the packet concise; it has separate instructions for folding its instructions. The patient asks what the clinical signature permits before the examination starts.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What is needed before additional research-only tissue sampling when Owen Reed has consented only to a clinical endoscopic examination for Barrett esophagus?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Complete an appropriate research consent process **(key)**

Rationale: The additional procedure is research-only and was not discussed in the treatment consent. Address its purpose, added procedure, and voluntary choice through appropriate research consent and oversight.

B. Extend the clinical consent to cover the extra sampling

Rationale: Clinical consent does not expand automatically to an additional undisclosed research procedure. Extending its label does not supply the missing research discussion or applicable oversight.

C. Use a signed research form as the entire consent process

Rationale: A signed research form alone is not the entire consent process. The participant needs the relevant understandable discussion and voluntary choice for the added activity.

D. Ask the treating surgeon to authorize the extra sampling

Rationale: The surgeon's approval is not the patient's research permission. It does not replace the appropriate consent process and applicable oversight.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Consent to treatment does not automatically authorize an additional research-only procedure. The research purpose, added procedure, and voluntary choice need appropriate research consent and applicable oversight. A signature has a scope: adding a research activity requires addressing that activity, rather than extending the clinical form by assumption or treating another form as the conversation.

**Short explanation fallback:** Complete the appropriate research consent process and applicable oversight before the additional sampling. The clinical-treatment signature does not automatically cover this undisclosed research-only procedure.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.research-consent.2`, `claim.gs028se.research-consent.1`, `claim.barrett-esophagus.intestinal-metaplasia`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent-research" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-research-7-1-2</a>; <a href="https://gi.org/topics/barretts-esophagus/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.acg-barrett-patient-2024</a>; <a href="https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/barretts-esophagus/diagnosis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.sc.niddk-barrett-diagnosis</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.research-consent.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Stable minimally symptomatic desmoid tumor

**Complaint:** Study assignment question

**Investigational premise:** Desmoid-tumor study agent (drug).

**Person and setting:** Claire Bennett; age 56, Female.

**Story:** Claire Bennett, a 56-year-old woman, has a stable, minimally symptomatic desmoid tumor under specialist assessment. A trial offers a new agent or control through random allocation. The presenter says every participant receives personalized attention, then reads the same greeting from a script. The patient expects the surgeon to select the personally best arm and asks how treatment will be assigned.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should research consent explain when Claire Bennett expects personally selected treatment in a desmoid-agent trial whose protocol uses random allocation?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Assignment is random and benefit is uncertain **(key)**

Rationale: The approved protocol assigns randomly and guarantees no better arm. Clarifying both points corrects the expectation of personally selected treatment with assured benefit.

B. Assignment is clinical and benefit is individualized

Rationale: The protocol does not use individualized clinical selection. This wording preserves the patient's mistaken expectation instead of explaining random allocation and uncertain benefit.

C. Assignment is random and benefit is established

Rationale: Random assignment is correct, but established benefit is not. No arm is guaranteed better in the supplied protocol.

D. Assignment is chosen and benefit is uncertain

Rationale: Uncertainty about benefit is correct, but assignment is not chosen by the surgeon or participant. The protocol uses random allocation.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Research consent needs a clear account of allocation and uncertainty. This protocol assigns arms randomly; the surgeon does not choose a personally best arm, and benefit is not guaranteed. Remember to distinguish a research protocol from individually selected treatment. Understanding assignment helps the patient choose whether to join, not a detail for after enrollment.

**Short explanation fallback:** Explain that assignment is random and benefit uncertain. The protocol does not let the surgeon select a personally best arm or guarantee that an arm will be better.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.research-consent.3`, `claim.desmoid.initial-active-surveillance-selected-noncritical`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent-research" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-research-7-1-2</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38900421/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.kasper.current-management-desmoid-tumors.2024</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12000159/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.seom-geis.soft-tissue-sarcoma-guideline.2024</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.research-consent.v4`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Hidradenitis suppurativa

**Complaint:** Leaving optional research

**Investigational premise:** Topical wound or skin agent (drug).

**Person and setting:** Noah Walker; age 51, Male.

**Story:** Noah Walker, a 51-year-old man, has hidradenitis suppurativa and wants to leave an optional new topical-agent study. The patient wishes to continue ordinary clinical follow-up but declines further research visits. A coordinator threatens to cancel unrelated appointments unless participation continues. The patient asks whether leaving the study means losing access to the clinic's ordinary care.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team respond to Noah Walker's request to leave an optional hidradenitis-agent study when unrelated ordinary appointments are threatened?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Support withdrawal and preserve ordinary follow-up **(key)**

Rationale: Support the voluntary withdrawal and discuss ordinary follow-up separately. Unrelated care must not be used as leverage to require continued research participation.

B. Defer withdrawal until the remaining study visits are complete

Rationale: Requiring completion of remaining visits before withdrawal makes continued participation compulsory. That does not respect the voluntary withdrawal request.

C. Require the investigator's approval before withdrawal

Rationale: Investigator permission is not a condition that justifies compelling continued participation. The patient wants to leave a voluntary study.

D. Arrange transfer of ordinary care before allowing withdrawal

Rationale: Requiring transfer first still ties ordinary care to continued enrollment. Preserve the unrelated follow-up rather than using its loss to prevent withdrawal.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Voluntary participation includes being able to withdraw without coercive loss of ordinary care. Discuss ongoing clinical follow-up separately from whether research continues. Remember that stopping research participation and stopping clinical care are different choices. Neither completing unwanted study visits nor obtaining investigator permission should be used as the price of preserving unrelated follow-up.

**Short explanation fallback:** Support withdrawal and preserve ordinary clinical follow-up. The threatened cancellation of unrelated care must not be used to compel continued participation in a voluntary study.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.research-consent.4`, `claim.gs028se.research-consent.1`, `claim.hs.pattern-recognition`, `claim.hs.multimodal-planning`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-consent-2-1-1</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/informed-consent-research" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-research-7-1-2</a>; <a href="https://doi.org/10.1111/ajd.14388" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.hs.australasian-2025</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9131894/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.hs.north-american-2019</a>.

## Seek appropriate QI and research oversight

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.qi-versus-research`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qi-versus-research.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Anemia in the perioperative setting

**Complaint:** Anemia pathway review

**Investigational premise:** Perioperative anemia assessment pathway (care pathway).

**Person and setting:** Grace Patel; age 32, Female.

**Story:** Grace Patel, a 32-year-old woman, has perioperative anemia and is discussing an authorized pilot of a new assessment pathway. Its stated purpose is local improvement, and the team hopes to publish. The editor asks for a dramatic title before receiving the manuscript. The patient asks whether publication plans by themselves make the project research requiring a different review route.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should publication plans affect classification of the local anemia-assessment pathway project involving Grace Patel?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Publication plans alone do not define research **(key)**

Rationale: A journal plan alone does not determine research classification. The institution must consider the project's purpose, design, and applicable definitions.

B. Publication intent establishes a generalizable research aim

Rationale: Wanting publication is not by itself proof of a generalizable research aim. The actual purpose and activities still require assessment.

C. Journal submission determines the need for research review

Rationale: Journal submission is a publishing step, not the institutional determination of applicable research requirements. The project cannot be classified from that step alone.

D. Peer review determines the project's research classification

Rationale: Peer review evaluates a submission; it does not by itself classify the underlying activity under applicable research definitions.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Publication intent alone does not determine whether an activity is research. Classification depends on its purpose, design, and applicable definitions, assessed through the appropriate institution. Remember that where a report may appear is different from what the project does. This exercise does not establish an exemption or replace assessment with a publishing plan.

**Short explanation fallback:** Explain that publication plans alone do not define research. Appropriate institutional assessment must consider the project's purpose, design, and applicable definitions rather than its intended journal destination.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.qi-versus-research.1`, `claim.gs028f.oxygen.anemia-content-limit`, `claim.gs028f.oxygen.hemoglobin-carriage`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.fda.gov/media/175828/download" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.fda_oximetry2024</a>; <a href="https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/anemia/diagnosis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.nhlbi_anemia2025</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/ohrp/regulations-and-policy/guidance/faq/quality-improvement-activities/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ohrp-qi</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qi-versus-research.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Gastroesophageal reflux disease under evaluation

**Complaint:** Experimental reflux project

**Investigational premise:** Reflux treatment delivery device (device).

**Person and setting:** Adrian Chen; age 74, Male.

**Story:** Adrian Chen, a 74-year-old man, is undergoing assessment for reflux disease and is offered a new experimental treatment-delivery device. A project labeled QI proposes assigning device use to test a generalizable hypothesis. The organizer calls review straightforward, then forwards five messages asking who owns the straightforward-review form. The patient asks what assessment should occur before the additional intervention is introduced.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What oversight step is needed before a project labeled QI tests an experimental reflux-treatment device with Adrian Chen for a generalizable hypothesis?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Seek institutional assessment before proceeding **(key)**

Rationale: The experimental intervention and generalizable hypothesis require institutional assessment before proceeding. A QI label does not remove possible research requirements.

B. Proceed under the local QI designation while collecting data

Rationale: Proceeding under the label assumes the very determination that needs assessment. Local improvement terminology cannot exempt an experimental research component by itself.

C. Use publication plans to decide whether review is needed

Rationale: Publication plans alone do not determine whether review is needed. The project's experimental activity and purpose must be assessed.

D. Use standard treatment consent for the added intervention

Rationale: Standard treatment consent does not settle research classification or applicable research consent requirements for the added intervention.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Calling an activity QI does not settle whether it also contains human-subjects research. The added experimental intervention and generalizable aim need institutional assessment before proceeding. Remember to examine purpose and activities rather than accepting a label as an exemption. Applicable review and consent requirements cannot be decided by project branding or ordinary treatment consent alone.

**Short explanation fallback:** Seek institutional assessment before proceeding. The QI label does not resolve the experimental research features or establish that ordinary treatment consent is sufficient.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.qi-versus-research.2`, `claim.gs028se.qi-versus-research.1`, `claim.early.gerd.off-ppi-reflux-monitoring`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8754510/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.acg.gerd.2022</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/ohrp/regulations-and-policy/guidance/faq/quality-improvement-activities/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ohrp-qi</a>; <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31375601/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.icarus.antireflux.2019</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qi-versus-research.v3`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Persistently high-output ileostomy after stabilization

**Complaint:** Ileostomy improvement project

**Person and setting:** Nora Hayes; age 45, Female.

**Story:** Nora Hayes, a 45-year-old woman, has a high-output ileostomy after stabilization. The team is implementing an accepted sodium-glucose rehydration plan solely to improve local delivery, without an experimental addition or generalizable research hypothesis. The administrator requests a mission statement for the hydration service before approving cups. The patient asks why practical local measurements do not automatically determine research classification.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should the team explain about research classification when Nora Hayes's ileostomy project implements accepted rehydration practice solely for local improvement?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. The stated purpose supports local improvement **(key)**

Rationale: The stated project implements established practice for practical local improvement, with no research component identified. That supports this rationale without erasing other institutional obligations.

B. The local measurements support research classification

Rationale: Collecting practical implementation measurements does not by itself make the activity research. Purpose and activities matter, not measurement alone.

C. The publication plans support research classification

Rationale: Publication intent alone does not classify an activity as research. The supplied local implementation purpose remains the relevant information to assess.

D. The patient involvement supports research classification

Rationale: Involving patients does not automatically establish a research purpose. The activity's purpose and design still determine how it should be assessed.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Implementing established practice and collecting practical local improvement data may fall outside the research definition when no research component is identified. That does not erase other institutional obligations. Remember to ask why the activity is being done and what it adds; measurement, publication interest, or patient involvement alone does not automatically make it research.

**Short explanation fallback:** The stated purpose supports local improvement rather than an identified research component. Practical measurement alone does not determine classification, and other institutional obligations still need consideration.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.qi-versus-research.3`, `claim.gs028se.qi-versus-research.1`, `claim.bread-butter.high-output-ileostomy.renal-electrolyte-magnesium-assessment`, `claim.bread-butter.high-output-ileostomy.sodium-glucose-oral-rehydration`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.bapen.org.uk/pdfs/bifa/bifa-top-tips-series-1-updated.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.bifa-high-output-stoma-2023</a>; <a href="https://www.bapen.org.uk/pdfs/bifa/bifa-top-tips-series-30.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bread-butter.bifa-oral-rehydration-2025</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/ohrp/regulations-and-policy/guidance/faq/quality-improvement-activities/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ohrp-qi</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.qi-versus-research.v4`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Documented malignant-hyperthermia susceptibility

**Complaint:** Anesthesia project review

**Investigational premise:** Anesthesia monitoring sensor (device).

**Person and setting:** Julian Torres; age 58, Male.

**Story:** Julian Torres, a 58-year-old man, has documented malignant-hyperthermia susceptibility and a planned trigger-free anesthetic. A project combines local delivery improvement with an experimental monitoring sensor and a generalizable research comparison. Six listed coauthors have yet to see the project; the organizer calls this an ambitious collaboration. The patient asks how the combined project should be assessed before the operation.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the team handle review of Julian Torres's anesthesia project when it combines local improvement with an experimental monitoring comparison?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Seek institutional review of the research component **(key)**

Rationale: The activity includes an experimental generalizable research component as well as local improvement. Seek institutional review of that component and its applicable requirements.

B. Review the project under its local improvement designation

Rationale: A local improvement designation does not erase the research aim or added experimental comparison. Both components need appropriate consideration.

C. Review the project after generalizable results are obtained

Rationale: Waiting for generalizable results postpones assessment until after the relevant research activity. Review of applicable requirements belongs before proceeding.

D. Review the project using publication intent as the criterion

Rationale: Publication intent alone cannot classify the combined activities. The supplied experimental comparison and generalizable purpose already require appropriate assessment.

**Visible teachingPoint:** An activity can serve improvement and research purposes together. A local benefit does not remove the need to assess the research component and its applicable review and consent requirements. Remember that combined aims require examining both components, rather than forcing the project into a single convenient label or waiting until results exist.

**Short explanation fallback:** Seek institutional review of the research component. A project can pursue local improvement and generalizable research together; the local aim does not erase applicable research requirements.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.qi-versus-research.4`, `claim.gs028se.qi-versus-research.1`, `claim.gs028se.qi-versus-research.2`, `claim.gs028d.mh.trigger-free-plan`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://www.emhg.org/recommendations-1/2018/2/27/perioperative-management-of-mh-suspected-or-susceptible-patients" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.emhg.mh-susceptible.2018</a>; <a href="https://www.hhs.gov/ohrp/regulations-and-policy/guidance/faq/quality-improvement-activities/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ohrp-qi</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12860801/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.jsa.malignant-hyperthermia.2026</a>.

## Protect confidential patient information

Concept: `concept.statistics-ethics.confidentiality`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.statistics-ethics.confidentiality.v1`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Uncomplicated duodenal ulcer and H. pylori evaluation

**Complaint:** Requested ulcer results

**Person and setting:** Maya Reed; age 71, Female.

**Story:** Maya Reed, a 71-year-old woman, has an uncomplicated H. pylori ulcer and is discussing follow-up. A friend calls for results without authorization or another applicable disclosure exception. The friend describes herself as the patient's unofficial chief information officer, then asks whether that title can be added to the contact list. The clinic has not shared the requested information.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the clinic handle a friend's request for Maya Reed's H. pylori results when authorization and another disclosure exception are absent?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Decline disclosure without patient authorization **(key)**

Rationale: The patient has not authorized disclosure and no applicable exception exists. Decline the friend's request rather than treating interest as permission.

B. Ask the friend to verify the patient's date of birth

Rationale: Knowing identifying details may verify knowledge of the patient, but it does not establish authorization to receive the diagnosis.

C. Confirm the diagnosis already known to the friend

Rationale: Confirming a diagnosis is still disclosure. The friend's claim to know it does not provide the missing authorized basis for the clinic to confirm it.

D. Give the friend a general summary of the procedure

Rationale: A shorter or more general summary still shares private information. Its length does not replace the missing authorization or exception.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Interest in a patient does not itself authorize access to confidential information. Here the patient has not authorized the friend's request and no applicable exception exists, so decline disclosure. Remember that confirming a detail or giving a short summary still shares information. Verifying identity details does not supply the missing permission to share the diagnosis.

**Short explanation fallback:** Decline disclosure without patient authorization in this setting. A friend's knowledge, identifying details, or request for only a summary does not create the missing authorized basis.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.confidentiality.1`, `claim.brief.h-pylori.active-infection-testing`, `claim.brief.h-pylori.test-of-cure-plan`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://webfiles.gi.org/links/journals/ACG-Hpylori-Guidelines-Highlights-2024-FINAL.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.acg-h-pylori-2024</a>; <a href="https://gi.org/topics/peptic-ulcer-disease/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.acg-peptic-ulcer-patient</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/confidentiality" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-confidentiality-3-2-1</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.confidentiality.v2`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Confirmed uncomplicated familial hypocalciuric hypercalcemia

**Complaint:** Chart access question

**Person and setting:** Owen Reed; age 35, Male.

**Story:** Owen Reed, a 35-year-old man, has confirmed uncomplicated familial hypocalciuric hypercalcemia and is attending follow-up. A clinic employee who knows the family wants to open the chart solely from curiosity. The employee says being nosy is community engagement, then asks whether it belongs on the appraisal form. No relevant work purpose requires access to this patient's records.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What should govern a clinic employee's access to Owen Reed's calcium-disorder chart when the employee has only personal curiosity and no relevant work purpose?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. An authorized work-related reason for access **(key)**

Rationale: Access needs an authorized work-related purpose. This neighbor has no care or administrative role requiring the chart, so familiarity and employment do not justify browsing.

B. A personal invitation to look up the patient's diagnosis

Rationale: A personal invitation does not establish a role in authorized care or administrative work. It does not supply the missing work-related purpose for this access.

C. A verified relationship with the patient's family

Rationale: Knowing the family is a personal relationship, not an authorized clinical or administrative reason to read the chart.

D. An active clinic login with chart-reading permission

Rationale: An active login supplies a technical ability to open records. It does not authorize browsing this chart without a relevant work purpose.

**Visible teachingPoint:** A clinic login supplies technical access, not a work-related reason to open every chart. Access should have an authorized purpose connected to the employee's work. Here familiarity and curiosity do not provide one. Remember to distinguish what the system permits someone to click from what their role authorizes them to read for this patient.

**Short explanation fallback:** An authorized work-related reason must govern access. Employment, knowing the family, or having a chart-reading login does not establish a role requiring this patient's information.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.confidentiality.2`, `claim.fhh.reassurance-observation-avoid-parathyroid-surgery`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36245251/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bilezikian.fifth-workshop-phpt-guideline.2022</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8789028/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.bollerslev.parat-parathyroid-consensus.2022</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/confidentiality" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-confidentiality-3-2-1</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.confidentiality.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Adrenal incidentaloma

**Complaint:** Private adrenal discussion

**Person and setting:** Claire Bennett; age 56, Female.

**Story:** Claire Bennett, a 56-year-old woman, has an adrenal incidentaloma and is discussing biochemical evaluation. Staff begin an identifiable, nonurgent case conversation in an elevator where visitors can hear. A private clinical workspace is available. The patient has not authorized public discussion, and the team must decide where to continue the conversation about the planned assessment.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** Where should the team conduct further identifiable discussion of Claire Bennett's adrenal-incidentaloma assessment after the nonurgent elevator conversation?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Move the discussion to a private clinical setting **(key)**

Rationale: Move the nonurgent discussion to the available private clinical workspace. That supports care while avoiding unnecessary exposure to the crowded public setting.

B. Continue quietly and avoid speaking the patient's name

Rationale: Omitting the name and speaking quietly does not necessarily prevent identification from other details. The available private workspace addresses the setting directly.

C. Send the discussion to the team's personal message thread

Rationale: A personal message thread does not establish an appropriate clinical communication setting. Moving the details there does not resolve the authorized-purpose and exposure concerns.

D. Discuss the case at the next hallway team huddle

Rationale: A later hallway huddle remains public. Changing the time does not address who can hear an identifiable case discussion.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Clinical discussion needs a setting that avoids unnecessary exposure of identifiable information. Move this nonurgent conversation to the available private clinical workspace. Remember that removing a name or lowering voices does not necessarily remove identification by other details. Changing the time of a public discussion also fails to address who can hear it.

**Short explanation fallback:** Move the discussion to the available private clinical setting. The case remains identifiable, and neither quieter speech nor omitting the name resolves unnecessary public exposure.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.confidentiality.3`, `claim.brief.adrenal-incidentaloma.one-mg-dst`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamasurgery/fullarticle/2795363" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.aaes-adrenalectomy-2022</a>; <a href="https://academic.oup.com/ejendo/article/189/1/G1/7198474" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.brief.ese-adrenal-incidentaloma-2023</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/confidentiality" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-confidentiality-3-2-1</a>.

### `question.statistics-ethics.confidentiality.v4`

**Humor:** Situational.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Uncomplicated Mondor disease

**Complaint:** Authorized specialist coordination

**Person and setting:** Noah Walker; age 51, Male.

**Story:** Noah Walker, a 51-year-old man, has uncomplicated Mondor disease and authorizes relevant information sharing for a treating specialist's consultation. The referral coordinator offers to send everything because selecting relevant records would involve reading them. The specialist requests the history and findings needed for this visit. The team has the authorization and is preparing the information to share.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** How should the clinic handle the treating specialist's relevant information request after Noah Walker authorizes Mondor-disease care coordination?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Share relevant information for authorized care **(key)**

Rationale: The patient has authorized coordination, and the specialist requests relevant information. Share for that purpose while avoiding unnecessary disclosure.

B. Send the complete chart for the specialist to filter

Rationale: The whole chart may include information beyond the consultation's purpose. Authorization to coordinate does not justify unnecessary disclosure for the specialist to filter later.

C. Request a new authorization for each relevant chart item

Rationale: The supplied authorization already supports relevant coordination. Treating every relevant item as newly prohibited obstructs that authorized care purpose.

D. Send a de-identified summary for the treating consultation

Rationale: The specialist needs relevant patient-specific consultation information. A de-identified substitute does not provide the authorized coordination requested in this scenario.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Confidentiality permits appropriate exchange for authorized care; it does not prohibit all coordination. Share information relevant to the consultation while avoiding unnecessary disclosure. Remember to match the information to its purpose. Sending the whole chart for someone else to filter, or treating every relevant item as newly forbidden, misses that balance in this supplied setting.

**Short explanation fallback:** Share relevant patient-specific information for the authorized consultation, avoiding unnecessary disclosure. The stated authorization supports that coordination; confidentiality does not require blocking every exchange or sending the entire chart.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028se.confidentiality.4`, `claim.mondor-disease.classic-clinical-phenotype`, `claim.mondor-disease.supportive-management-and-boundaries`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6191595/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.amano-shimizu.mondor-review.2018</a>; <a href="https://code-medical-ethics.ama-assn.org/ethics-opinions/confidentiality" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028se.ama-confidentiality-3-2-1</a>; <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK538282/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.statpearls.mondor-disease.2023</a>.

## Match RCA and FMEA to the safety task

Concept: `concept.gs028f.outcomes.rca-versus-fmea-purpose`. 4 variants; objective and stable identity retained.

### `question.gs028f.outcomes.rca-versus-fmea-purpose.v1`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Confirmed sharp esophageal foreign body

**Complaint:** Referral close-call review

**Person and setting:** Grace Patel; age 40, Female.

**Story:** Grace Patel, a 40-year-old woman, returns after hospital removal of a sharp esophageal foreign body. During referral, an emergency request nearly entered a routine queue, but the mismatch was caught before delay. The hospital is reconstructing that close call and examining referral and handoff processes. The patient brings its letter and asks what kind of investigation is underway.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** Which safety method fits reconstruction of Grace Patel's completed emergency-referral close call and its contributing systems problems?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Root cause analysis **(key)**

Rationale: The review starts with a specific completed close call and reconstructs how referral-priority and handoff systems contributed. That is the retrospective purpose of RCA.

B. Healthcare failure mode and effects analysis

Rationale: Healthcare FMEA anticipates possible process failures prospectively. This review instead starts with a particular close call that already occurred.

C. Individual performance audit

Rationale: The described review examines contributing referral-priority and handoff systems. An individual performance judgment alone does not describe that event-centered systems purpose.

D. Patient satisfaction survey

Rationale: The patient asks about reconstruction of a specific safety event. Measuring satisfaction would answer a different question from how the systems contributed.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Root cause analysis starts with an event or close call that already happened and reconstructs contributing systems problems. Here the referral handoff is the starting event. Remember: start with what happened and examine how the process contributed. Healthcare FMEA instead looks ahead for possible process failures; neither label is a verdict of individual blame.

**Short explanation fallback:** This is root cause analysis: reconstruction of a completed close call to identify systems contributors. The review is retrospective, rather than a prospective assessment of possible future failures.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028f.safety.rca-retrospective`, `claim.gs028f.safety.fmea-prospective`, `claim.gs028f.foreign-body.sharp-esophageal-emergency`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://psnet.ahrq.gov/primer/root-cause-analysis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.ahrq_rca2024</a>; <a href="https://www.esge.com/assets/downloads/pdfs/guidelines/2016_s_0042_100456.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.esge_foreignbody2016</a>; <a href="https://www.patientsafety.va.gov/professionals/onthejob/hfmea.asp" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.va_pra2026</a>; <a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6544956/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.wses_esophagus2019</a>.

### `question.gs028f.outcomes.rca-versus-fmea-purpose.v2`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Human renal allograft with immunosuppression

**Complaint:** Planned transplant handoff

**Person and setting:** Adrian Chen; age 33, Male.

**Story:** Adrian Chen, a 33-year-old man, has a renal allograft and is attending follow-up. The hospital plans a new immunosuppression handoff that has not started. Its safety team will examine process steps for potential failures and consider safeguards in advance. The patient brings the notice and asks about the review before the medication-coordination workflow is introduced locally.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** Which safety method fits advance assessment of possible failures in Adrian Chen's planned immunosuppression handoff process?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Healthcare FMEA **(key)**

Rationale: The team is anticipating ways a process might fail before using it. That prospective process-centered task fits healthcare FMEA.

B. Root cause analysis

Rationale: RCA reconstructs a completed event or close call. No such event is the starting point of this planned workflow assessment.

C. Individual performance audit

Rationale: The planned task examines potential failures across process steps. It is not primarily an assessment of one worker's performance.

D. Patient satisfaction survey

Rationale: The team is identifying potential process failures before launch. Asking about satisfaction would not identify the prospective method described.

**Visible teachingPoint:** Healthcare FMEA looks ahead at a process to identify potential failures before those problems occur. Here the planned immunosuppression handoff is being examined before use. Remember the starting point: a process to anticipate, rather than an event to reconstruct. That purpose differs from retrospective RCA and does not guarantee that all risk will disappear.

**Short explanation fallback:** Healthcare FMEA fits the prospective assessment of possible handoff failures before the workflow starts. The starting point is a planned process, not a completed event to reconstruct.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028f.safety.rca-retrospective`, `claim.gs028f.safety.fmea-prospective`, `claim.gs028f.transplant.different-human`, `claim.gs028f.tacrolimus.t-cell-activation`.

**Existing sources:** <a href="https://psnet.ahrq.gov/primer/root-cause-analysis" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.ahrq_rca2024</a>; <a href="https://www.cancer.gov/publications/dictionaries/cancer-terms/def/allograft" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.nci.allograft</a>; <a href="https://dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/drugInfo.cfm?setid=7f667de1-9dfa-4bd6-8ba0-15ee2d78873b" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.prograf2023</a>; <a href="https://www.patientsafety.va.gov/professionals/onthejob/hfmea.asp" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">source.gs028f.va_pra2026</a>.

### `question.gs028f.outcomes.rca-versus-fmea-purpose.v3`

**Humor:** Straight.

**Diagnosis anchor:** Fever with asplenia

**Complaint:** Delayed fever assessment

**Person and setting:** Nora Hayes; age 47, Female.

**Story:** Nora Hayes, a 47-year-old woman, returns after hospital assessment of fever with asplenia. A message reached the wrong inbox and urgent assessment was delayed. Current care is arranged. The completed investigation, labeled root cause analysis, examines message-routing and escalation systems. The patient asks what that label means before discussing the findings from the hospital's review.

**Exhibit:** None.

**Question:** What is root cause analysis intended to assess in Nora Hayes's completed delayed-message event?

**Choices — KEY FIRST FOR REVIEW ONLY:**

A. Reconstruct the event and its systems contributors **(key)**

Rationale: RCA works backward from the completed routing event to examine how systems contributed. It is broader than assigning an individual performance verdict.

B. Predict failures before launching an untested process

Rationale: That anticipatory task describes healthcare FMEA. This document instead starts with a particular routing event that has already occurred.

C. Compare satisfaction scores across completed clinic visits

Rationale: Comparing satisfaction does not reconstruct the delayed-message event or identify its contributing systems problems. It answers a different review question.

D. Judge an individual worker's performance in isolation

Rationale: Individual blame is not RCA's central purpose. Restricting the review to one worker leaves its contributing systems question unanswered.

**Visible teachingPoint:** An RCA label identifies a retrospective systems question: what happened in this event, and which process problems contributed? The delayed message event is its starting point. Remember to look beyond a worker in isolation. The method's purpose does not, by itself, establish legal liability, assign individual blame, or guarantee that improvement has occurred.

**Short explanation fallback:** RCA reconstructs the completed delayed-message event and examines systems contributors. It does not primarily forecast a new workflow or decide blame for one worker in isolation.

**Existing claim bindings:** `claim.gs028f.safety.rca-retrospective`, `claim.gs028f.safety.fmea-prospective`, `claim.asplenia.fever-immediate-emergency-care`.
